Distribution Testing in the Presence of Arbitrarily Dominant Noise with Verification Queries
Hadley Black, Christopher Ye

Abstract
We study distribution testing without direct access to a source of relevant data, but rather to a highly contaminated one, from which only a tiny fraction (e.g. 1 % 1\% ) is relevant. To enable this, we introduce the following verification query model. The goal is to perform a statistical task on distribution 𝒑 \bm{p} given sample access to a mixture 𝒓 = λ ​ 𝒑 + ( 1 − λ ) ​ 𝒒 \bm{r}=\lambda\bm{p}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q} and the ability to query whether a sample x ∼ 𝒓 x\sim\bm{r} was generated by 𝒑 \bm{p} (relevant) or by 𝒒 \bm{q} (irrelevant). This captures scenarios where it is cheap to acquire data from a massive pool, but expensive to verify whether it is of interest for the specific task. In general, if m 0 m_{0} clean samples from 𝒑 \bm{p} suffice for a task, then O ⁡ ( m 0 / λ ) O(m_{0}/\lambda) samples and verification queries trivially suffice in our model. We ask, are there tasks for which the number of queries can be significantly reduced? We show that for the canonical problems in distribution testing (uniformity, identity, and closeness), the answer is yes. In fact, we obtain matching upper and lower bounds that reveal smooth trade-offs between sample and query complexity. For all m ≤ n m\leq n , we obtain (i) a uniformity and identity tester using O ⁡ ( m + n ε 2 ​ λ ) \smash{O(m+\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}\lambda})} samples and O ⁡ ( n m ​ ε 4 ​ λ 2 ) \smash{O(\frac{n}{m\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{2}})} queries, and (ii) a closeness tester using O ⁡ ( m + n 2 / 3 ε 4 / 3 ​ λ + 1 ε 4 ​ λ 3 ) \smash{O(m+\frac{n^{2/3}}{\varepsilon^{4/3}\lambda}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{3}})} samples and O ⁡ ( n 2 m 2 ​ ε 4 ​ λ 3 ) \smash{O(\frac{n^{2}}{m^{2}\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{3}})} queries. Moreover, we show that these query complexities are tight for all testers using m ≪ n m\ll n samples. In particular, obtaining the optimal sample complexity for standard testing requires every sample to be verified, while if one takes O ⁡ ( n ) O(n) samples, then only poly ⁡ ( 1 / ε ​ λ ) \mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon\lambda) queries suffice. Next, we show that for testing closeness (and hence also identity and uniformity) using m = O ~ ​ ( n / ε 2 ​ λ ) m=\widetilde{O}(n/\varepsilon^{2}\lambda) samples we can achieve query complexity O ~ ​ ( 1 / ε 2 ​ λ ) \widetilde{O}(1/\varepsilon^{2}\lambda) which is nearly optimal even for the basic task of bias estimation (uniformity testing when n = 2 n=2 ) with unbounded samples. Our uniformity testers work even in the more challenging setting where the contaminated samples are generated by an adaptive adversary (at the cost of a log ⁡ n \log n factor). Finally, we show that the sample-query trade-off lower bounds can be circumvented if the algorithm is provided with the PDF of the mixture 𝒓 \bm{r} .



1
Introduction

In machine learning and algorithm design, direct access to data that is representative of the phenomena we wish to learn or understand is an assumption that is typically taken for granted. In other words, the process of acquiring and verifying data is completely decoupled from the role of algorithms. We imagine this process as follows: first data is gathered from an undiscerning source (a mixture of good and bad data), then verified and filtered by an expert to produce the input (purely good data) for an algorithm. If a
\lambda
-fraction of examples are good, and
m
good examples are needed, then one will need to acquire and verify roughly
m/\lambda
samples. If verification is expensive, then one may wish to improve this. Is this possible? We initiate the study of a new model for solving statistical problems when an arbitrarily large fraction of data is bad by giving the algorithm access to a
verification oracle
, therefore
coupling algorithm design with data verification
. Our central question is the following:

By letting an algorithm decide the most informative samples to verify on the fly, is it possible to significantly reduce the total amount of data verification that is needed to solve a problem?

We show that for certain basic statistical tasks, namely distribution testing, the answer is yes.
Our aim is to address scenarios, such as the following examples, with a large fraction of irrelevant data:

1.

(
Respecting privacy of a protected group:
) You are interested in the income distribution of a relatively small protected group (e.g. by gender and/or ethnicity, etc), but you only have sample access to the broader general population. It is possible to ask (query) an individual to disclose whether they are a part of the protected group, but this is time consuming and may compromise privacy.

2.

(
Being robust to fake news:
) You are conducting a survey of news and research articles, a significant fraction of which contain fabricated statistics or non-reproducible results. You can employ (query) an expert to investigate the trustworthiness or quality of an article.

3.

(
Astronomy and particle physics:
) In experiments like those at the Large Hadron Collider, or in telescope data, a large majority of recorded events are due to background noise. However, patterns involving rare events must be identified and analyzed. Expert verification can distinguish signal vs. background noise, but this can only be done sparingly due to the volume of data.

The Verification Query Model.

Formalizing the above discussion, we initiate the study of a new
verification query
model for statistical tasks. We are given sample access to a mixture
\bm{r}=\lambda\bm{p}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}
where
\bm{p}
is the input distribution of interest over a domain
{\cal X}
,
\bm{q}
is an arbitrary contamination distribution over
{\cal X}
, and
\lambda\in(0,1)
is an unknown mixture parameter which we think of as a small constant, but can be arbitrarily small. The distribution
\bm{p}
represents a pure source of relevant data lying within a larger, perhaps contaminated source,
\bm{r}
. Given a sample
x\sim\bm{r}
, the
verification query oracle
returns "relevant" or "irrelevant", indicating whether
x
was generated by
\bm{p}
or by
\bm{q}
, respectively. In general, we view queries as more expensive then samples. In fact, we obtain algorithms whose query complexity decreases smoothly as a function of the number of samples, and thus can be adapted to the relative cost of queries vs. samples in a given application.

Verification queries can also be interpreted as revealing whether a particular event occurred for a sample. I.e., consider a distribution
\mathcal{D}
over domain
\Omega
, and an event
\mathcal{E}\subset\Omega
. We can express
\mathcal{D}
as a mixture

\mathcal{D}=\Pr[\mathcal{E}]\cdot\mathcal{D}|_{\mathcal{E}}+(1-\Pr[\mathcal{E}])\cdot\mathcal{D}|_{\Omega\setminus\mathcal{E}}

where
\mathcal{D}|_{\cdot}
denotes the distribution conditioned on
(\cdot)
. The verification query model then captures the problem of performing a statistical task for the distribution
\mathcal{D}|_{\mathcal{E}}
, given sample access to
\mathcal{D}
and the ability to query whether
\mathcal{E}
occurred for a given sample. As mentioned, we imagine that this event query is expensive, invasive, or otherwise undesirable to perform extensively.

If
m
samples suffice for a given task in the standard model, then
O(m/\lambda)
samples and queries are always sufficient in the verification query model since this many samples from
\bm{r}
will reveal at least
m
samples from
\bm{p}
with high probability, allowing for the standard algorithm to be applied as a black-box. When can we do significantly better than this?

Comparison with Semi-verified Learning.

To the best of our knowledge, there have been very few works which consider settings where a majority of data is irrelevant.
For example, list-decodable algorithms return a list of answers containing at least one accurate answer, but provably cannot produce a single correct output
[
CSV17
]
.
The closest model to ours is
semi-verified learning

[
CSV17
,
MV18
,
ZS23
]
introduced by
[
CSV17
]
. In this model, like ours, only a small fraction of data is good (generated by
\bm{p}
), and to remedy this the algorithm is provided with a small set of
k
pre-verified samples from
\bm{p}
as side information. In our verification
query
model one can obtain this side information using
O(k/\lambda)
samples and queries, and so up to this multiplicative
\frac{1}{\lambda}
term, our model is stronger.

The key difference between these models is that
verification queries
allow the algorithm the power to carefully
choose samples to query
in order to extract as much signal as possible, as opposed to seeing
k
independent samples from
\bm{p}
. In other words, the algorithm has some control over the good data it pays to see, and our results rely heavily on this additional power. Conceptually, our results show that strengthening the semi-verified model in this small and natural way significantly improves the capability to perform certain basic statistical tasks, namely distribution testing. (See
Section

1.3
for a more formal discussion on the relationship with semi-verified learning.)

On Robust Statistics and Tolerant Testing.

Over the last two decades the design of noise-robust algorithms has been a central focus in statistics, learning theory, and algorithm design broadly.
There are various ways of modeling noise, for example by manipulation of data
labels
(random classification, Massart, etc), or by adding or subtracting data points entirely (Huber contamination), resulting in a data-set which violates the underlying distributional assumptions.
The latter has led notably to the growing field of
robust statistics
(see e.g.
[
DK20
]
for a survey), as well as related models such as robust distribution testing
[
CHL+23
]
and
tolerant testing
(see e.g.
[
PRR06
]
) from the property testing community.
However, not surprisingly, these models face the fundamental information-theoretic limitation that the fraction of noisy data cannot exceed the desired testing or learning error,
\varepsilon
.
Our work aims to circumvent this limitation by adding the power to distinguish relevant data from contamination.

1.1
Our Results: Distribution Testing with Verification Queries

As an initial foray into the verification query model, we investigate basic testing questions for discrete probability distributions. Namely, we study the canonical problems in the area of
distribution testing
: uniformity and closeness testing, which have been extremely well-studied in the literature (see e.g.
[
GR97
,
GGR98
,
BFF+01
,
Bat01
,
Pan08
,
BFR+13
,
CDVV14
,
DKN14
,
DK16
,
VV17a
,
DGPP18
,
DGPP19
]
for a sample of relevant works and
[
Can20
]
for a survey). We use the following definitions and terminology:

•

A distribution
\bm{r}
is a
(1-\lambda)
-contaminated source for
\bm{p}
if
\bm{r}=\lambda\bm{p}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}
for some distribution
\bm{q}
.

•

A statistical task with data domain
{\cal X}
and output space
{\cal Y}
is a family of pairs
{\cal T}=\left\{(D,G_{D})\right\}
where
G_{D}\subset{\cal Y}
is the set of good outputs for distribution
D
over
{\cal X}
.

•

A (randomized) algorithm
\mathcal{A}
is
\lambda
-robust against distributional contamination for statistical task
{\cal T}
with sample complexity
m
and query complexity
q
if for every
(D_{0},G_{D_{0}})\in{\cal T}
and every
(1-\lambda)
-contaminated source for
D_{0}
(denoted
D
),

\Pr_{S\sim D^{m},\mathcal{A}}\left(\mathcal{A}(S)\in G_{D_{0}}\right)>\frac{2}{3}\text{.}

Succinctly, we say
\mathcal{A}
is a
\lambda
-distributionally robust algorithm with complexity
(m,q)
. We say
\mathcal{A}
is
\delta
-correct if the output lies in
G_{D_{0}}
with probability
1-\delta
.

Perhaps the most basic statistical task is bias estimation: estimating the bias of a coin up to error
\varepsilon
.
It is well known that
\Theta(\varepsilon^{-2})
samples are necessary and sufficient.
As mentioned, there is a naive algorithm using
O\left(\varepsilon^{-2}\lambda^{-1}\right)
samples and queries.
We show that this is in fact optimal.

Theorem 1.1
.

(Bias estimation lower bound.)

Any
\lambda
-distributionally robust
\varepsilon
-bias estimator requires
\Omega\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}\lambda}\right)
samples and
\Omega\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}\lambda}\right)
queries.

While verification queries do not allow a bias estimation algorithm to reduce the amount of data verification required, we show that this is in fact possible for testing uniformity and closeness over
[n]
.

Tight Sample vs. Query Tradeoffs.

Uniformity testing is perhaps the most fundamental question in distribution testing. The goal is to determine whether an input distribution
\bm{p}
supported over
[n]
is
uniform
or
\varepsilon
-far from uniform in total variation distance
1
1

1

The total variation distance between
\bm{p},\bm{q}
supported over
[n]
is defined as
d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\bm{p},\bm{q})=\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\bm{p}-\bm{q}\right\rVert_{1}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\bm{p}[i]-\bm{q}[i]|
.
We say that
\bm{p}
is
\varepsilon
-far from uniform if
d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\bm{p},U_{n})>\varepsilon
where
U_{n}
denotes the uniform distribution over
[n]
.
given
sample-access
to
\bm{p}
(in the standard model).
It is well-known that
\Theta(\sqrt{n}/\varepsilon^{2})
samples are both necessary and sufficient
[
Pan08
]
.
One reason for its central importance is that it is at least as hard as testing whether
\bm{p}=\bm{p}^{\ast}
for any
given and fixed

\bm{p}^{\ast}
(identity testing)
[
Gol20
]
.
In our setting, samples and queries can be simulated by an identical reduction, so that all of our uniformity testing algorithms imply identity testing algorithms with identical sample and query complexity up to constant factors (see
Theorem

4.3
).
In the verification query model, sample access is given instead to a mixture
\bm{r}=\lambda\bm{p}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}
where
\bm{p}
is the distribution we want to test, and
\bm{q}
is an adversarially chosen source of contamination.
In particular, it is possible that
\bm{r}=U_{n}
while either
d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\bm{p},U_{n})>\varepsilon
or
\bm{p}=U_{n}
, and so it is impossible to distinguish with samples alone. As mentioned, an algorithm using
\smash{O(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}\lambda})}
samples and queries is trivial to obtain using known algorithms for standard testing. We show that by increasing the number of samples
m
, there is an algorithm with query complexity that improves by a factor
\frac{1}{m}
.

Theorem 1.2
.

(Uniformity testing upper bound.)

For all
m\leq O(n),\varepsilon,\lambda\in(0,1)
, there is a
\lambda
-distributionally robust
\varepsilon
-uniformity (resp. identity) tester with sample complexity
O\left(m+\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}\lambda}\right)
and query complexity

O\left(\frac{n}{m\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{2}}\right)
.

When
m=\Theta(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}\lambda})
, we recover the trivial algorithm, whereas when
m=\Theta(n)
we obtain a query complexity
O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{2}})
,
independent of

n
.
In fact, we show that this sample-query tradeoff is optimal in the sub-linear regime i.e.,
m\leq cn
where
c>0
is a sufficiently small constant.
Our lower bound holds even when the algorithm is given
\lambda
, which we do not assume in our upper bounds.

Theorem 1.3
.

(Uniformity testing lower bound.)

Let
\lambda\leq\frac{1}{2}
and
\varepsilon<0.1
.
Any
\lambda
-distributionally robust
\varepsilon
-uniformity tester with
m\leq n/100
samples requires
\Omega\left(\frac{n}{m\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{2}}\right)
queries.

Next, we study the problem of
closeness testing
: given sample access to two distributions
\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2}
supported over
[n]
, distinguish between the case of
\bm{p}_{1}=\bm{p}_{2}
vs.
d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2})>\varepsilon
. In the standard model, the sample-complexity of this question is well-known to be
\smash{\Theta(\frac{n^{2/3}}{\varepsilon^{4/3}}+\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}})}
(
[
BFR+13
,
CDVV14
,
DK16
]
). In the verification query model, we have sample and verification query access to
two mixtures

\bm{r}_{1}=\lambda\bm{p}_{1}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}_{1}
and
\bm{r}_{2}=\lambda\bm{p}_{2}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}_{2}
and the objective
2
2

2

Note that we are assuming here that the two sources share a common mixture parameter
\lambda
, which may not be the case in some settings. We give a reduction in
Lemma

A.3
showing that we can reduce from the case of two different mixture parameters
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
to a common one obtained by their product,
\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}
. Therefore, this is without loss of generality, up to a potentially quadratic blowup in the dependence on
\lambda
. An interesting question is whether there is a more efficient reduction to common mixture parameter
\min(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
instead of the product, which would be best possible.
is to test closeness of
\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2}
. Again,
\smash{\Theta((\frac{n^{2/3}}{\varepsilon^{4/3}}+\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}})\frac{1}{\lambda})}
samples and queries is trivial to obtain. Similar to our results for uniformity testing, we show that the query complexity of testing closeness improves as the number of samples is increased, this time by a factor
\frac{1}{m^{2}}
.

Theorem 1.4
.

(Closeness testing upper bound.)

For all
m\leq O(n),\varepsilon,\lambda\in(0,1)
, there is a
\lambda
-distributionally robust
\varepsilon
-closeness tester with sample complexity
O\left(m+\frac{n^{2/3}}{\varepsilon^{4/3}\lambda}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{3}}\right)
and query complexity
O\left(\frac{n^{2}}{m^{2}\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{3}}\right)
.

We establish that the sample-query trade-off achieved by the above algorithm is tight for
m\leq cn
.
Again, this lower bound holds even when the algorithm is given
\lambda
.

Theorem 1.5
.

(Closeness testing lower bound.)

Let
\lambda\leq\frac{1}{2}
and
\varepsilon<0.1
.
Any
\lambda
-distributionally robust
\varepsilon
-closeness tester with
m\leq n/100
samples requires
\Omega\left(\frac{n^{2}}{m^{2}\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{3}}\right)
queries.

Algorithms with Optimal Query Complexity.

Notice that the upper bounds on the query complexity of uniformity and closeness testing achieved by
Theorem

1.2
and
Theorem

1.4
are
O(\varepsilon^{-4}\lambda^{-2})
and
O(\varepsilon^{-4}\lambda^{-3})
at best, respectively. (Achieved using
O(n)
samples). We have established in the corresponding lower bounds that this is optimal given a budget of
O(n)
samples, but it is natural to ask whether the query complexity can be improved further using
m\gg n
samples. We show in the following theorem that this is indeed the case. In fact, we give a closeness tester (and hence also an identity tester, and uniformity tester) using
\widetilde{O}(\varepsilon^{-2}\lambda^{-1})
queries, matching our lower bound for bias estimation (
Theorem

1.1
) up to
\mathrm{poly}\log(\varepsilon^{-1}\lambda^{-1})
factors. This tester follows a different strategy which we outline in
Section

2.3
.

Theorem 1.6
.

(Query-optimal closeness testing,
Theorem

7.3
informal.)

For all
\varepsilon,\lambda\in(0,1)
, there is a
\lambda
-distributionally robust
\varepsilon
-closeness tester with sample complexity
\widetilde{O}(n/\varepsilon^{2}\lambda)
and query complexity
\widetilde{O}(1/\varepsilon^{2}\lambda)
.

Algorithms Against Adaptive Contamination.

So far, we have considered only
distributional
sources of contamination.
That is, although the contamination distribution
\bm{q}
is worst-case (chosen adversarially in response to
\bm{p}
), the contaminated samples are drawn i.i.d. from
\bm{q}
. Alternatively, one could consider a more challenging model where these samples are chosen by an adaptive adversary (also called Huber contamination), which leads to further technical challenges.

•

An adversarially
(1-\lambda)
-contaminated source for
\bm{p}
is sampled from as follows:

1.

m
samples are drawn iid from
\bm{p}
.

2.

An adversary arbitrarily corrupts up to a
(1-\lambda)
-fraction of the samples drawn from
\bm{p}
and returns the corrupted dataset.
3
3

3

Note that the adversary may corrupt less than
(1-\lambda)
-fraction of samples.

•

The notion of an
adversarially robust algorithm
is precisely analogous to the distributional definition given earlier in the section, but defined with respect to adversarially contaminated sources.

Unsurprisingly, obtaining adversarially robust algorithms is more technically challenging. Nevertheless, we are able to strengthen our upper bounds for uniformity testing to hold in this more difficult setting, with similar sample and query complexity (up to log-factors).

Theorem 1.7
.

(Adversarial uniformity.)

For all
m\in\mathbb{N},\varepsilon,\lambda\in(0,1)
, there is a
\lambda
-adversarially robust
\varepsilon
-uniformity (resp. identity) tester with sample complexity
O\left(m+\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}\lambda}\right)
and query complexity
O\left(\frac{n\log n}{m\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{2}}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{2}}\right)
.

Similarly, we give a strengthening of
Theorem

1.6
for the special case of testing uniformity.

Theorem 1.8
.

(Query-optimal adversarial uniformity,
Theorem

8.1
informal.)

For all
\varepsilon,\lambda\in(0,1)
, there is a
\lambda
-adversarially robust
\varepsilon
-uniformity (resp. identity) tester with sample complexity
\widetilde{O}(n/\varepsilon^{2}\lambda)
and query complexity
\widetilde{O}(1/\varepsilon^{2}\lambda)
.

This query complexity is nearly optimal in light of our lower bound for bias estimation,
Theorem

1.1
.

Circumventing Query Lower Bounds with Mixture Knowledge.

Next, we ask what happens when the algorithm is provided with an explicit description of the mixture distribution
\bm{r}
as input.
We show that this added information enables us to circumvent the lower bound of
Theorem

1.3
and achieve near-optimal (up to
\mathrm{poly}(\log n,\varepsilon^{-1},\lambda^{-1})
factors) dependence on
n
for both sample and query complexity, simultaneously.

Theorem 1.9
.

(Uniformity testing with mixture knowledge,
Theorem

9.1
informal.)
For all
\lambda,\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, given complete knowledge of the PDF of the distributionally contaminated source, there is a
\lambda
-distributionally robust
\varepsilon
-uniformity (resp. identity) tester with sample complexity
\tilde{O}\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon\lambda)}\right)
and query complexity
\mathrm{poly}\left(\frac{\log n}{\varepsilon\lambda}\right)
.

We remark that in the interest of simplicity we have not optimized our dependence on
\varepsilon,\lambda
in
Theorem

1.9
.

Table of our results. Polylogarithmic terms are omitted for simplicity. When

, all query complexities are optimal up to constant factors. All lower bounds hold in the distributional setting, and therefore in the adversarial setting as well. Our uniformity testing algorithms also hold more generally for identity testing.

Paper organization.

For the sublinear sample regime (
m\leq O(n)
) we prove our upper bounds for testing uniformity (
Theorem

1.2
and
Theorem

1.7
) in
Section

4
and closeness (
Theorem

1.4
) in
Section

5
. We prove our lower bounds (
Theorem

1.3
and
Theorem

1.5
) in
Section

6
. We obtain our query-optimal testers for closeness (
Theorem

1.6
) and uniformity (
Theorem

1.8
) in
Section

7
and
Section

8
. Our uniformity tester with perfect mixture knowledge (
Theorem

1.9
) is presented in
Section

9
. We provide technical overviews for the proofs of our main results on bias estimation, uniformity, and closeness testing in
Section

2
.

1.2
Discussion and Open Questions

We propose a novel algorithmic framework for statistical analysis on heavily contaminated data sources: verification queries which reveal the trustworthiness of a sample.
Within this framework, we have given nearly tight upper and lower bounds for fundamental problems in distribution testing: bias estimation, uniformity testing, and closeness testing.

A natural open question is to investigate the power of verification queries for other statistical problems.
For example, one can consider mean estimation in high dimensional settings, or more general learning tasks such as PAC learning.
As usual, the naive algorithm is to run the standard algorithm and query all samples.
Is this optimal, or is there a better algorithm?

Our work also leaves open the question of whether there is a separation in our model between sources of distributional vs. adversarial contamination.
For uniformity, we have shown that our algorithms can be adapted to the adversarial setting with near optimal query complexity.
Can the same be done for any statistical task?
More generally, is there a generic way to make a distributionally robust algorithm adversarially robust?
Is there a separation (perhaps via some other problem) between the two contamination models?

1.3
Related Work and Comparison with Other Models

On Tolerant Testing.

An alternative approach to addressing distributional contamination is the concept of
tolerant testing

[
PRR06
]
.
In the tolerant uniformity testing problem, an algorithm must decide if
\bm{p}
is
\varepsilon
-close to uniform or
2\varepsilon
-far from uniform.
In the verification query model setting, if
\bm{p}
is uniform, then
\bm{r}
is
(1-\lambda)
-close to uniform, while if
\bm{p}
is
\varepsilon
-far from uniform,
\bm{r}
is
\varepsilon\lambda
-far from uniform.
Then, if
\lambda\geq\frac{1}{1+\varepsilon}
(low contamination), we can in fact test uniformity without any verification queries.

However, this approach immediately runs into two significant obstacles.
First, tolerant testing algorithms succeed only when contamination is low (
\lambda\geq\frac{1}{2}
), while our algorithms succeed for arbitrary contamination parameters
\lambda
.
Second, even for constant
4
4

4

See
[
CJKL22
]
for the dependency on tolerance parameters for the full landscape of the problem.

\varepsilon
the sample complexity of tolerant testing is barely sublinear
\Theta(n/\log n)

[
VV10
,
VV17b
,
CJKL22
]
.
In contrast, our sample-query trade-off (
Theorem

1.2
) yields an algorithm with strongly sub-linear sample complexity at the cost of querying a tiny fraction of the samples (e.g.
n^{0.9}
samples and
n^{0.1}
queries).

Contrasting with Semi-verified Learning.

Our results show that verification queries are significantly more powerful than semi-verified learning
[
CSV17
]
for the problems we study. Consider the uniformity testing problem with
\lambda=1/2
. Using known lower bounds for uniformity testing in the standard model, we can construct distributions
\bm{p}_{\mathrm{yes}},\bm{p}_{\mathrm{no}},\bm{q}_{\mathrm{yes}},\bm{q}_{\mathrm{no}}
where the following hold:

1.

\bm{p}_{\mathrm{yes}}=U_{n}
,
d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\bm{p}_{\mathrm{no}},U_{n})>\varepsilon
.

2.

A set of
k=o(\sqrt{n}/\varepsilon^{2})
samples from
\bm{p}_{\mathrm{yes}}
or
\bm{p}_{\mathrm{no}}
are statistically close, i.e.
d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\bm{p}_{\mathrm{yes}}^{k},\bm{p}_{\mathrm{no}}^{k})=o(1)
.

3.

\bm{r}_{\mathrm{yes}}=\frac{1}{2}(\bm{p}_{\mathrm{yes}}+\bm{q}_{\mathrm{yes}})=\frac{1}{2}(\bm{p}_{\mathrm{no}}+\bm{q}_{\mathrm{no}})=\bm{r}_{\mathrm{no}}
by defining
\bm{q}_{\mathrm{yes}},\bm{q}_{\mathrm{no}}
appropriately.

By (1), a uniformity tester must be able to distinguish these two cases. However, by (2) unless
k=\Omega(\sqrt{n}/\varepsilon^{2})
, the side information is not useful, and by (3) samples from
\bm{r}_{\mathrm{yes}},\bm{r}_{\mathrm{no}}
are identically distributed (and in fact can even be simulated).
Therefore, in semi-verified learning, essentially the entire burden is placed on the side information, i.e. we must have
k\geq\Omega(\sqrt{n}/\varepsilon^{2})
pre-verified samples regardless of the number of corrupted samples.
In contrast, our
Theorem

1.2
shows that in the verification query model it is possible to distinguish these cases using only
\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)
verified samples.

Additional Related Work.

One of the primary themes in our work is the use of queries to overcome uncertainty about sampled data. Although we are the first to study the verification query model we consider, other models have been studied with a similar theme. For instance, the
huge object

[
GR23
]
and the
confused collector
models
[
JH24
,
JH23
]
for distribution testing.

Another high-level theme of our work is to study algorithms equipped with both a weak oracle that is cheap, as well as a stronger oracle that is expensive.
This is similar in spirit to
active testing

[
BBBY12
]
and dual-access models considered in the context of metric clustering, e.g.
[
BDKR05
,
GMV06
]
.

Finally, we consider distribution testing of
\bm{p}
with sample access to a mixture
\lambda\bm{p}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}
. It is worth noting that the work of
[
AKR19
]
considers the problem of testing whether a distribution
\bm{p}
can be written as
\bm{p}=\lambda\bm{q}_{1}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}_{2}
for some
\lambda
, given sample access to
\bm{p},\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2}
.

We have primarily studied the problems of uniformity and closeness testing.
These fundamental distribution testing problems have been studied in a variety of other settings, see e.g.
[
CFGM13
,
CRS15
,
ASZ18
,
ADR18
,
ADKR19
,
LY24
,
CKO24
,
AAC+25
,
DGK+25
,
AFL25
,
BCW25
,
GR25
]
.

2
Technical Overview

In the interest of emphasizing our main ideas, we assume
\lambda=\frac{1}{2}
throughout the overview.
In reality, we design algorithms that succeed given a lower bound on
\lambda
and spend
O\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)
samples and queries to obtain this lower bound.
Note that this term never dominates as
O\left(\frac{m}{\lambda}\right)
samples are required to obtain sufficiently many samples from
\bm{p}
and
O\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)
queries are required just to detect a single sample from
\bm{p}
.

2.1
Upper Bounds in the Sublinear Sample Regime

Uniformity Testing.

In uniformity testing, we are given sample access to
\bm{r}=\frac{1}{2}(\bm{p}+\bm{q})
and asked to determine if
\bm{p}
is uniform or far from uniform.
If we have an
m
sample algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{0}
for uniformity testing (without noise), then we have a distributionally robust algorithm with
O(m)
samples and
O(m)
queries.
For example, with
O(m)
samples we guarantee that at least
m
samples come from
\bm{p}
, and we can identify these samples with
O(m)
queries and run
\mathcal{A}_{0}
.
Of course,
\Omega(m)
samples are necessary.
At first glance, it is not obvious that there is even an algorithm with
o(m)
queries.
In fact in the bias estimation problem, we prove that
\Omega(\varepsilon^{-2})
queries are necessary (
Theorem

1.1
).

For uniformity testing, we begin with the crucial observation that there are very few influential samples (although it may take many samples to observe them).
In particular, it suffices to count collisions to determine if
\bm{p}
is uniform or not, and indeed this approach yields an optimal tester in the standard setting
[
DGPP19
]
.
Given
m
samples, a uniform distribution produces roughly
\frac{m^{2}}{n}
collisions while a distribution
\varepsilon
-far from uniform produces at least
\frac{(1+\varepsilon^{2})m^{2}}{n}
collisions with high probability.
Thus, with
m=\Theta\left(n^{1/2}\varepsilon^{-2}\right)
samples, a uniform distribution only produces
\Theta\left(\varepsilon^{-4}\right)
collisions.
By only querying collisions, we can estimate how many were produced by
\bm{p}
, and this is sufficient to test uniformity of
\bm{p}
.
In an ideal setting, we would like to directly query collisions from
\bm{p}
, in which case we can even hope for query complexity independent of
n
!

However, we can only query collisions to
\bm{r}
.
If
\left\lVert\bm{r}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\gg\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
, most collisions in the observed samples from
\bm{r}
will be produced by
\bm{q}
and not
\bm{p}
.
For example, if
\bm{q}
is uniform over a subdomain of size
10m
, then
\bm{q}
will produce
\Theta(m)
collisions but we will not be able to detect the domain of
\bm{q}
without using many queries.
Still, when
\left\lVert\bm{r}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq t\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
is not too large, we obtain an efficient algorithm: repeatedly query
random
collisions in the set of samples in order to estimate the number of collisions produced by
\bm{p}
.
Since we find a
\bm{p}
-collision with every
O(t)
queries, we obtain an algorithm using
O(t/\mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon))
queries.

The final ingredient is to bound
t
.
Intuitively, if
\bm{r}
has significant mass on any element, we can detect this in
m
samples, i.e. we can learn the mass of any element with mass greater than
\frac{1}{m}
.
This intuition is captured by the flattening technique
[
DK16
]
which states that using
O(m)
samples, we can assume without loss of generality that
\left\lVert\bm{r}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{m}
(and therefore
\left\lVert\bm{q}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{m}
).

This leads to our final algorithm.
We use
O(m)
samples to flatten (
[
DK16
]
) the input distribution
\bm{r}
so that every bucket has mass at most
\frac{1}{m}
.
Under this assumption, if we take another
m
samples the observed number of collisions is in expectation at most
\binom{m}{2}\left\lVert\bm{r}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=O(m)
.
Now, consider the two cases.
If
\bm{p}
is uniform, it produces at most
\binom{m}{2}\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{m^{2}}{n}
collisions.
If
\bm{p}
is
\varepsilon
-far from uniform, a standard calculation (see e.g.
[
Can20
]
) shows that it produces at least
\binom{m}{2}\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert^{2}_{2}\geq\frac{m^{2}(1+\varepsilon^{2})}{n}
collisions.
If we query a random collision, we find a collision between two elements sampled from
\bm{p}
with probability at most
\frac{m}{n}
in the former case and at least
\frac{(1+\varepsilon^{2})m}{n}
in the latter case.
Since this reduces the problem of uniformity testing to differentiating a coin with bias
\frac{m}{n}
from one with bias
\frac{(1+\varepsilon^{2})m}{n}
, it suffices to query
\frac{n}{m\varepsilon^{4}}
samples.

There is still one remaining issue:
\bm{p}
can produce many collisions for two reasons: 1)
\bm{p}
is
\varepsilon
-far from uniform, or 2) more samples come from
\bm{p}
than
\bm{q}
.
We must ensure that the signal from 1) outweighs the noise from 2).
To address this issue, we compute an estimate
\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}
of
m_{\bm{p}}
, where
m=m_{\bm{p}}+m_{\bm{q}}
denote the number of samples drawn from
\bm{p},\bm{q}
respectively.
5
5

5

Alternatively, we may increase the number of samples to address this issue. In particular,
m_{\bm{p}}=\frac{m}{2}\pm O(\sqrt{m})
so that in the completeness case we have the number of
\bm{p}
-collisions is at most
\frac{m_{\bm{p}}^{2}}{n}\ll\frac{m^{2}+O(m^{1.5})}{n}
while in the soundness case the number of
\bm{p}
-collisions is at least
\frac{m_{\bm{p}}^{2}(1+\varepsilon^{2})}{n}\gg\frac{m^{2}+m^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}{n}
. Thus,
m\gg\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}}
ensures that
\varepsilon^{2}m^{2}\gg m^{1.5}
.

Clearly, a randomly queried sample reveals
\bm{p}
with probability
\frac{m_{\bm{p}}}{m}
.
With
O(\varepsilon^{-4})
queries, we can estimate
m_{\bm{p}}
up to accuracy
\varepsilon^{2}m
.
In particular, we can estimate
\frac{m_{\bm{p}}(1+0.5\varepsilon^{2})}{n}
up to accuracy
\ll\frac{\varepsilon^{2}m}{n}
which suffices to ensure that the signal from 1) dominates the noise from 2).

Adversarially Robust Uniformity Testing.

We can make our uniformity testing algorithm adversarially robust (
Theorem

1.7
).
A close inspection of our distributionally robust uniformity tester reveals that it relies on the following two facts:

1.

There are enough samples from
\bm{p}
to guarantee that there are many
\bm{p}
-collisions when
\bm{p}
is far from uniform and few
\bm{p}
-collisions when
\bm{p}
is uniform.
This is guaranteed by taking
\gg\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\lambda\varepsilon^{2}}
samples.

2.

There are not too many collisions in
\bm{r}
.
This is guaranteed by the flattening procedure
[
DK16
]
.
As discussed above, we use this to bound the number of queries, since this ensures we can efficiently find a
\bm{p}
-collision by querying a random collision.

In the adversarial setting, we can no longer first apply the flattening algorithm to the first half of samples and then test uniformity on the flattened distribution with the remaining samples, since we are no longer guaranteed sample access to the same distribution.
However, if we randomly subsample our data into a flattening dataset and a testing dataset, we can still ensure that there are not too many collisions in the testing dataset (since random sub-sampling ensures that any bucket with high frequency in the testing dataset must also have high frequency in the flattening dataset).
To meet the first requirement, note that we still collect sufficiently many samples from
\bm{p}
to ensure that the number of collisions are separated in the completeness and soundness cases.

Closeness Testing.

In closeness testing, we are given sample access to
\bm{r}_{1}=\frac{1}{2}(\bm{p}_{1}+\bm{q}_{1})
and
\bm{r}_{2}=\frac{1}{2}(\bm{p}_{2}+\bm{q}_{2})
and asked to determine if
\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2}
are identical or far from each other.
Our algorithm aims to use queries to simulate a collision based closeness testing algorithm
[
DGPP19
]
.
In the standard setting,
[
DGPP19
]
show that there is an optimal collision based estimator, which computes
Z=C_{\bm{p}_{1}}+C_{\bm{p}_{2}}-\frac{m-1}{m}C_{\bm{p}_{1}\bm{p}_{2}}
where
C_{\bm{p}_{1}},C_{\bm{p}_{2}},C_{\bm{p}_{1}\bm{p}_{2}}
denote the
\bm{p}_{1}
-collisions,
\bm{p}_{2}
-collisions and collisions between
\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2}
.
It is easy to see that
\mathbb{E}[Z]=\binom{m}{2}\left\lVert\bm{p}_{1}-\bm{p}_{2}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
so that
Z
gives a closeness tester in
\ell_{2}
norm.

Given an upper bound
\left\lVert\bm{p}_{1}\right\rVert_{2}^{2},\left\lVert\bm{p}_{2}\right\rVert^{2}\leq b
,
[
DGPP19
]
show that
Z
does not deviate from its mean by more than
\simeq m^{2}\max(\alpha,\varepsilon^{2})
where
\alpha=\left\lVert\bm{p}_{1}-\bm{p}_{2}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
.
Thus, our goal is to estimate the collision counts up to this error.
For instance, we hope to estimate
\hat{C}_{\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2}}
such that
|\hat{C}_{\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2}}-C_{\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2}}|\ll m^{2}\varepsilon^{2}
.
Since there are at most
O(m^{2}b)=O(m)
collisions (as flattening ensure
b=O(1/m)
) this is equivalent to bias estimation up to accuracy
m\varepsilon^{2}
which requires
O\left(\frac{1}{m^{2}\varepsilon^{4}}\right)
queries.
Since
\left\lVert\bm{p}_{1}-\bm{p}_{2}\right\rVert_{1}\geq\varepsilon
implies that
\left\lVert\bm{p}_{1}-\bm{p}_{2}\right\rVert_{2}\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{n}}
, we obtain an algorithm using
O\left(\frac{n^{2}}{m^{2}\varepsilon^{4}}\right)
queries.

\hat{C}_{\bm{p}_{1}},\hat{C}_{\bm{p}_{2}}
can be estimated similarly.

However, it is still not obvious that we can simply compare the estimated test statistic
\hat{Z}=\hat{C}_{\bm{p}_{1}}+\hat{C}_{\bm{p}_{2}}-\frac{m-1}{m}\hat{C}_{\bm{p}_{1}\bm{p}_{2}}
to some predetermined threshold.
In particular, we do not have control over the number of samples from
\bm{r}_{1},\bm{r}_{2}
that are drawn by
\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2}
and we do not have a query-efficient way to compute these numbers to sufficient accuracy, unlike in the uniformity testing case, where cruder estimates are sufficient.
(Of course, we do know that these numbers are well-concentrated around their expectation
\lambda m
, but this is not enough on its own.) Thus, another technical issue is that differing numbers of samples from
\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2}
can cause the collision counts
\hat{C}_{\bm{p}_{1}},\hat{C}_{\bm{p}_{2}}
to look very different, even when
\bm{p}_{1}=\bm{p}_{2}
, causing the tester to reject. Therefore, bounding the difference
|Z-\hat{Z}|
requires carefully bounding the effects of these "extra" collisions (see the proof of
Theorem

5.1
for details). We remark that this technical issue would be avoided in a simpler, but less realistic model, where an
exactly

\lambda
-fraction of samples are drawn from the target distributions
\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2}
. However, we feel that this would significantly sacrifice the applicability and appeal of the model.

2.2
Lower Bounds

Bias Estimation Lower Bound.

We begin with a simple lower bound for bias estimation.
Our construction for general
\lambda
is slightly more involved.
In the completeness case
\bm{p},\bm{q}
are both fair coins (i.e. uniform), while in the soundness case
\bm{p}
has bias
\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon
while
\bm{q}
has bias
\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon
(i.e. far from uniform).
Since in both cases,
\bm{r}
is a fair coin, any given sample reveals no information about
\bm{p}
.
In general, our hard instances will ensure that
\bm{r}
is identical in both completeness and soundness cases so that samples alone reveal no information.
Consider a sample that shows heads.
If both
\bm{p},\bm{q}
are fair coins, a query on this sample reveals
\bm{p}
with probability
\frac{1}{2}
.
On the other hand, if
\bm{p}
has bias
\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon
, a query reveals
\bm{p}
with probability
\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon
.
In particular, each query simulates a coin flip.
A standard bias estimation lower bound then shows that
\Omega(\varepsilon^{-2})
queries are necessary.

Uniformity Testing Lower Bound.

We begin by showing that any algorithm that takes
m\leq n^{0.99}
samples requires
\Omega\left(\frac{n}{m}\right)
queries (see
Theorem

6.3
).
Consider the following instance (see
Definition

6.12
).
There are
m
heavy buckets where
\bm{p}
has mass
\frac{1}{n}
and
\bm{q}
has mass
\frac{4\varepsilon}{n}+\frac{1-4\varepsilon}{m}
.
In the completeness case,
\bm{p}
has mass
\frac{1}{n}
on the remaining
n-m
light buckets while
\bm{q}
has mass
\frac{4\varepsilon}{n}
.
In the soundness case,
\bm{p},\bm{q}
have
\frac{1+2\varepsilon}{n}
and
\frac{2\varepsilon}{n}
respectively on half of the remaining light buckets and mass
\frac{1-2\varepsilon}{n}
and
\frac{6\varepsilon}{n}
respectively on the other half.
In both cases, the light buckets collectively produce at most
\Theta\left(\frac{m^{2}}{n}\right)
collisions while
\bm{r}
produces at least
m^{2}\left\lVert\bm{r}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\Theta(m)
collisions.
In fact, for any fixed frequency
c
, there are
\Theta(m)

c
-collisions among the heavy buckets.
Since
\bm{p}
is identical on heavy buckets, these queries should not reveal any information and any correct algorithm must query at least one light bucket.
However, any collision query lands on a light bucket with probability at most
O\left(\frac{m^{2}/n}{m}\right)=O\left(\frac{m}{n}\right)
, so any uniformity tester requires
\Omega\left(\frac{n}{m}\right)
queries.

Examining this instance more closely also yields the
\Omega\left(\frac{n}{m\varepsilon^{4}}\right)
lower bound.
Consider an algorithm that randomly queries collisions.
The distribution produces
\Theta(m)
collisions (mostly on heavy buckets).
On the other hand
\bm{p}
produces
\frac{m^{2}}{n}
collisions when
\bm{p}
is uniform and
\geq\frac{m^{2}(1+\varepsilon^{2})}{n}
collisions when
\bm{p}
is far from uniform.
Thus, a randomly chosen collision is a
\bm{p}
collision with probability
\frac{m}{n}
in the former case and
\frac{m(1+\varepsilon^{2})}{n}
in the latter.
A standard lower bound for bias estimation shows that
\Omega\left(\frac{n}{m\varepsilon^{4}}\right)
queries are necessary to distinguish the two cases.
Here, note that since
\frac{m}{n}\ll 1
, only
O\left(\frac{n}{m\varepsilon^{4}}\right)
queries are necessary to estimate the bias of a coin with small entropy in contrast to the
O\left(\frac{n^{2}}{m^{2}\varepsilon^{4}}\right)
queries necessary to estimate the bias of a coin with high entropy up to the same accuracy.

To formalize our intuition (and obtain a lower bound for arbitrary algorithms), we employ a mutual information based framework for obtaining the tight
\Omega\left(\frac{n}{m\varepsilon^{4}}\right)
lower bound, which first appeared in the distribution testing context in
[
DK16
]
.
Let
\mathcal{A}
be a uniformity tester with
m
samples and
q
queries.
First, we make two standard assumptions 1)
\mathcal{A}
takes
\mathrm{Poi}(m)
samples, and 2) each bucket is heavy with probability
\frac{m}{n}
and light with probability
\frac{n-m}{n}
.
6
6

6

In the soundness case, each bucket is each type of light bucket with equal probability.

For 1) it is easy to see that
\mathrm{Poi}(m)=O(m)
with high probability.
For 2), while
\bm{p},\bm{q}
chosen in this way may not be distributions, they are non-negative measures with total mass close to
1
.
Normalizing by
\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{1},\left\lVert\bm{q}\right\rVert_{1}
therefore should not significantly change the distribution.
These assumptions allow us to assume that samples observed from each bucket are independent, which will be crucial to our analysis.
In particular, we may assume that the algorithm observes
Y_{i}\sim\mathrm{Poi}(m\bm{r}[i])
samples from the
i
-th bucket, where
Y_{i}=A_{i}+B_{i}
with
A_{i}\sim\mathrm{Poi}(m\bm{p}[i]/2),B_{i}\sim\mathrm{Poi}(m\bm{q}[i]/2)
are sampled independently.

Suppose an algorithm
\mathcal{A}
takes
m
samples and
q
queries.
We will in fact assume that a single query reveals all the samples on the queried bucket, and prove a lower bound on the number of queried buckets.
Note that this assumption only strengthens
\mathcal{A}
.
Let
Q\subset[n]
be the set of queried buckets.
Let
T
be the input to the algorithm.
In particular, let
T=(T_{1},\dotsc,T_{n})
where
T_{i}=(A_{i},B_{i})
if
i\in Q
is queried and
T_{i}=Y_{i}
otherwise.
We design an adversary that flips an unbiased bit
X
: and produces a completeness instance (
\bm{p}
uniform) if
X=0
and a soundness instance (
\bm{p}
far from uniform) if
X=1
.
To prove a lower bound on
q
, we require two facts:

1.

If
\mathcal{A}
is correct, then
I(T:X)=\Omega(1)
.

2.

If
\mathcal{A}
makes
q
queries, then
I(T:X)=O\left(q\cdot\frac{\varepsilon^{4}m}{n}\right)
.

The lower bound on
q
immediately follows from the above two observations.

Item

1
, which states that any correct algorithm must observe samples correlated with the desired output, follows essentially from the data processing inequality.
Since
\mathcal{A}
is a randomized function such that
\mathcal{A}(T)=X
with probability
\geq\frac{2}{3}
,
\mathcal{A}(T)
and therefore
T
must be correlated with
X
(see
Lemma

3.8
).

Item

2
says that few queries do not have significant correlation with the desired output
X
.
To prove the bound
Item

2
, we observe that if
i\not\in Q
is not queried,
T_{i}=Y_{i}\sim\mathrm{Poi}(m\bm{r}[i])
is independent of
X
.
Furthermore, since the samples of bucket
i
are independent (even conditioned on
X
) it suffices to bound the mutual information gained from a single bucket.
This follows from a technical calculation (see
Lemma

6.16
for details).

Closeness Testing Lower Bound.

Consider the following instance (see
Definition

6.20
).
As before, we assume
\mathcal{A}
takes
\mathrm{Poi}(m)
samples.
Each bucket is independently heavy with probability
\frac{m}{n}
, light with probability
\frac{n-m}{n}
.
Light buckets are type 1 and 2 with equal probability.
On heavy buckets
\bm{p}_{1},\bm{q}_{1},\bm{p}_{2},\bm{q}_{2}
have mass
\frac{1-\varepsilon}{m}
.
In the completeness case, on type 1 light buckets
\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2}
have mass
\frac{3\varepsilon}{2(n-m)}
and
\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2}
have mass
\frac{\varepsilon}{2(n-m)}
, and on type 2 buckets,
\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2}
have mass
\frac{3\varepsilon}{2(n-m)}
and
\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2}
have mass
\frac{\varepsilon}{2(n-m)}
.
Note that
\bm{p}_{1}=\bm{p}_{2}
.
In the soundness case, on type 1 light buckets
\bm{p}_{1},\bm{q}_{2}
have mass
\frac{3\varepsilon}{2(n-m)}
and
\bm{q}_{1},\bm{p}_{2}
have mass
\frac{\varepsilon}{2(n-m)}
, and on type 2 buckets,
\bm{q}_{1},\bm{p}_{2}
have mass
\frac{3\varepsilon}{2(n-m)}
and
\bm{p}_{1},\bm{q}_{2}
have mass
\frac{\varepsilon}{2(n-m)}
.
Note
\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2}
are
\varepsilon
-far apart with high probability.
Now, consider the number of collisions produced between every pair of distributions in the two scenarios.

\bm{p}_{1}

\bm{q}_{1}

\bm{p}_{2}

\bm{q}_{2}

\bm{p}_{1}

\frac{m}{2}+\frac{5\varepsilon^{2}m^{2}}{2n}

\frac{m}{2}+\frac{3\varepsilon^{2}m^{2}}{2n}

\frac{m}{2}+\frac{5\varepsilon^{2}m^{2}}{2n}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}m^{2}}{n}}

\frac{m}{2}+\frac{3\varepsilon^{2}m^{2}}{2n}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}m^{2}}{n}}

\bm{q}_{1}

\frac{m}{2}+\frac{5\varepsilon^{2}m^{2}}{2n}

\frac{m}{2}+\frac{3\varepsilon^{2}m^{2}}{2n}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}m^{2}}{n}}

\frac{m}{2}+\frac{5\varepsilon^{2}m^{2}}{2n}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}m^{2}}{n}}

\bm{p}_{2}

\frac{m}{2}+\frac{5\varepsilon^{2}m^{2}}{2n}

\frac{m}{2}+\frac{3\varepsilon^{2}m^{2}}{2n}

\bm{q}_{2}

\frac{m}{2}+\frac{5\varepsilon^{2}m^{2}}{2n}

Table 2:
The expected number of collisions between every pair of distributions.
For simplicity of expressions, we assume we draw exactly
m
samples from
\bm{p},\bm{q}
,
\binom{m}{2}\simeq\frac{m^{2}}{2}
,
1-\varepsilon\simeq 1
, and
n-m\simeq n
.
The expected number of collisions shown in black is in the completeness case.
Any changes in the soundness case are shown in red.
No red indicates that the expected number of collisions is identical in both cases.

As before, consider an algorithm
\mathcal{A}
that queries random collisions.
Suppose for example that this algorithm attempts to test closeness by counting the number of
(\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2})
-collisions.
On average,
\bm{r}_{1},\bm{r}_{2}
produces
\Theta(m)
collisions.
In the completeness case, there are
\frac{m}{2}+\frac{5\varepsilon^{2}m^{2}}{2n}

(\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2})
-collisions while in the soundness case there are only
\frac{m}{2}+\frac{3\varepsilon^{2}m^{2}}{2n}
.
In particular, a random collision yields
(\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2})
with probability
\Theta(1)+\frac{5\varepsilon^{2}m}{n}
in the former case and
\Theta(1)+\frac{3\varepsilon^{2}m}{n}
in the latter.
Estimating the bias of a coin (with bias
\Omega(1)
) up to accuracy
\frac{\varepsilon^{2}m}{n}
requires
O\left(\frac{n^{2}}{m^{2}\varepsilon^{4}}\right)
queries.
Note that in contrast to uniformity testing, we obtain a stronger lower bound as we now have to estimate the bias of a coin with high entropy.

To formalize this (again for arbitrary algorithms), we employ a similar mutual information framework to the uniformity testing problem.
Much of the formal argument is similar, except we obtain a stronger result that states that querying a bucket
i
only reveals
O\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{4}m^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)
information (
Lemma

6.24
).
The query lower bound then follows identically.

2.3
Query-Optimal Testing with Superlinear Samples

It is a standard fact that
O(n/\varepsilon^{2})
samples are sufficient to
learn
a discrete distribution
\bm{p}
up to total variation distance
\varepsilon
. When this many samples are allowed, we exploit this fact to obtain testers with query complexity
\widetilde{O}(1/\varepsilon^{2}\lambda)
, which is optimal up to
\mathrm{polylog}(1/\varepsilon\lambda)
factors. Our testers in this regime follow a different approach from the sublinear sample setting, outlined in
Section

2.1
, which we now describe.

Closeness Testing.

First, using
O(n\log n)
samples up-front, a flattening argument (
Theorem

A.2
) allows us to assume our distributions have bounded infinity norm, namely
\left\lVert\bm{r}_{1}\right\rVert_{\infty},\left\lVert\bm{r}_{2}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq O(1/n)
and
\left\lVert\bm{p}_{1}\right\rVert_{\infty},\left\lVert\bm{p}_{2}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq O(1/\lambda n)
. Another simple reduction (
Lemma

7.5
) allows us to further assume that
\bm{r}_{b}[i]=\Theta(1/n)
for every
i\in[n]
, and so we will assume this throughout the discussion.

We begin by taking
m=\widetilde{O}(n/\varepsilon^{2}\lambda)
samples (enough for learning) from each mixture
\bm{r}_{b}
(for
b\in\{1,2\}
) and let
Z_{1}^{(b)},\ldots,Z_{n}^{(b)}
denote the partition on these samples by domain element. For each
i\in[n]
, let
P_{b}[i],Q_{b}[i],R_{b}[i]=P_{b}[i]+Q_{b}[i]
denote the number of samples in
Z_{i}^{(b)}
which were drawn according to
\bm{p}_{b},\bm{q}_{b},\bm{r}_{b}
, respectively. Note that the tester knows
R_{b}[i]
without making any queries, but not
P_{b}[i],Q_{b}[i]
. By the learning bound, we have

\frac{P_{b}[i]}{\lambda m}\approx\bm{p}_{b}[i]~\text{ and }~\frac{R_{b}[i]}{m}\approx\bm{r}_{b}[i]\approx\frac{1}{n}\text{.}

(In particular, we need a small additive error estimate of
\bm{p}_{b}[i]
, which relies on our bound on the infinity norms guaranteed by the flattening reduction.) These estimates would clearly be useful for testing closeness of
\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2}
, but as mentioned we do not know
P_{b}[i]
. However, by performing a verification query on a uniform random sample in
Z^{(b)}_{i}
, we simulate a
\rho_{b}[i]:=P_{b}[i]/R_{b}[i]
probability coin. Thus, the idea is to use queries to simulate these coin flips in order to estimate
P_{b}[i]/\lambda m\approx\bm{p}_{b}[i]
. If in doing so we can detect that
\bm{p}_{1}[i]\neq\bm{p}_{2}[i]
for some
i\in[n]
, then we can safely reject.

When
\left\lVert\bm{p}_{1}-\bm{p}_{2}\right\rVert\geq\varepsilon
(and using our assumption that
\left\lVert\bm{p}_{b}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq O(1/\lambda n)
), a bucketing argument reveals that for some
j\leq O(\log(1/\varepsilon\lambda))
, sampling
i\sim[n]
uniformly yields
|\bm{p}_{1}[i]-\bm{p}_{2}[i]|\geq\varepsilon 2^{j}/n
with probability at least
\gtrsim 2^{-j}
. Thus, for each such
j
, we sample a set
X_{j}
of
\approx 2^{j}
uniform samples. Then, for each element
X_{j}
it suffices to obtain an estimate of
\bm{p}_{b}[i]
within additive error
\lesssim\varepsilon 2^{j}/n
. Now, since

\rho_{b}[i]=\frac{P_{b}[i]}{R_{b}[i]}\approx\bm{p}_{b}[i]\cdot\frac{\lambda m}{R_{b}[i]}\approx\bm{p}_{b}[i]\cdot\lambda n

(where
R_{b}[i]
is known), this translates to a
\lesssim\lambda\varepsilon 2^{j}
additive error estimate of
\rho_{b}[i]
. By Markov’s inequality, we can argue that every
i\in X_{j}
satisfies
\bm{p}_{b}[i]\lesssim 2^{j}/n
and so
\rho_{b}[i]\lesssim\lambda 2^{j}
. Then, using a standard bias estimation (
Lemma

3.13
), this costs
\lesssim\frac{\lambda 2^{j}}{(\lambda\varepsilon 2^{j})^{2}}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}\lambda 2^{j}}
queries to obtain. (Each query simulates an independent
\mathrm{Bern}(\rho_{b}[i])
trial). Summing over all
j
yields the desired query complexity.

Uniformity Testing against Adversarial Contamination.

In the above closeness tester, we used the flattening technique of
[
DK16
]
adapted to obtain a bound on the infinity norm (
\left\lVert\bm{p}_{b}\right\rVert_{\infty}\lesssim 1/\lambda n
) using
O(n\log n)
samples. This was useful for two reasons:

1.

(Sample complexity) Our approach relies on
P_{b}[i]/\lambda m
being a low additive error (
\lesssim\varepsilon/n
) empirical estimate of
\bm{p}_{b}[i]
. To guarantee this, the sample complexity depends on
\bm{p}_{1}[i],\bm{p}_{2}[i]
(see
Theorem

7.1
,
Corollary

7.2
). In particular, to get sample complexity
\widetilde{O}(n\cdot\mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon^{-1},\lambda^{-1}))
, we need a bound
\bm{p}_{1}[i],\bm{p}_{2}[i]\leq\mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon^{-1},\lambda^{-1})/n
.

2.

(Query complexity) Our bucketing argument allowed us to say that sampling
i\sim[n]

uniformly at random
will, for some
j\leq O(\log(1/\varepsilon\lambda))
, guarantee
|\bm{p}_{1}[i]-\bm{p}_{2}[i]|\gtrsim\varepsilon 2^{j}/n
with probability
\gtrsim 2^{-j}
. Then, since the test elements
X_{j}
are sampled uniformly, we can argue
\bm{p}_{b}[i]\lesssim 2^{j}/n
by Markov’s inequality. This allowed us to give an upper bound on the coin probability
\rho_{b}[i]
, crucially helping us bound the number of queries needed for the bias estimation step (
Lemma

3.13
). Without flattening, one will also need to sample test elements from
\bm{p}_{b}
, resulting in possibly much larger
\rho_{b}[i]
, and leading to more expensive bias estimations.

In the context of
adversarial contamination
, it is not clear how we can use flattening to obtain the properties we need for this approach. However, in
uniformity testing
, the above issues go away since we are only comparing
\bm{p}[i]
against the uniform probability
1/n
. In particular, as
\bm{p}[i]
becomes much larger than
1/n
, the error we can tolerate in the estimate of
\bm{p}[i]
also increases. Concretely, when
\bm{p}[i]>2/n
, we only need a constant factor multiplicative estimate of
\bm{p}[i]
. In summary, for uniformity testing we do not rely on flattening to achieve optimal query complexity and this allows for our approach to tolerate adversarial contamination, with some additional technical effort.

3
Preliminaries

Let
[n]=\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}
.
Let
a\ll b
denote that
a
is a (large) constant factor less than
b
.
We will use
\bm{p},\bm{q},\bm{r},D
to denote non-negative measures (typically probability distributions).
Given measures
\bm{p}_{1},\dotsc,\bm{p}_{t}
, let
\sum_{i=1}^{t}c_{i}\bm{p}_{i}
denote the measure that has mass
\sum c_{i}\bm{p}_{i}
.
In particular,
\frac{\bm{p}+\bm{q}}{2}
is a balanced mixture of
\bm{p},\bm{q}
.
We typically use
\bm{p}
to denote the target distribution (for which we would like to test some property) and
\bm{q}
to denote the noise distribution.

For a given subset
S\subseteq[n]
, let
(\bm{p}|S)
denote the conditional distribution of
\bm{p}
on
S
and
\bm{p}[S]
the pseudodistribution induced by restricting
\bm{p}
to
S
.
For example,
\bm{p}[i]
is the mass of the
i
-th bucket under
\bm{p}
.

Given a multi-set of samples
T\in[n]^{m}
over
[n]
, we say the frequency of the
i
-th bucket is the number of occurrences of
i
in
T
.
A
c
-collision is a tuple of
c
indices
(i_{1},\dotsc,i_{c})
such that
T_{i_{1}}=\dotsc=T_{i_{c}}
.
If
T
is sampled from a mixture
\bm{r}=\lambda\bm{p}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}
, a
\bm{p}
-collision (resp.
\bm{q}
-collision) is a pair of indices
(i,j)
where
T_{i}=T_{j}
and both are sampled from
\bm{p}
(resp.
\bm{q}
).

We give some useful concentration bounds.

Lemma 3.1

(Poisson Concentration (see e.g.
[
Can19
]
)
.

Let
X\sim\mathrm{Poi}(\lambda)
.
Then for any
x>0
,

\max(\Pr(X\geq\lambda+x),\Pr(X\leq\lambda-x))<e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2(\lambda+x)}}\text{.}

Note that
\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{c}^{c}
is the probability a given
c
-tuple of samples collide.
Linearity of expectation then yields the following lemma.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
c\geq 2
be an integer.
Let
X
be the number of
c
-collisions given
m
i.i.d. samples drawn from
\bm{p}
.
Then,
\mathbb{E}[X]=\binom{m}{c}\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{c}^{c}
.

Using Markov’s inequality, we can obtain a simple bound on the probability that a
c
-collision occurs when
\mathbb{E}[X]\ll 1
.

Corollary 3.3
.

Let
c\geq 2
be an integer and
t>1
.
Let
X
be the number of
c
-collisions given
m
i.i.d. samples drawn from
p
.
Then,
\Pr(X\geq t)\leq\frac{\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{c}^{c}\binom{m}{c}}{t}\leq O_{c}(\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{c}^{c}m^{c}/t)
.

3.1
Information Theory

We need the following tools from information theory.

Definition 3.4
.

Let
X
be a random variable over domain
{\cal X}
.
The
entropy
of
X
is

H(X)=\sum_{x\in{\cal X}}\Pr(X=x)\log\frac{1}{\Pr(X=x)}.

Definition 3.5
.

Let
X,Y
be random variables over domain
{\cal X}
.
The
KL-divergence
of
X,Y
is

D(X||Y)=\sum_{x\in{\cal X}}\Pr(X=x)\log\frac{\Pr(X=x)}{\Pr(Y=x)}.

Definition 3.6
.

Let
X,Y
be random variables over domain
{\cal X}
.
The
mutual information
of
X,Y
is

I(X:Y)=\sum_{x,y\in{\cal X}}\Pr((X,Y)=(x,y))\log\frac{\Pr((X,Y)=(x,y))}{\Pr(X=x)\Pr(Y=y)}.

Lemma 3.7

(Data Processing Inequality)
.

Let
X,Y,Z
be random variables over domain
{\cal X}
such that
Z
is independent of
X
conditioned on
Y
.
Then

I(X:Y)\geq I(X:Z).

The following shows that

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
X\sim\mathrm{Bern}(\frac{1}{2})
and
T
be a random variable possibly correlated with
X
.
If there exists a (randomized) function
f
such that
f(T)=X
with probability at least
51\%
probability, then
I(X:T)\geq 2\cdot 10^{-4}
.

The following inequality is an useful tool for bounding the mutual information.

Claim 3.9

(Asymptotic Upper Bound of Mutual Information)
.

Let
X
be an unbiased uniform random bit, and
M
be a discrete random variable possibly correlated with
X
.
Then the mutual information between the two random variables satisfies that

I(X:M)\leq 2\sum_{a}\frac{(\Pr[M=a|X=0]-\Pr[M=a|X=1])^{2}}{\Pr[M=a|X=0]+\Pr[M=a|X=1]}\text{.}

We give the proof of these standard facts for completeness in
Appendix

A
.

3.2
Split Distributions and Flattening

We require the flattening technique of
[
DK16
]
.

Definition 3.10

(Definition 2.4 of
[
DK16
]
)
.

Given a distribution
\bm{r}
on
[n]
and a multi-set
S
of elements of
[n]
, define the split distribution
\bm{r}_{S}
on
[n+|S|]
as follows:
For
1\leq i\leq n
, let
a_{i}
denote
1
plus the number of elements of
S
that are equal to
i
.
Thus,
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}=n+|S|
.
We can therefore associate the elements of
[n+|S|]
to elements of the set
B=\left\{(i,j):i\in[n],1\leq j\leq a_{i}\right\}
.
We now define a distribution
\bm{r}_{S}
with support
B
, by letting a random sample from
\bm{r}_{S}
be given by
(i,j)
, where
i
is drawn randomly from
\bm{r}
and
j
is drawn randomly from
[a_{i}]
.

Fact 3.11

(Fact 2.5 of
[
DK16
]
)
.

Let
\bm{p}
and
\bm{q}
be probability distributions on
[n]
, and
S
a given multi-set of
[n]
.
Then:
(1) We can simulate a sample from
\bm{p}_{S}
or
\bm{q}_{S}
by taking a single sample from
\bm{p}
or
\bm{q}
, respectively.
(2) It holds
\left\lVert\bm{p}_{S}-\bm{q}_{S}\right\rVert_{1}=\left\lVert\bm{p}-\bm{q}\right\rVert_{1}
.

Formally, (1) says that there is a function
f:=f_{S}
where
f(x)\sim\bm{p}_{S}
if
x\sim S
.

Lemma 3.12

(Lemma 2.6 of
[
DK16
]
)
.

Let
\bm{p}
be a distribution on
[n]
.
Then:
(1) For any multi-sets
S\subseteq S^{\prime}
of
[n]
,
\left\lVert\bm{p}_{S^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\left\lVert\bm{p}_{S}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
, and
(2) If
S
is obtained by taking
\mathrm{Poi}(m)
samples from
\bm{p}
, then
\mathbb{E}[\left\lVert\bm{p}_{S}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}]\leq\frac{1}{m}
.

3.3
Bias Estimation

We make heavy use of the following standard bias estimation result.

Lemma 3.13
.

Let
q<\frac{1}{2}
.
Given unknown coin probability
\rho\in(0,q)
,
O\left(\frac{q}{\varepsilon^{2}}\log(1/\delta)\right)
i.i.d.
\mathrm{Bern}(\rho)
trials suffice to attain an estimate
\hat{\rho}
such that
\Pr[|\rho-\hat{\rho}|>\varepsilon]<\delta
.

Proof.

Let
\hat{\rho}
be the empirical bias estimate from
m
trials.
The result follows immediately from a Chernoff bound.
In particular,

\Pr(|m\hat{\rho}-m\rho|>m\varepsilon)=\Pr\left(|m\hat{\rho}-m\rho|>\frac{\varepsilon}{\rho}m\rho\right)<e^{-\Omega\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\rho^{2}}m\rho\right)}=e^{-\Omega\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\rho}m\right)}\ll\delta

whenever
m\gg\frac{\rho}{\varepsilon^{2}}\log(1/\delta)
.
∎

In some cases, we will need a constant-factor approximation of an unknown coin probability. In such cases the above does not suffice, as we would need to set
\varepsilon\approx\rho
, but
\rho
is unknown. Thus, we will use the following. Note that we have not made an effort to optimize constants in favor of simplicity.

Lemma 3.14
.

Given unknown coin probability
\rho\in(0,1)
,
O(\rho^{-1})
i.i.d.
\mathrm{Bern}(\rho)
trials suffice to attain an estimate
\hat{\rho}
such that
\Pr[\rho/C\leq\hat{\rho}\leq C\rho]\geq 1-2/C
, for any
C\geq 1
.

Proof.

Perform
C/\rho
i.i.d.
\mathrm{Bern}(\rho)
trials. If no heads is seen, output fail, otherwise let
n
denote the location of the first heads and set
\hat{\rho}=1/n
. Observe that
n<t
means that a heads appears in the first
t
trials, and so

\Pr\left[n<1/(C\rho)\right]\leq\frac{1}{C\rho}\cdot\rho=\frac{1}{C}

by a union bound, whereas
n>t
means that the first
t
trials were entirely tails, and so

\Pr\left[n>C/\rho\right]\leq(1-\rho)^{C/\rho}\leq e^{-C}\leq 1/C

and so by a union bound
\Pr[1/(C\rho)\leq n\leq C/\rho]\geq 1-2/C
, and observe that
1/(C\rho)\leq n\leq C/\rho
is equivalent to
\rho/C\leq\hat{\rho}\leq C\rho
. ∎

Fact 3.15

(Basic Mixture Parameter Estimation)
.

Let
\bm{r}=\lambda\bm{p}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}
where
\lambda\in(0,1)
is an unknown mixture parameter. There is a procedure using
O(1/\lambda)
samples and queries which with probability
0.99
returns
\hat{\lambda}\in(\lambda/40000,\lambda]
.

Proof.

By querying one sample from
r
we have access to a
\lambda
-biased coin. Therefore, by invoking
Lemma

3.14
with
C=200
and dividing the result by
200
we obtain
\hat{\lambda}\in(\lambda/40000,\lambda]
with probability
0.99
. ∎

We can also obtain a more accurate estimate.

Lemma 3.16

(Mixture Parameter Estimation)
.

Let
\bm{r}=\lambda\bm{p}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}
where
\lambda\in(0,1)
is an unknown mixture parameter.
There is a procedure using
O(1/\eta^{2}\lambda)
samples and queries which with probability
0.98
returns
\hat{\lambda}\in((1-2\eta)\lambda,\lambda]
.

Proof.

First, compute
\hat{\lambda}_{0}\in(\lambda/40000,\lambda)
from
3.15
.
Then, we know
\lambda\leq 40000\hat{\lambda}_{0}
.
Now, with
Lemma

3.13
compute an estimate
|\hat{\lambda}-\lambda|\leq\eta\lambda
using
O\left(\frac{\hat{\lambda}}{\eta^{2}\lambda^{2}}\right)=O\left(\frac{1}{\eta^{2}\lambda}\right)
.
Note that this step succeeds with high constant probability.
Finally, note that
\hat{\lambda}-\eta\lambda\leq\lambda
and

(1-2\eta)\lambda\leq\hat{\lambda}-\eta\lambda\leq\lambda\text{.}

∎

4
Uniformity Testing

In this section, we present our algorithm for distributionally robust uniformity testing.

See
1.2

If
m=O\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}\lambda}\right)
, we can simply query all samples to obtain
O\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)
samples from
\bm{p}
and test uniformity of
\bm{p}
using known uniformity testing algorithms.
Note that such an algorithm uses
\Theta\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)=\Theta\left(\frac{n}{m\varepsilon^{4}}\right)
queries when
m=o(n)
.
Thus, we assume that
m=\Omega\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)
.

Let
\bm{r}:=\lambda\bm{p}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}
denote the mixture distribution to which we have sample access, where
\bm{p}
is the target distribution for which we would like to test uniformity.
Our algorithm will require the flattening technique of
[
DK16
]
.
Given a distribution
D
over
[n]
and function
f:[n]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, let
f(D)
denote the distribution given by
f(x)
where
x\sim D
.

Lemma 4.1

(Mixture Flattening)
.

Fix an integer
m>0
.
There is an algorithm that given
O(m)
samples to a distribution
\bm{r}=\lambda\bm{p}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}
over
[n]
with probability at least
0.99
outputs a random map
f\colon[n]\rightarrow[n+O(m)]
satisfying:

1.

\left\lVert f(D)-f(D^{\prime})\right\rVert_{1}=\left\lVert D-D^{\prime}\right\rVert_{1}
for any two distributions
D,D^{\prime}
over
[n]
.

2.

\left\lVert f\left(\bm{r}\right)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{m}
.

3.

\left\lVert f\left(\bm{p}\right)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda m}
.

Proof.

Our function
f:=f_{S}
where
S
consists of
\mathrm{Poi}(1000m)
iid samples from
\bm{r}
.
Thus, the first item immediately follows from
3.11
.
For the second item, since
Lemma

3.12
implies
\mathbb{E}[\left\lVert f(\bm{r})\right\rVert_{2}^{2}]\leq\frac{1}{1000m}
, Markov’s inequality guarantees that the second item holds with probability
0.999
.
For the third item, note that our multi-set
S
contains as a subset
\mathrm{Poi}(1000\lambda m)
iid samples from
\bm{p}
, we obtain the result using
Lemma

3.12
.

To bound the sample complexity, we terminate whenever
\mathrm{Poi}(1000m)
exceeds
10000m=O(m)
.
By
Lemma

3.1
), this occurs with probability at most
\exp(-\frac{81000m^{2}}{2000m})<0.001
.
A union bound concludes the proof.
∎

Next, we need a reduction from identity testing to uniformity testing.

Definition 4.2

(Identity Testing)
.

Given an explicit distribution
D^{*}
over
[n]
and sample access to distribution
D
over
[n]
, determine if
D=D^{*}
or
D
is
\varepsilon
-far from
D^{*}
.

Theorem 4.3

(
[
Gol20
]
)
.

For every distribution
D^{*}
over
[n]
and every
\varepsilon>0
, there is a reduction
F:[n]\rightarrow[6n]
that reduces
\varepsilon
-testing equality of unknown distribution
D
to known
D^{*}
to
\varepsilon/3
-testing uniformity of unknown distribution
F(D)
.
The same reduction
F
can be used for all
\varepsilon>0
.

Furthermore, for all distributions
D
,
\left\lVert F(D)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq 5\left\lVert D\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
.

Note that the above theorem immediately implies that our uniformity testing algorithms also give identity testing algorithms.

Proof.

We need to prove the last statement (the rest is immediate from
[
Gol20
]
).
Observe that in our setting we have that
F(\bm{r})=\lambda F(\bm{p})+(1-\lambda F(\bm{q})
so that samples and queries can be directly simulated.

Note that the reduction
F
is designed in three steps (see
[
Gol20
]
for details).
Let
D
be any distribution.
First, we transform samples from
D
to an even mixture of
D_{1}:=\frac{U_{n}+D}{2}
which guarantees every element has mass at least
\frac{1}{2n}
and at most
\frac{D[i]}{2}+\frac{1}{2n}
which is at most
\frac{1}{n}
if
D[i]\leq\frac{1}{n}
and at most
D[i]
otherwise.
Thus, assume
D_{1}[i]\leq\max(D[i],\frac{1}{n})
.

Now, assume any distribution
D_{1}
satisfies
D_{1}[i]\geq\frac{1}{2n}
for all
i
(and in particular
D^{*}
).
To define
D_{2}
, we transform samples
x\sim D_{1}
to output
x
with probability
\frac{\lfloor 6n\cdot D^{*}[i]\rfloor/6n}{D^{*}[i]}
and
n+1
otherwise.
Since
D^{*}[i]\geq\frac{1}{2n}
, we have
\frac{\lfloor 6n\cdot D^{*}[i]\rfloor/6n}{D^{*}[i]}\geq\frac{2}{3}
.
In particular, every
i\in[n]
has mass
D_{2}[i]\leq D[i]
and
[n+1]
has some mass at most
\frac{1}{3}
.
Note that
D_{2}[n+1]
depends only on
D^{*}
.

Now, note that
D_{2}[i]
is a multiple of
\frac{1}{6n}
for all
i
(this is guaranteed by the previous step).
We define
F(D):=D_{3}
by taking every element in
[n+1]
with mass
\frac{k}{6n}
for some integer
k
and splitting it uniformly into
k
buckets, which are disjoint for all
i
.
For any
i\in[n]
, note that
D_{3}[j]\leq D[i]
for all
j
that the
i
-th bucket is split into.
The bucket
[n+1]
which may be heavy is split into buckets each with mass
\frac{1}{6n}
.
Note that the mass of
[n+1]
does not depend on the input distribution
D
but only the fixed identity distribution
D^{*}
, so that these buckets have mass
\frac{1}{6n}
regardless of the input distribution
D
.
Thus, overall the
\ell_{2}
-norm of
D_{3}
for any input distribution
D
can be bounded by

\left\lVert D_{3}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{6n}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\max\left(D[i],\frac{1}{n}\right)^{2}\leq\frac{1}{6n}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(D[i]+\frac{1}{n}\right)^{2}\leq\left\lVert D\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\frac{19}{6n}\leq 5\left\lVert D\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

since any distribution
D
on
[n]
has
\ell_{2}
-norm at least
\frac{1}{n}
.
∎

Given the flattening technique, it suffices to consider distributions with small
\ell_{2}
norm (at the cost of collecting enough samples to flatten the distribution appropriately).
We thus design an algorithm for testing uniformity under the assumption that
\bm{r}
has bounded
\ell_{2}
-norm.

Lemma 4.4
.

Suppose
\left\lVert\bm{r}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq b
.
Then, there is an
\lambda
-distributionally robust
\varepsilon
-uniformity tester with
O\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}\lambda}\right)
sample complexity and
O\left(\frac{nb}{\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{2}}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{2}}\right)
query complexity.

Proof.

First, we use
Lemma

3.14
to obtain
\hat{\lambda}
such that
\frac{\lambda}{40000}\leq\hat{\lambda}\leq\lambda
with probability
0.99
.
We will design an algorithm that succeeds given a lower bound on
\lambda
.
Note that this requires
O\left(\lambda^{-1}\right)
samples and queries.
Thus, in the following assume that
\bm{r}=\lambda\bm{p}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}
where
\lambda\geq\hat{\lambda}
.

Let
m=O\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)
.
Our algorithm takes
\frac{m}{\hat{\lambda}}
samples from
\bm{r}
.
Let
m_{\bm{p}}
denote the (unknown) random variable that represents the number of samples drawn from
\bm{p}
.
Let
c(S_{\bm{p}})
denote the (unknown) random variable that represents the number of
(\bm{p},\bm{p})
-collisions in the sample and
c(S_{\bm{r}})
the (known) number of collisions in the sample.
Then, the analysis of
[
DGPP19
]
gives the following guarantee on
c(S_{\bm{p}})
.

Theorem 4.5

(
[
DGPP19
]
)
.

Suppose
m\gg\frac{3200\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}}
.
Let
c(S_{\bm{p}})
denote the number of collisions in
m
samples from
\bm{p}
.
Then, with probability at least
0.999
, the following hold:

1.

(Completeness) If
\bm{p}=U_{n}
is uniform, then
c(S_{\bm{p}})<\binom{m}{2}\frac{1+0.1\varepsilon^{2}}{n}
.

2.

(Soundness) If
\bm{p}
is
\varepsilon
-far from uniform, then
c(S_{\bm{p}})>\binom{m}{2}\frac{1+0.75\varepsilon^{2}}{n}
.

Due to further estimation errors in our algorithm, we have stated a slightly stronger correctness guarantee than
[
DGPP19
]
.
The details are deferred to the end of this section.

Our high level goal is therefore to estimate
c(S_{\bm{p}})
and to compare it to
\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{1+\frac{3}{4}\varepsilon^{2}}{n}
.
Note that
\mathbb{E}[m_{\bm{p}}]\geq m
but we do not know
m_{\bm{p}}
exactly.
First, we randomly query samples to compute an estimate
\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}
.

Claim 4.6
.

With
O\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{2}}\right)
queries, there is an algorithm that with probability
0.99
computes an estimate
\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}
such that

\frac{2}{n}\left|m_{\bm{p}}^{2}-\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}^{2}\right|\leq 0.01\frac{\varepsilon^{2}m^{2}}{n}\text{.}

Then, we randomly query collisions to compute an estimate
\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}})
.

Claim 4.7
.

There exists a universal constant
C>0
such that if
m_{\bm{p}}=\frac{C\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}}
there is an algorithm that uses
O\left(\frac{nb}{\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{2}}\right)
queries, and with probability
0.99
computes an estimate
\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}})
such that the following hold:

1.

If
\bm{p}
is uniform, then

\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}})\leq\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{1+0.1\varepsilon^{2}}{n}\text{.}

2.

If
\bm{p}
is far from uniform, then

\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}})-\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{1+0.4\varepsilon^{2}}{n}\geq\max\left(\frac{m_{\bm{p}}^{2}\alpha}{30n},\frac{0.3m_{\bm{p}}^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}{n}\right)

where
\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\frac{1+\alpha}{n}
.

We are now ready to describe the full uniformity testing algorithm.

1.

Estimate
\hat{\lambda}
using
3.15
.

2.

Let
m\geq\frac{Cn^{1/2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}
for some sufficiently large constant
C
.

3.

Draw
\frac{m}{\hat{\lambda}}
samples from
\bm{r}
.

4.

Compute
\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}
using
4.6
.

5.

Compute
\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}})
using
4.7
.

6.

Output accept if
\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}})<\binom{\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{1+0.4\varepsilon^{2}}{n}
.
Otherwise reject.

We begin by arguing that
0.99m\leq m_{\bm{p}}
.
Since each sample comes from
\bm{p}
with probability
\lambda\geq\hat{\lambda}
and we take
m/\hat{\lambda}
samples, a simple Chernoff bound guarantees that

\Pr\left(|m_{\bm{p}}-m|\leq 0.01m\right)<e^{-\Omega\left(m\right)}\ll 0.01\text{.}

Condition on the event that
m_{\bm{p}}\geq 0.99m>\frac{C\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}}
for some large constant
C
.

Suppose
\bm{p}
is uniform.
By
4.7
, we have that with probability at least
0.99
,

\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}})\leq\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{1+0.1\varepsilon^{2}}{n}

Of course, we do not know
m_{\bm{p}}
, but we can estimate it using
4.6
.
In particular, we have

\displaystyle\left|\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{1+0.4\varepsilon^{2}}{n}-\binom{\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{1+0.4\varepsilon^{2}}{n}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{n}\left|\frac{(m_{\bm{p}}^{2}-m_{\bm{p}})-(\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}^{2}-\hat{m}_{\bm{p}})}{2}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{n}\left|m_{\bm{p}}^{2}-\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}^{2}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{0.1\varepsilon^{2}m^{2}}{n}\text{.}

Using the triangle inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle\binom{\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{1+0.4\varepsilon^{2}}{n}-\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}})

\displaystyle\geq\left(\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{1+0.4\varepsilon^{2}}{n}-\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}})\right)-\left|\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{1+0.4\varepsilon^{2}}{n}-\binom{\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{1+0.4\varepsilon^{2}}{n}\right|

\displaystyle\geq\left(\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{1+0.4\varepsilon^{2}}{n}-\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}})\right)-\frac{0.01\varepsilon^{2}m^{2}}{n}>0\text{.}

Thus, the algorithm outputs accept given the conditioned events, which occur with probability at least
0.9
by a union bound.

The soundness case (
\bm{p}
is
\varepsilon
-far from uniform) follows similarly.

4.7
guarantees the following

\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}})-\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{1+0.4\varepsilon^{2}}{n}\geq\max\left(\frac{m_{\bm{p}}^{2}\alpha}{30n},\frac{0.3m_{\bm{p}}^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}{n}\right)\text{.}

In particular, since

0.1\frac{\varepsilon^{2}m^{2}}{n}\leq\max\left(\frac{m_{\bm{p}}^{2}\alpha}{30n},\frac{0.3m_{\bm{p}}^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}{n}\right)

the algorithm outputs reject with probability at least
0.9
as desired (applying the union bound over required events as in the case for uniformity).

We now analyze the complexity.
The algorithm takes
O\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\lambda\varepsilon^{2}}\right)
samples and
O\left(\frac{nb}{\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{2}}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{2}}\right)
queries.
Note that the samples and queries required to obtain our initial estimate of
\lambda
do not affect the final complexity.
∎

We are now ready to conclude the proof of
Theorem

1.2
.

Proof of
Theorem

1.2
.

Fix
m\geq\frac{C\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}\hat{\lambda}}
for a sufficiently large constant
C
as required by
Lemma

4.4
.
Consider two cases.
Let
m\leq n
.
Using
Lemma

4.1
and
Theorem

4.3
, we can use
O(m)
samples to obtain a reduction
F
such that
\left\lVert F(\bm{r})\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{m}
and we may consider testing uniformity of
F(\bm{p})
on the domain
[n+O(m)]=[O(n)]
.
Now, consider two cases.

Lemma

4.4
yields an algorithm using
O(m)
samples and
O\left(\frac{n}{m\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{2}}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{2}}\right)=O\left(\frac{n}{m\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{2}}\right)
queries.

If
m\geq n
, we instead use
O(n)=O(m)
samples to obtain a reduction
F
such that
\left\lVert F(\bm{r})\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{n}
and we may consider testing uniformity of
F(\bm{p})
on the domain
[O(n)]
.

Lemma

4.4
yields an algorithm using
O(m)
samples and
O\left(\frac{n}{n\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{2}}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{2}}\right)=O\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{2}}\right)
queries.
∎

4.1
Deferred Proofs for Uniformity Testing

We begin with the correctness analysis of the uniformity tester of
[
DGPP19
]
.

Proof of
Theorem

4.5
.

Lemmas 2 and 3 of
[
DGPP19
]
states that

\mathbb{E}[c(S_{\bm{p}})]=\binom{m}{2}\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\quad,\quad\mathrm{Var}(c(S_{\bm{p}}))\leq m^{2}\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+m^{3}(\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{3}^{3}-\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{4})\text{.}

(1)

We begin with the completeness case.
Since
\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{3}^{3}=\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{4}=\frac{1}{n^{2}}
we have
\mathrm{Var}(c(S_{\bm{p}}))\leq m^{2}/n
.
By Chebyshev’s inequality,

\Pr\left(c(S_{\bm{p}})>\binom{m}{2}\frac{1+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{10}}{n}\right)=\Pr\left(c(S_{\bm{p}})-\mathbb{E}[c(S_{\bm{p}})]>\binom{m}{2}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{10m\sqrt{n}}\frac{m}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\leq\frac{n}{100\varepsilon^{4}m^{2}}

which is less than
0.001
for
m\geq\frac{1000\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}}
.
The soundness case follows directly from the correctness analysis of
[
DGPP19
]
.
∎

We show how to estimate
\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}
.

Proof of
4.6
.

Fix some threshold
t
.
Recall that our algorithm draws
\frac{m}{\hat{\lambda}}
samples where each sample is independently drawn from
\bm{p}
with probability
\lambda
and from
\bm{q}
otherwise.
If we query a random sample, it is drawn from
