\bm{p}
with probability
\frac{m_{\bm{p}}}{m/\lambda}
.
We query
q
random samples.
Let
\hat{s}_{\bm{p}}
be the number of queried samples drawn from
\bm{p}
so that

\mathbb{E}[\hat{s}_{\bm{p}}]=q\frac{m_{\bm{p}}}{m/\lambda}=m_{\bm{p}}\frac{\lambda q}{m}\text{.}

Define
\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}=\hat{s}_{\bm{p}}\frac{m}{\lambda q}
so that
\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}
is an unbiased estimator of
m_{\bm{p}}
.
By a standard Chernoff bound, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left(\left|\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}-m_{\bm{p}}\right|>tm\right)

\displaystyle=\Pr\left(\left|\hat{s}_{\bm{p}}-s_{\bm{p}}\right|>\lambda qt\right)<\exp\left(-2t^{2}q\lambda^{2}\right)<0.01

for
q=O\left(t^{-2}\lambda^{-2}\right)
.
Then, note that

|\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}^{2}-m_{\bm{p}}^{2}|=(\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}+m_{\bm{p}})|\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}-m_{\bm{p}}|\leq 2m^{2}t

so that

\frac{2}{n}|\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}^{2}-m_{\bm{p}}^{2}|\leq\frac{4m^{2}t}{n}\ll\frac{\varepsilon^{2}m^{2}}{n}

for
t\ll\varepsilon^{2}
.
In particular, the algorithm requires
q=O\left(\varepsilon^{-4}\lambda^{-2}\right)
queries.
∎

We show how to estimate
\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}})
.

Proof of
4.7
.

We begin by bounding
c(S_{\bm{r}})
.
Since
\mathbb{E}[c(S_{\bm{r}})]=\binom{m/\hat{\lambda}}{2}\left\lVert\bm{r}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
we have by Markov’s inequality that
c(S_{\bm{r}})\leq 1000\frac{m^{2}}{\hat{\lambda}^{2}}\left\lVert\bm{r}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq 1000\frac{m^{2}b}{\hat{\lambda}^{2}}
with probability
0.999
.
Similarly,
\mathbb{E}[c(S_{\bm{p}})]=\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
and Markov’s inequality implies that
c(S_{\bm{p}})=O\left(m^{2}\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)
with probability
0.999
.
We condition on these events.
Furthermore, note that if we query a random collision, it is a
(\bm{p},\bm{p})
-collision with probability
\frac{c(S_{\bm{p}})}{c(S_{\bm{r}})}
.

Consider the case where
\bm{p}
is uniform.
Then, from (
1
) we have
\mathrm{Var}(c(S_{\bm{p}}))\leq m_{\bm{p}}^{2}/n
so that
\sigma:=\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}(c(S_{\bm{p}}))}\leq\frac{m_{\bm{p}}}{\sqrt{n}}
.
Recall that we condition on the event that
|m_{\bm{p}}-m|\leq 0.001m
.
By Chebyshev’s inequality, we have that

\Pr\left(c(S_{\bm{p}})-\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{1}{n}>\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{0.01\varepsilon^{2}}{n}\right)=\Pr\left(c(S_{\bm{p}})-\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{1}{n}>\frac{0.004m_{\bm{p}}^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}{n}\right)=O\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}m_{\bm{p}}}\right)\leq 0.001

for
m_{\bm{p}}=O\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)
for some sufficiently large constant.
Then, Markov’s inequality implies that with probability
0.999
we have that
c(S_{\bm{p}})=O\left(\frac{m^{2}}{n}\right)
and
c(S_{\bm{r}})=O\left(m^{2}b\lambda^{-2}\right)
.
We query
q
independently chosen collisions to obtain an estimate
\frac{\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}})}{c(S_{\bm{r}})}
such that with probability at least
0.999
,

\left|\frac{\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}})}{c(S_{\bm{r}})}-\frac{c(S_{\bm{p}})}{c(S_{\bm{r}})}\right|\ll\frac{\varepsilon^{2}m^{2}}{n\cdot c(S_{\bm{r}})}\text{.}

From
Lemma

3.13
, this requires

O\left(\frac{n^{2}\cdot c(S_{\bm{r}})^{2}}{m^{4}\varepsilon^{4}}\cdot\frac{c(S_{\bm{p}})}{c(S_{\bm{r}})}\right)=O\left(\frac{n^{2}\cdot c(S_{\bm{r}})\cdot c(S_{\bm{p}})}{m^{4}\varepsilon^{4}}\right)

queries.
Applying our bounds on
c(S_{\bm{p}}),c(S_{\bm{r}})
, we obtain the query bound
O\left(\frac{nb}{\lambda^{2}\varepsilon^{4}}\right)
.
Thus, if both estimates are correct we have that

\displaystyle\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{1+0.4\varepsilon^{2}}{n}-\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}})

\displaystyle\geq\left|\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{1+0.4\varepsilon^{2}}{n}-\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{1}{n}\right|-\left|\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{1}{n}-c(S_{\bm{p}})\right|-\left|\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}})-c(S_{\bm{p}})\right|

\displaystyle\geq\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{0.3\varepsilon^{2}}{n}\text{.}

Rearranging, we obtain the desired bound.

Now, consider the case that
\bm{p}
is far from uniform.
A standard calculation shows that

\left\lVert\bm{p}-\frac{1}{n}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}-\frac{1}{n}\geq\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{n}\text{.}

In particular, let
\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\frac{1+\alpha}{n}
where
\alpha\geq\varepsilon^{2}
.
If
\alpha=O\left(1\right)
, then the we apply the same argument as in the uniform case since we have the same bound on
c(S_{\bm{p}})
.
Thus assume
\alpha=\omega(1)
(e.g.
\alpha\geq 30
).

We consider two cases.
Either
m_{\bm{p}}^{2}\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\geq m_{\bm{p}}^{3}(\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{3}^{3}-\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{4})
or vice versa.
Let
\sigma:=\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}(c(S_{\bm{p}}))}
denote the standard deviation of
c(S_{\bm{p}})
.

Case 1:
m^{2}\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\geq m^{3}(\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{3}^{3}-\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{4})

Since
\mathrm{Var}(c(S_{\bm{p}}))\leq 2m^{2}\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
, we have
\sigma^{2}\leq 2m^{2}\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\frac{2m_{\bm{p}}^{2}(1+\alpha)}{n}
.
In particular, with high constant probability,
c(S_{\bm{p}})
is within
O\left(\frac{m_{\bm{p}}\sqrt{1+\alpha}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)
of its expectation
\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{1+\alpha}{n}
.
Note that for large enough
n
(and therefore large enough
m_{\bm{p}}
)

\mathbb{E}[c(S_{\bm{p}})]-\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{1+0.4\varepsilon^{2}}{n}=\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{\alpha-0.4\varepsilon^{2}}{n}\geq\frac{m_{\bm{p}}^{2}\alpha}{10n}\text{.}

(2)

Furthermore, we have

O\left(\frac{m_{\bm{p}}\sqrt{1+\alpha}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\ll\frac{m_{\bm{p}}^{2}\alpha}{10n}

(3)

as long as
m_{\bm{p}}=O\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\alpha}\right)=O\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)
for some sufficiently large constant.
In particular, by Chebyshev’s inequality, the expectation gap (
2
) and variance bound (
3
) we have with high constant probability that

c(S_{\bm{p}})-\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{1+0.4\varepsilon^{2}}{n}\geq\frac{m_{\bm{p}}^{2}\alpha}{20n}\text{.}

Our goal is now to bound

|\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}})-c(S_{\bm{p}})|\ll\frac{m_{\bm{p}}^{2}\alpha}{n}\text{.}

(4)

Following similar arguments using
Lemma

3.13
this requires

O\left(\frac{n^{2}c(S_{\bm{r}})^{2}}{m_{\bm{p}}^{4}\alpha^{2}}\cdot\frac{c(S_{\bm{p}})}{c(S_{\bm{r}})}\right)=O\left(\frac{n^{2}c(S_{\bm{r}})}{m_{\bm{p}}^{4}\alpha^{2}}\cdot c(S_{\bm{p}})\right)=O\left(\frac{nb}{\alpha\lambda^{2}}\right)=O\left(\frac{nb}{\varepsilon^{2}\lambda^{2}}\right)

queries where we have observed that
c(S_{\bm{p}})=O\left(m^{2}\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)=O\left(\frac{m^{2}\alpha}{n}\right)
.
In particular, with high constant probability, we have
\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}})-\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{1+0.4\varepsilon^{2}}{n}\geq\frac{m_{\bm{p}}^{2}\alpha}{30n}
.

Case 2:
m^{2}\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq m^{3}(\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{3}^{3}-\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{4})

Let
\bm{p}[i]=\frac{1}{n}+a[i]
for some vector
a
.
We bound the variance as

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}(c(S_{\bm{p}}))

\displaystyle\leq 2m_{\bm{p}}^{3}\left(\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{3}^{3}-\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{4}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2m_{\bm{p}}^{3}\left(\left(\sum_{i}\left(\frac{1}{n}+a[i]\right)^{3}\right)-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=2m_{\bm{p}}^{3}\left(\left(\sum_{i}\frac{1}{n^{3}}+\frac{3a[i]}{n^{2}}+\frac{3a[i]^{2}}{n}+a[i]^{3}\right)-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=2m_{\bm{p}}^{3}\left(\frac{3\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}{n}+\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{3}^{3}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2m_{\bm{p}}^{3}\left(\frac{3\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}{n}+\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{2}^{3}\right)

where we have used
\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{n}
for all distributions
\bm{p}
and
\sum_{i}a[i]=0
.
Now, observe that
\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\frac{1+\alpha}{n}
so that
\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\frac{\alpha}{n}
so that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}(c(S_{\bm{p}}))

\displaystyle\leq 2m_{\bm{p}}^{3}\left(\frac{3\alpha}{n^{2}}+\frac{\alpha^{3/2}}{n^{3/2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{6m_{\bm{p}}^{3}\alpha}{n^{2}}+\frac{2m_{\bm{p}}^{3}\alpha^{3/2}}{n^{3/2}}\text{.}

In particular, we have
\sigma\leq\frac{6m_{\bm{p}}^{3/2}\alpha^{1/2}}{n}+\frac{2m_{\bm{p}}^{3/2}\alpha^{3/4}}{n^{3/4}}
.
As in Case 1, we hope to bound the standard deviation against the expectation gap (
2
) i.e.

\displaystyle\frac{6m_{\bm{p}}^{3/2}\alpha^{1/2}}{n}+\frac{2m_{\bm{p}}^{3/2}\alpha^{3/4}}{n^{3/4}}\ll\frac{m_{\bm{p}}^{2}\alpha}{n}

which follows as long as
m_{\bm{p}}=O\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}+\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\sqrt{\alpha}}\right)
for some sufficiently large constant.
In particular,
m_{\bm{p}}=O\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)
suffices.
The remainder of the argument (estimating
c(S_{\bm{p}})
using
\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}})
) follows identically as in Case 1.
∎

4.2
Adversarially Robust Algorithm for Uniformity Testing

We present an
\lambda
-adversarially robust
\varepsilon
-uniformity tester with near-optimal sample and query complexity.

See
1.7

We begin with a high level overview of the algorithm.

Algorithm Overview

A close inspection of our distributionally robust algorithm shows that the algorithm essentially requires two main ingredients:

1.

There are thresholds
T_{1}\ll T_{2}
such that in the completeness case (
\bm{p}
is uniform) there are at most
T_{1}

\bm{p}
-collisions while in the soundness case (
\bm{p}
is far from uniform) that are at least
T_{2}

\bm{p}
-collisions.
This condition is met as long as sufficiently many samples are taken from
\bm{p}
(i.e.
m_{\bm{p}}\gg\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}}
).

2.

S_{\bm{r}}
does not produce too many collisions so we can estimate the number of
\bm{p}
-collisions without too many queries.
This condition is met as long as the flattening procedure succeeds (i.e.
\left\lVert\bm{r}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\ll\frac{1}{m}
).

In the adversarial setting, the first condition is easy to ensure by taking at least
O\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}\lambda}\right)
samples.
However, the second condition is no longer evident, as the adversarial samples do not have to be chosen iid from some distribution.
Nevertheless, we are able to design an adversarially robust algorithm using a more delicate flattening procedure.
Instead of using the first half of samples to flatten and the second half to test uniformity of the flattened distribution, we will randomly choose which set of samples to flatten with and which set of samples to test uniformity of
\bm{p}
with.

In the distributional setting, we were able to show that
\left\lVert\bm{r}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{m}
so that
m
samples produce
O(m)
collisions.
In the adversarial setting, we would similarly hope to bound the number of collisions as
O(m)
.
If
Y_{i}
is the observed frequency of the
i
-th bucket (so
m=\sum Y_{i}
) this is roughly
\sum_{i}Y_{i}^{2}
which subject to the constraint is maximized when a single
Y_{i}
is maximized.
If
\max_{i}Y_{i}\leq B
, then we have at most
O(mB)
-collisions.
If we randomly choose each sample to be a flattening sample with probability
\frac{1}{2}
, then we ensure that for large
Y_{i}>B
, the number of flattening samples in the
i
-th bucket is at least
\Omega(Y_{i})
with high probability (e.g.
1-e^{-\Omega(B)}
).
Thus, a union bound shows that it suffices to choose
B=O(\log n)
which yields an upper bound of
O(m\log n)
collisions in the randomly subsampled test dataset.

We are now ready to prove
Theorem

1.7
.

Proof of
Theorem

1.7
.

We begin by estimating
\lambda
using
3.15
.
A key ingredient will be the following analogue to flattening
Lemma

4.1
.

Lemma 4.8

(Adversarial Flattening)
.

There is an algorithm that given an
m
samples from a
(1-\lambda)
-adversarially contaminated source, with probability
0.99
outputs a map
F:[n]\rightarrow[n+m]
and a dataset
S_{{\rm test}}\subset S
satisfying the following:

1.

F(S_{{\rm test}})
contains at most
O(m\log n)
-collisions,

2.

S_{{\rm test}}
contains at least
\Omega\left(\lambda m\right)
iid samples from
\bm{p}
,

3.

\left\lVert\bm{q}_{1}-\bm{q}_{2}\right\rVert_{1}=\left\lVert F(\bm{q}_{1})-F(\bm{q}_{2})\right\rVert_{1}
for all distributions
\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2}
.

Thus, if we fix
m\geq\frac{C^{\prime}\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}\hat{\lambda}}
for sufficiently large constant
C^{\prime}
, the above lemma yields a dataset
F(S_{{\rm test}})
with at most
O(m\log n)
-collisions and at least
\frac{C\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}}
iid samples from
\bm{p}
for
C
required by
Lemma

4.4
.

Proof of
Lemma

4.8
.

Consider an algorithm that takes
m
iid samples
S
and evenly distributes (independently with probability
\frac{1}{2}
) the observed samples
S
into two datasets:
S_{{\rm flat}},S_{{\rm test}}
.
Let
Y_{i}
denote the frequency of the
i
-th bucket in
S
and
Y_{i}^{{\rm flat}},Y_{i}^{{\rm test}}
denote the frequency of the
i
-th bucket in
S_{{\rm flat}},S_{{\rm test}}
respectively.
We argue that for large
Y_{i}
,
Y_{i}^{{\rm flat}}=\Omega(Y_{i})
.

Claim 4.9
.

With probability at least
0.999
,
Y_{i}^{{\rm flat}}\geq\frac{Y_{i}}{4}
for all
Y_{i}\geq 100\log n
.

Proof.

Note that
Y_{i}^{{\rm flat}}
is the sum of
Y_{i}
iid Bernoulli random variables with parameter
\frac{1}{2}
.
Thus,
\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}^{{\rm flat}}]=\frac{Y_{i}}{2}
.
By a standard Chernoff bound, we have
\Pr\left(Y_{i}^{{\rm flat}}<\frac{Y_{i}}{4}\right)<\exp\left(-\frac{Y_{i}}{16}\right)<\frac{0.001}{n}
for
Y_{i}\geq 100\log n
.
∎

We now describe how to obtain
F
.
Let
F
describe the (randomized) map
F_{0}:[n]\rightarrow[n+|S_{{\rm flat}}|]
defined as the split distribution induced by
S_{{\rm flat}}
(see
Definition

3.10
).

Let
F(S_{{\rm test}})
denote the dataset obtained by independently applying
F
to each element of
S_{{\rm test}}
.
Let
c(F(S_{{\rm test}}))
denote the number of collisions in
F(S_{{\rm test}})
.
We bound the number of collisions in
F(S_{{\rm test}})
.

Lemma 4.10
.

With probability
0.999
over samples
S
,
c(F(S_{{\rm test}}))=O(m\log n)
.

Proof.

Fix a bucket
i\in[n]
.
Suppose
Y_{i}\geq 100\log n
.
Two samples in the
i
-th bucket are mapped to the same sub-bucket
j
with probability
\frac{1}{Y_{i}^{{\rm flat}}+1}
.
Since there are
\binom{Y_{i}^{{\rm test}}}{2}\leq Y_{i}^{2}
pairs in the
i
-th bucket the number of collisions in the
i
-th bucket is in expectation
O\left(Y_{i}\right)
.
Summing over all
n
buckets, the number of collisions in total is in expectation
O\left(m\right)
.
Thus, Markov’s inequality implies that the total number of collisions in
F(S_{{\rm test}})
is
O(m)
with probability
0.999
.

It remains to count collisions in buckets with
Y_{i}\leq 100\log n
.
Note that the number of collisions is at most
\sum_{Y_{i}\leq 100\log n}\binom{Y_{i}^{{\rm test}}}{2}=O\left(m\log n\right)
since the sum of squares (constrained on
\sum Y_{i}\leq m
) is maximized when individual terms are maximized.
∎

Finally, we argue that
S_{{\rm test}}
contains many samples from
\bm{p}
.

Claim 4.11
.

With probability
0.999
,
m_{\bm{p}}\geq\frac{C\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}}
for a sufficiently large constant
C
required by
4.7
.

Proof.

Note that
|S_{{\rm test}}|
is a sum of
|S|=m
iid Bernoulli random variables with parameter
\frac{1}{2}
so that a standard Chernoff bound shows
\frac{m}{2}-0.001m\leq|S_{{\rm test}}|\leq\frac{m}{2}+0.001m
with high probability as
m\geq\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}\lambda}
.
Note that
m_{\bm{p}}
is the sum of (at least)
\lambda m\geq\frac{C\lambda\sqrt{n}}{\hat{\lambda}\varepsilon^{2}}\geq\frac{C\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}}
iid Bernoulli random variables with parameter
\frac{1}{2}
so a Chernoff bound yields
m_{\bm{p}}\geq\frac{\lambda m}{4}\geq\frac{C\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}}
with high probability.
We conclude with a union bound.
∎

A union bound concludes the proof of
Lemma

4.8
.
Note that the final item follows directly from
3.11
.
∎

Let
F(U_{n})
denote the split distribution obtained by taking a sample from
U_{n}
and applying
F
.
Then, let
F^{*}(x)=F^{\prime}(F(x))
where
F^{\prime}
is the reduction reducing
\varepsilon
-testing of equality to
F(U_{n})
to
\varepsilon/3
-testing of uniformity on
[6(n+|S_{{\rm flat}}|)]
.

Now, we argue that we can determine if
\bm{p}
is uniform or far from uniform efficiently with queries.
As in the distributional case, note that
F(U_{n})
is uniform if
\bm{p}
is uniform while
F(\bm{p})
is
\varepsilon/3
-far from uniform if
\bm{p}
is
\varepsilon
-far from uniform.
Furthermore, by applying
F
independently to each sample from
S_{{\rm test}}
, the uncorrupted iid samples from
\bm{p}
in
S_{{\rm test}}
are simply uncorrupted iid samples from
F(\bm{p})
in
F(S_{{\rm test}})
.
Thus,
F(S_{{\rm test}})
contains at least
m_{\bm{p}}=\Omega\left(\lambda m\right)\geq\frac{C\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}}
samples from
\bm{p}
.

Note that we can estimate
\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}
using
4.6
.
In particular, this since this is bias estimation up to error
\varepsilon^{2}\lambda
which requires
O(\varepsilon^{-4}\lambda^{-2})
queries.

It remains to show how to estimate
c(F(S_{{\rm test}})_{\bm{p}})
, the number of
\bm{p}
-collisions in
F(S_{{\rm test}})
, analogously to
4.7
.
By our lower bound on
m_{\bm{p}}
, the assumption of
4.7
is satisfied.
Following similar analysis, we obtain the following sub-routine.

Claim 4.12
.

There exists a universal constant
C>0
such that if
m_{\bm{p}}=\frac{C\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}}
there is an algorithm that uses
O\left(\frac{n\log n}{\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{2}}\right)
queries, and with probability
0.99
computes an estimate
\hat{c}
such that the following hold:

1.

If
\bm{p}
is uniform, then

\hat{c}\leq\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{1+0.1\varepsilon^{2}}{n}\text{.}

2.

If
\bm{p}
is
\varepsilon
-far from uniform, then

\hat{c}-\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{1+0.4\varepsilon^{2}}{n}\geq\max\left(\frac{m_{\bm{p}}^{2}\alpha}{30n},\frac{0.3m_{\bm{p}}^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}{n}\right)

where
\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\frac{1+\alpha}{n}
.

Proof.

Since
m_{\bm{p}}
is sufficiently large, the expectation and variance of
c(F(S_{{\rm test}})_{\bm{p}})
holds exactly as in
4.7
.
In particular,
c(F(S_{{\rm test}})_{\bm{p}})
satisfies the same properties as
c(S_{\bm{p}})
in both completeness and soundness cases.

The query bound to estimate
\hat{c}
then follows from analogous analysis in
4.7
and
Lemma

4.10
.
For example, in the case
\bm{p}
is uniform, we can obtain an estimate
\hat{c}(F(S_{{\rm test}})_{\bm{p}})
with the same guarantees using

O\left(\frac{n^{2}c(F(S_{{\rm test}}))c(F(S_{{\rm test}})_{\bm{p}})}{m_{\bm{p}}^{4}\varepsilon^{4}}\right)=O\left(\frac{n^{2}(m\log n)(\lambda^{2}m^{2}/n)}{(\lambda^{4}m^{4})\varepsilon^{4}}\right)=O\left(\frac{n\log n}{m\lambda^{2}\varepsilon^{4}}\right)

queries, where in the second inequality we have upper bounded
c(F(S_{{\rm test}}))
from
Lemma

4.10
and
c(F(S_{{\rm test}})_{\bm{p}})
from (the proof of)
4.7
, and lower bounded
m_{\bm{p}}
from
4.11
.

A similar query bound follows for the case when
\bm{p}
is far from uniform.
For example, if
\alpha\geq 30
, we require

O\left(\frac{n^{2}c(F(S_{{\rm test}}))c(F(S_{{\rm test}})_{\bm{p}})}{m_{\bm{p}}^{4}\alpha^{2}}\right)=O\left(\frac{n^{2}(m\log n)(\lambda^{2}m^{2}\alpha/n)}{(\lambda^{4}m^{4})\alpha^{2}}\right)=O\left(\frac{n\log n}{m\lambda^{2}\alpha}\right)=O\left(\frac{n\log n}{m\lambda^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}\right)

queries.
Note that we have lost a
O(\log n)
-factor in query complexity as
Lemma

4.10
is weaker than the upper bound on
c(S_{\bm{r}})
in the distributional contamination setting.
∎

Following the above discussion, we describe the full adversarially robust algorithm.

1.

Estimate
\hat{\lambda}
using
3.15
.

2.

Let
m\geq\frac{Cn^{1/2}}{\varepsilon^{2}\hat{\lambda}}
for some sufficiently large constant
C
. Drawn
m
iid samples, denoted
S
.

3.

Compute
S_{{\rm test}},F
with
Lemma

4.8
.

4.

In the remaining algorithm, consider testing uniformity against
F(U_{n})
.

5.

Compute
\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}
using
4.6
and
\hat{c}
using
4.12
.

6.

Output accept if
\hat{c}<\binom{\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{1+0.4\varepsilon^{2}}{n}
.
Otherwise reject.

Correctness follows from identical analysis as in the distributional contamination setting (
Theorem

1.2
).
The sample complexity and query complexity bounds follow as above.
∎

5
Closeness Testing

In this section, we establish the following upper bound for closeness testing in the verification query model.

Theorem 5.1

(Closeness Testing with Verification Queries)
.

Suppose we are given sample access and verification query access to two mixtures
\bm{r}_{1}=\lambda\bm{p}_{1}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}_{1}
,
r_{2}=\lambda\bm{p}_{2}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}_{2}
with unknown mixture parameter
\lambda\in(0,1)
, and arbitrary distributions
\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2},\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2}\colon[n]\to[0,1]
. For all
m\leq O(n)
, there is an algorithm that distinguishes between the case of
\bm{p}_{1}=\bm{p}_{2}
and
\left\lVert\bm{p}_{1}-\bm{p}_{2}\right\rVert_{1}\geq\varepsilon
with probability
2/3
using

O\left(m+\frac{n^{2/3}}{\varepsilon^{4/3}\lambda}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{3}}\right)~\text{ samples and }~O\left(\frac{n^{2}}{m^{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{3}}\right)~\text{ verification queries.}~

Our proof works in two steps similarly to standard closeness testing: first, use the classic flattening technique of
[
DK16
]
to "reduce" the
2
-norm of the mixtures
\bm{r}_{1},\bm{r}_{2}
to
b\approx 1/m
using
O(m)
samples up-front, then use a closeness tester for
\ell_{2}
distance where the sample complexity improves for small
b
. For the first step we slightly strengthen the flattening lemma, which we also used crucially for our uniformity testing result, to the distributional contamination setting.

See
4.1

Next, we use the following closeness testing result under
\ell_{2}
distance. This is the main component of the proof which we obtain by extending the result of
[
DGPP19
]
to the verification query setting.

Theorem 5.2
.

(Tolerant
\ell_{2}
Closeness Testing with Verification Queries.)

Suppose we are given sample access and verification query access to two mixtures
\bm{r}_{1}=\lambda\bm{p}_{1}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}_{1}
,
\bm{r}_{2}=\lambda\bm{p}_{2}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}_{2}
with unknown mixture parameter
\lambda\in(0,1)
, and arbitrary distributions
\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2},\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2}\colon[n]\to[0,1]
. Moreover, suppose we are provided a number
b\in[1/n,1]
such that
\left\lVert\bm{r}_{1}\right\rVert_{2}^{2},\left\lVert\bm{r}_{2}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq b
and
\left\lVert\bm{p}_{1}\right\rVert_{2}^{2},\left\lVert\bm{p}_{2}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq b/\lambda
. Then, there is an algorithm that distinguishes between the case of
\left\lVert\bm{p}_{1}-\bm{p}_{2}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\varepsilon/4
and
\left\lVert\bm{p}_{1}-\bm{p}_{2}\right\rVert_{2}\geq\varepsilon
with probability
9/10
using

O\left(\frac{\sqrt{b}}{\varepsilon^{2}\lambda^{3/2}}+\frac{b^{2}}{\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{3}}\right)~\text{ samples and }~O\left(\frac{b^{2}}{\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{3}}\right)~\text{ verification queries.}

We prove
Theorem

5.2
in
Section

5.1
, and now continue to the proof of the main theorem.

Proof of
Theorem

5.1
.
Our tester is defined as follows.

1.

Let
\min\left(n,\frac{5600n^{2/3}}{\varepsilon^{4/3}\lambda}\right)\leq m\leq n
.

2.

Invoke the flattening procedure (
Lemma

4.1
) using
O(m)
samples from
\frac{1}{2}(\bm{r}_{1}+\bm{r}_{2})
to obtain a random map
f
preserving
\ell_{1}
distances (item (1) of
Lemma

4.1
) for which
\left\lVert f(\frac{1}{2}(\bm{r}_{1}+\bm{r}_{2}))\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{m}
.

3.

Invoke the
\ell_{2}
-closeness tester (
Theorem

5.1
) on
f(\bm{r}_{1})=\lambda f(\bm{p}_{1})+(1-\lambda)f(\bm{q}_{1})
and
f(\bm{r}_{2})=\lambda f(\bm{p}_{2})+(1-\lambda)f(\bm{q}_{2})
using parameters
b:=4/m
and
\varepsilon^{\prime}:=\varepsilon/\sqrt{n}
, and return the result.

First, observe that by
Lemma

4.1
, with probability
0.99
, the random map
f
obtained in line (2) satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}\geq\left\lVert f\left(\frac{1}{2}(\bm{r}_{1}+\bm{r}_{2})\right)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\left\lVert\frac{1}{2}\left(f(\bm{r}_{1})+f(\bm{r}_{2})\right)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{4}\left(\left\lVert f(\bm{r}_{1})\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\left\lVert f(\bm{r}_{2})\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)

which implies that
\left\lVert f(\bm{r}_{1})\right\rVert_{2}^{2},\left\lVert f(\bm{r}_{2})\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq 4/m:=b
where
b
is the parameter used in line (3). Similarly,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda m}\geq\left\lVert f\left(\frac{1}{2}(\bm{p}_{1}+\bm{p}_{2})\right)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\left\lVert\frac{1}{2}\left(f(\bm{p}_{1})+f(\bm{p}_{2})\right)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{4}\left(\left\lVert f(\bm{p}_{1})\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\left\lVert f(\bm{p}_{2})\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)

and so
\left\lVert f(\bm{p}_{1})\right\rVert_{2}^{2},\left\lVert f(\bm{p}_{2})\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq 4/\lambda m=b/\lambda
.
Now, if
\bm{p}_{1}=\bm{p}_{2}
, then
f(\bm{p}_{1})=f(\bm{p}_{2})
, and clearly
\left\lVert f(\bm{p}_{1})-f(\bm{p}_{2})\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=0
and so by
Theorem

5.2
, the tester will correctly output "close" with probability
9/10
. Next, if
\left\lVert\bm{p}_{1}-\bm{p}_{2}\right\rVert_{1}\geq\varepsilon
, then
\left\lVert f(\bm{p}_{1})-f(\bm{p}_{2})\right\rVert_{1}\geq\varepsilon
, and so
\left\lVert f(\bm{p}_{1})-f(\bm{p}_{2})\right\rVert_{2}\geq\varepsilon/\sqrt{n}=\varepsilon^{\prime}
, thus satisfying the required bound for the use of
Theorem

5.2
. Therefore, the tester correctly outputs "far" with probability
9/10
in this case. All in all, by union bounding the failure probability of flattening and the
\ell_{2}
-closeness tester, the overall failure probability in both cases is at most
1/100+1/10<1/3
. This completes the proof of correctness.

We now bound the number of queries. By
Theorem

5.2
, and our definition of
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\varepsilon/\sqrt{n}
and
b=4/m
, the query complexity is bounded by

O\left(\frac{b^{2}}{(\varepsilon^{\prime})^{4}\lambda^{3}}\right)=O\left(\frac{(1/m)^{2}}{(\varepsilon/\sqrt{n})^{4}\lambda^{3}}\right)=O\left(\left\lceil\frac{n^{2}}{m^{2}}\right\rceil\cdot\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{3}}\right)

as claimed. We now bound the number of samples. By
Theorem

5.2
and our definitions
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\varepsilon/\sqrt{n}
and
b=O(1/m)
, the total number of samples used is bounded by

\displaystyle O\left(\frac{1}{b}+\frac{\sqrt{b}}{(\varepsilon^{\prime})^{2}\lambda^{3/2}}+\frac{b^{2}}{(\varepsilon^{\prime})^{4}\lambda^{3}}\right)=O\left(m+\frac{n}{\sqrt{m}\varepsilon^{2}\lambda^{3/2}}+\frac{n^{2}}{m^{2}\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{3}}\right)\text{.}

(5)

Case 1.

If
n=\Omega(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{3}})
, then
n^{1/3}=\Omega(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4/3}\lambda})
, which implies that the bound
m=\Omega\left(\frac{n^{2/3}}{\varepsilon^{4/3}\lambda}\right)
holds by the bounds on
m
in line (1) of the tester. Observe that in this case the first term in
eq.

5
dominates. Thus, the sample complexity in this case is
O\left(m+\frac{n^{2/3}}{\varepsilon^{4/3}\lambda}\right)
.

Case 2.

If
n=O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{3}})
, then
m=n
by the bounds on
m
in line (1) of the tester. In this case, the second and third terms are bounded by
O\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{2}\lambda^{3/2}}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{3}}\right)=O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{3}})
. ∎

5.1
\ell_{2}
Closeness Testing

In this section we establish our upper bound for closeness testing under
\ell_{2}
distance in the verification query model, restated here for convenience.

See
5.2

Proof.

We show that by using verification queries we are able to essentially simulate a standard
\ell_{2}
closeness tester due to
[
DGPP19
]
on the distributions
p_{1},p_{2}
. This tester uses
O(\sqrt{b}/\varepsilon^{2})
samples and reports "close" or "far" based on the number of
\bm{p}_{1}
-
\bm{p}_{1}
,
\bm{p}_{2}
-
\bm{p}_{2}
, and
\bm{p}_{1}
-
\bm{p}_{2}
collisions it sees. For our purposes, it suffices to (a) take enough samples from the mixtures
\bm{r}_{1},\bm{r}_{2}
so that enough
\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2}
samples are present to simulate this tester, then (b) use verification queries to estimate the number of relevant collisions (those not involving
\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2}
). To perform (a), we need at least
\approx m/\lambda
samples from
\bm{r}_{1},\bm{r}_{2}
, where
m
is the number of samples needed for the standard closeness tester.

Notation.

For a mixture
\bm{r}=\lambda\bm{p}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}
and set of samples
S_{\bm{r}}\sim\bm{r}
, we use
S_{\bm{p}},S_{\bm{q}}
to denote the partition of
S_{\bm{r}}
into those samples generated by
\bm{p},\bm{q}
respectively. For a multi-set
S\subseteq[n]
of samples, we use
C(S)=\{(x,y)\in\binom{S}{2}\colon x=y\}
to denote the set of pairwise collisions in
S
and let
c(S):=|C(S)|
. For two sets of samples
S_{1},S_{2}
, we use
C(S_{1},S_{2})=\{(x,y)\in S_{1}\times S_{2}\colon x=y\}
to denote the set of pairwise cross-collisions between
S_{1},S_{2}
and let
c(S_{1},S_{2}):=|C(S_{1},S_{2})|
.

We start by stating the testing result of
7
7

7

We give a slightly modified version which follows by their same arguments. The reason is that we require a constant factor gap in the thresholds for the ”close” and ”far” cases since we will only be able to attain an approximation of the relevant statistics.

[
DGPP19
]
. For completeness we give a proof in
Section

5.1.3
.

Theorem 5.3

(Tolerant
\ell_{2}
Closeness Tester,
[
DGPP19
]
)
.

Let
\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2}\colon[n]\to[0,1]
be arbitrary distributions with
\left\lVert\bm{p}_{1}\right\rVert_{2}^{2},\left\lVert\bm{p}_{2}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq b
for
b\in(0,1]
. Let
S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}}
be two sets of
m\geq 250000\cdot\frac{\sqrt{b}}{\varepsilon^{2}}
samples from
\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2}
for sufficiently large constant
c>0
, and let
Z=c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}})+c(S_{\bm{p}_{2}})-\frac{m-1}{m}\cdot c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})
. The following hold.

1.

If
\left\lVert\bm{p}_{1}-\bm{p}_{2}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\varepsilon/4
, then
\Pr\left[Z<{m\choose 2}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{8}\right]\geq 0.99
.

2.

If
\left\lVert\bm{p}_{1}-\bm{p}_{2}\right\rVert_{2}\geq\varepsilon
, then
\Pr\left[Z>{m\choose 2}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\right]\geq 0.99
.

Before describing the tester, we provide two claims which show how verification queries are used to estimate the number of relevant collisions. We defer their proofs to
Section

5.1.2
. The first claim allows us to estimate the number of
\bm{p}_{1}
-
\bm{p}_{1}
and
\bm{p}_{2}
-
\bm{p}_{2}
collisions.

Claim 5.4
.

Let
S_{\bm{r}}
be a collection of
m/\lambda
samples drawn from a mixture
\bm{r}=\lambda\bm{p}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}
where
\left\lVert\bm{r}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq b
and
\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert\leq b/\lambda
. Then, using
O(\frac{b^{2}}{\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{3}})
verification queries
Q\subseteq S
we can compute an estimate
\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}})
such that

\Pr_{S_{\bm{r}},Q}\left[|\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}})-c(S_{\bm{p}})|>{m\choose 2}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{100}\right]<0.01\text{.}

The second claim allows us to estimate the number of
\bm{p}_{1}
-
\bm{p}_{2}
collisions.

Claim 5.5
.

Let
S_{\bm{r}_{1}},S_{\bm{r}_{2}}
be two collections of
m/\lambda
samples drawn from mixtures
\bm{r}_{1}=\lambda\bm{p}_{1}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}_{1}
and
\bm{r}_{2}=\lambda\bm{p}_{2}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}_{2}
where
\left\lVert\bm{r}_{1}\right\rVert_{2}^{2},\left\lVert\bm{r}_{2}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq b
and
\left\lVert\bm{p}_{1}\right\rVert_{2}^{2},\left\lVert\bm{p}_{2}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq b/\lambda
. Then, using
O(\frac{b^{2}}{\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{3}})
verification queries
Q\subseteq S_{\bm{r}_{1}}\cup S_{\bm{r}_{2}}
we can compute an estimate
\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})
such that

\Pr_{S_{\bm{r}_{1}},S_{\bm{r}_{2}},Q}\left[|\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})-c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})|>{m\choose 2}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{100}\right]<0.01\text{.}

We now describe our tester for
Theorem

5.2
.

1.

Invoke
3.15
to obtain an estimate
\hat{\lambda}
satisfying
\lambda/40000<\hat{\lambda}\leq\lambda
with probability
0.99
using
O(1/\lambda)
samples and queries. Let
m=C\left\lceil\frac{\sqrt{b/\hat{\lambda}}}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{b^{2}}{\varepsilon^{4}\hat{\lambda}^{2}}\right\rceil
where
C>1
is a sufficiently large constant and note that
C\left\lceil\frac{\sqrt{b}}{\varepsilon^{2}\sqrt{\lambda}}+\frac{b^{2}}{\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{2}}\right\rceil<m\leq O(\frac{\sqrt{b}}{\varepsilon^{2}\sqrt{\lambda}}+\frac{b^{2}}{\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{2}})
.

2.

Let
S_{\bm{r}_{1}},S_{\bm{r}_{2}}
be two sets of
m/\lambda
samples from
\bm{r}_{1},\bm{r}_{2}
respectively. Let
S_{\bm{p}_{1}}\subset S_{\bm{r}_{1}}
,
S_{\bm{p}_{2}}\subset S_{\bm{r}_{2}}
be the samples which were drawn from
\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2}
, respectively.

3.

Using
5.4
obtain estimates
\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{1}}),\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{2}})
for
c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}}),c(S_{\bm{p}_{2}})
.

4.

Using
5.5
obtain an estimate
\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})
for
c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})
.

5.

Let
\hat{Z}=\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{1}})+\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{2}})-\frac{m-1}{m}\cdot\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})
.

6.

If
\hat{Z}<{m\choose 2}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{4}
, then return "close". Otherwise, return "far".

We need to show that the estimator
\hat{Z}
used in line (4) suffices in place of the estimator
Z
from
Theorem

5.3
. First, since
\left\lVert\bm{p}_{1}\right\rVert_{2}^{2},\left\lVert\bm{p}_{2}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq b/\lambda
, it suffices to have
250000(\sqrt{b/\lambda})/\varepsilon^{2}=250000\sqrt{b}/(\sqrt{\lambda}\varepsilon^{2})
samples from
\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2}
are to simulate
Theorem

5.3
. This is the reason for the bound
m\gg\frac{\sqrt{b}}{\varepsilon^{2}\sqrt{\lambda}}
in line (1). The second bound
m\gg b^{2}/\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{2}
will be needed later in the proof for a different reason.

Let
m_{\bm{p}_{1}}=|S_{\bm{p}_{1}}|,m_{\bm{p}_{2}}=|S_{\bm{p}_{2}}|
be the random variables denoting the number of samples drawn from
\bm{r}_{1},\bm{r}_{2}
in line (2) which were generated by
\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2}
. Note that
\mathbb{E}[\bm{m}_{\bm{p}_{1}}]=\mathbb{E}[\bm{m}_{\bm{p}_{2}}]=m
and so by a Chernoff bound

\displaystyle\Pr\left[|m_{\bm{p}_{1}}-m|>\sqrt{18m}\right]\leq 2\exp\left(-\big(\sqrt{18/m}\big)^{2}m/3\right)<\frac{1}{200}

(6)

Note that since
m>C
for sufficiently large constant
C
, we have
\sqrt{18m}<0.01m
. The same bounds hold for
\bm{m}_{p_{2}}
. By a union bound we get the following.

Fact 5.6
.

Let
{\cal C}
denote the event that

0.99m<m-\sqrt{18m}\leq m_{\bm{p}_{1}},m_{\bm{p}_{2}}\leq m+\sqrt{18m}<1.01m\text{.}

Then,
\Pr[{\cal C}]\geq 0.99
.

We will now condition on the bounds from
5.6
holding for
m_{\bm{p}_{1}},m_{\bm{p}_{2}}
. In particular,
m_{\bm{p}_{1}},m_{\bm{p}_{2}}
are large enough to simulate
Theorem

5.3
on
\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2}
since
\left\lVert\bm{p}_{1}\right\rVert_{2}^{2},\left\lVert\bm{p}_{2}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq b/\lambda
. Then, write
S_{\bm{p}_{1}}=S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\prime}\sqcup S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\mathrm{extra}}
and
S_{\bm{p}_{2}}=S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\prime}\sqcup S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\mathrm{extra}}
where
S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\prime}
is the first
m_{\mathrm{min}}=\min(m_{\bm{p}_{1}},m_{\bm{p}_{2}})
samples in
S_{\bm{r}_{1}}
generated by
\bm{p}_{1}
and
S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\mathrm{extra}}
is the remaining
\max(0,m_{\bm{p}_{1}}-m_{\bm{p}_{2}})
, and
S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\prime},S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\mathrm{extra}}
are defined similarly. Then, let

\displaystyle Z=c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\prime})+c(S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\prime})-\frac{m_{\mathrm{min}}-1}{m_{\mathrm{min}}}\cdot c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\prime},S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\prime})

(7)

and recall that our tester uses the estimator,

\displaystyle\hat{Z}=\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{1}})+\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{2}})-\frac{m-1}{m}\cdot\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})\text{.}

(8)

Moreover,
Theorem

5.3
asserts the following:

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{p}_{1}-\bm{p}_{2}\right\rVert\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{4}~\implies~\Pr\left[Z<{m_{\mathrm{min}}\choose 2}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{8}\right]\geq 0.99

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{p}_{1}-\bm{p}_{2}\right\rVert\geq\varepsilon~\implies~\Pr\left[Z>{m_{\mathrm{min}}\choose 2}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\right]\geq 0.99

(9)

Therefore, it remains to show that the estimator
\hat{Z}
does not differ too much from
Z
and the value
{m\choose 2}
we use to threshold in line (6) does not differ too much from
{m_{\mathrm{min}}\choose 2}
. First, conditioning on the event
{\cal C}
from
5.6
, we have
m_{\mathrm{min}}=m-a
where
|a|\leq 0.01m
and so

\displaystyle{\cal C}

\displaystyle~\implies~{m\choose 2}={m_{\mathrm{min}}\choose 2}+\frac{m_{\mathrm{min}}a+(m_{\mathrm{min}}-1)a+a^{2}}{2}

\displaystyle~\implies~\left|{m_{\mathrm{min}}\choose 2}-{m\choose 2}\right|\leq 0.02m_{\mathrm{min}}\ll{m_{\mathrm{min}}\choose 2}\frac{1}{100}

(10)

The following lemma bounds the error between
\hat{Z}
and
Z
. The first source of error is due to the estimations obtained from
5.4
and
5.5
. The second source of error, which is more painful to deal with, is the fact that we do not know how many samples were generated by
\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2}
, and the number of samples are not equal. Nevertheless, they are the same in expectation, and are well-concentrated around their mean. We defer the proof of
Lemma

5.7
to
Section

5.1.1
. (The proof of this lemma is where we use the bound
m\gg b^{2}/\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{2}
.)

Lemma 5.7
.

Consider
\hat{Z}
computed in line (4) and
Z
defined in
eq.

7
. Then,
\Pr[|\hat{Z}-Z|>{m\choose 2}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{20}]<0.06
.

Now, recalling line (6) of the tester and the guarantee from
section

5.1
, the tester is correct with probability
0.99
as long as
|\hat{Z}-Z|\leq{m\choose 2}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{16}
and
\left|{m_{\mathrm{min}}\choose 2}-{m\choose 2}\right|\leq{m_{\mathrm{min}}\choose 2}\frac{1}{16}
. Therefore, by
3.15
,
5.6
,
section

5.1
,
Lemma

5.7
, and a union bound, the tester succeeds with probability
91/100>9/10
, as claimed. ∎

5.1.1
Bounding the Error Due to Unequal
p_{1},p_{2}
Samples: Proof of
Lemma

5.7

Proof.

First, by definition of
Z,\hat{Z}
and the triangle inequality, we have

\displaystyle|\hat{Z}-Z|\leq\left|\big(\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{1}})+\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{2}})-\frac{m-1}{m}\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})\big)-\big(c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\prime})+c(S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\prime})-\frac{m_{\mathrm{min}}-1}{m_{\mathrm{min}}}c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\prime},S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\prime})\big)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\Big(|\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{1}})-c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\prime})|+|\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{2}})-c(S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\prime})|+\left|\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})-c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\prime},S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\prime})\right|\Big)+\left|\frac{\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})}{m}-\frac{c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\prime},S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\prime})}{m_{\mathrm{min}}}\right|\text{.}

(11)

Bounding the first term of
section

5.1.1
.

By the triangle inequality

\displaystyle\Big(|\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{1}})-c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\prime})|+|\hat{c}(S_{p_{2}})-c(S_{p_{2}}^{\prime})|+\left|\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})-c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\prime},S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\prime})\right|\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\Big(|\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{1}})-c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}})|+|\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{2}})-c(S_{\bm{p}_{2}})|+\left|\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})-c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})\right|\Big)

\displaystyle+\Big(|c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}})-c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\prime})|+|c(S_{\bm{p}_{2}})-c(S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\prime})|+|c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})-c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\prime},S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\prime})|\Big)\text{.}

(12)

Now, let
\mathcal{E}_{1}
denote the following good event:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1}\colon~\max\big(|\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{1}})-c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}})|,|\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{2}})-c(S_{\bm{p}_{2}})|,|\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})-c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})|\big)\leq{m\choose 2}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{100}\text{.}

(13)

By
5.4
,
5.5
, and a union bound,
\Pr[\mathcal{E}_{1}]\geq 0.97
. Conditioned on
\mathcal{E}_{1}
, the first term of
section

5.1.1
is bounded by at most
3{m\choose 2}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{100}
. We now bound the second term of
section

5.1.1
. Note that the following hold:

•

c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\prime})=c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}})-(c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\mathrm{extra}})+c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\mathrm{extra}},S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\prime}))
,

•

c(S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\prime})=c(S_{\bm{p}_{2}})-(c(S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\mathrm{extra}})+c(S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\mathrm{extra}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\prime}))
, and

•

c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\prime},S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\prime})=c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})-\left(c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\mathrm{extra}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})+c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\mathrm{extra}})\right)
.

In particular, the second term of
section

5.1.1
is upper bounded by

\displaystyle|c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}})-c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\prime})|+|c(S_{\bm{p}_{2}})-c(S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\prime})|+|c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})-c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\prime},S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\mathrm{extra}})+c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\mathrm{extra}},S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\prime})+c(S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\mathrm{extra}})+c(S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\mathrm{extra}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\prime})+c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\mathrm{extra}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})+c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\mathrm{extra}})

(14)

which is precisely the number of collisions incident on
S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\mathrm{extra}}\cup S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\mathrm{extra}}
. Let
c^{\mathrm{extra}}
denote this quantity, which we now bound. By
5.6
, we have
|S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\mathrm{extra}}\cup S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\mathrm{extra}}|\leq 2\sqrt{18m}
and
|S_{\bm{p}_{1}}\cup S_{\bm{p}_{2}}|\leq 2.02m
. Moreover, for any distinct samples
x\in S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\mathrm{extra}}\cup S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\mathrm{extra}}
and
y\in S_{\bm{p}_{1}}\cup S_{\bm{p}_{2}}
, the probability that
x,y
collide is either
\langle\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2}\rangle
,
\left\lVert\bm{p}_{1}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
, or
\left\lVert\bm{p}_{2}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
, all of which are at most
b/\lambda
by our assumed bounds on
\left\lVert\bm{p}_{1}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
,
\left\lVert\bm{p}_{2}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[c^{\mathrm{extra}}]

\displaystyle\leq 2\sqrt{18m}\cdot 2.02m\cdot\frac{b}{\lambda}<\frac{18m^{3/2}b}{\lambda}<{m\choose 2}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{20000}

(15)

where the last inequality holds since
m>\frac{Cb^{2}}{\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{2}}
for sufficiently large constant
C
, i.e.
\sqrt{m}\varepsilon^{2}\gg b/\lambda
. Let
\mathcal{E}_{2}
denote the following good event:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{2}\colon c^{\mathrm{extra}}\leq{m\choose 2}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{200}~\text{ which satisfies }~\Pr\left[\mathcal{E}_{2}\right]\geq 0.99

(16)

by Markov’s inequality. Conditioning on the events
{\cal C},\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2}
, the first term of
section

5.1.1
is bounded by

\displaystyle{\cal C}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{1}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{2}~\implies~

\displaystyle|\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{1}})-c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}})|+|\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{2}})-c(S_{\bm{p}_{2}})|+\left|\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})-c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})\right|

\displaystyle\leq{m\choose 2}\frac{3\varepsilon^{2}}{100}+{m\choose 2}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{200}\leq{m\choose 2}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{28}\text{.}

(17)

Bounding the second term of
section

5.1.1
.

First, conditioning on the bounds from
5.6
and applying the triangle inequality, we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})}{m}-\frac{c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\prime},S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\prime})}{m_{\mathrm{min}}}\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m_{\mathrm{min}}}\left|\frac{m_{\mathrm{min}}}{m}\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})-c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\prime},S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\prime})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{m}\cdot\left|\frac{m_{\mathrm{min}}}{m}\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})-c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\prime},S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\prime})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{m}\cdot\left(\left|\frac{m_{\mathrm{min}}}{m}\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})-\frac{m_{\mathrm{min}}}{m}c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})\right|+\left|\frac{m_{\mathrm{min}}}{m}c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})-c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\prime},S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\prime})\right|\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{m}\left|\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})-c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})\right|+\frac{2}{m}\left|\frac{m_{\mathrm{min}}}{m}c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})-c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\prime},S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\prime})\right|

(18)

and we already have a bound on the first term of
section

5.1.1
by conditioning on
\mathcal{E}_{1}
(recall
eq.

13
). For the second term of
section

5.1.1
, recall that
c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\prime},S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\prime})=c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})-(c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\mathrm{extra}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})+c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\mathrm{extra}}))
and conditioning on the event
{\cal C}
(from
5.6
), we have
|m_{\mathrm{min}}-m|\leq\sqrt{18m}
, and so
|(m_{\mathrm{min}}/m)-1|\leq\sqrt{18/m})
. Thus,

\displaystyle{\cal C}~\implies~

\displaystyle\left|\frac{m_{\mathrm{min}}}{m}c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})-c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\prime},S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\prime})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\big(1+\sqrt{18/m}\big)\cdot c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})-c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})+(c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\mathrm{extra}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})+c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\mathrm{extra}}))\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{18}{m}}\cdot c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})+c^{\mathrm{extra}}\text{.}

(19)

where
c^{\mathrm{extra}}
is the random variable denoting the number of collisions incident on
S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\mathrm{extra}}\cup S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\mathrm{extra}}
. Now,
|S_{\bm{p}_{1}}|,|S_{\bm{p}_{2}}|\leq 1.01m
by the bound in
5.6
, and each
x\in S_{\bm{p}_{1}}
,
y\in S_{\bm{p}_{2}}
form a collision with probability at most
\langle\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2}\rangle\leq b/\lambda
. Thus,
\mathbb{E}[c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})]\leq(1.01m)^{2}\cdot(b/\lambda)
. Using this bound,
section

5.1.1
, and
eq.

15
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{2}{m}\cdot\left|\frac{m_{\mathrm{min}}}{m}c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})-c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\prime},S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\prime})\right|~\Bigg|~{\cal C}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{1}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{2}\right]\leq\frac{m^{1/2}b}{\lambda}+\frac{m\varepsilon^{2}}{10000}\leq\frac{m\varepsilon^{2}}{5000}

(20)

where the second inequality used the fact that
\sqrt{m}\gg b/(\varepsilon^{2}\lambda)
. Now, returning to
section

5.1.1
, applying
eq.

13
and
eq.

20
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})}{m}-\frac{c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\prime},S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\prime})}{m_{\mathrm{min}}}\right|~\Bigg|~{\cal C}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{1}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{m\varepsilon^{2}}{10}\leq{m\choose 2}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{100000}

(21)

and so by Markov’s

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\left|\frac{\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})}{m}-\frac{c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\prime},S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\prime})}{m_{\mathrm{min}}}\right|\leq{m\choose 2}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{1000}~\Bigg|~{\cal C}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{1}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{2}\right]\geq 0.99\text{.}

(22)

Bounding
|\hat{Z}-Z|
.

Finally, let
{\cal C}
denote the good event that the bounds from
5.6
hold. Returning to
section

5.1.1
, by a union bound and conditioning on the events
{\cal C},\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2}
, and using
eq.

22
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[|\hat{Z}-Z|>{m\choose 2}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{20}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr[\neg{\cal C}\vee\neg\mathcal{E}_{1}\vee\neg\mathcal{E}_{2}]+\Pr\left[\left|\frac{\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})}{m}-\frac{c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}}^{\prime},S_{\bm{p}_{2}}^{\prime})}{m_{\mathrm{min}}}\right|>{m\choose 2}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{1000}~\Bigg|~{\cal C}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{1}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 0.06

and this completes the proof of
Lemma

5.7
. ∎

5.1.2
Estimating Collision Counts with Verification Queries

Proof of
5.4
.
First, since
\left\lVert\bm{r}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq b
and
\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq b/\lambda
we have

\mathbb{E}_{S_{\bm{r}}}[c(S_{\bm{r}})]=\sum_{(x,y)\in{S_{\bm{r}}\choose 2}}\Pr[x=y]\leq{m/\lambda\choose 2}\cdot b\leq O(m^{2}b/\lambda^{2})

\mathbb{E}_{S_{\bm{r}}}[c(S_{\bm{p}})]\leq\sum_{(x,y)\in{S_{\bm{r}}\choose 2}}\Pr[x,y\sim\bm{p}]\cdot\Pr[x=y~|~x,y\sim\bm{p}]\leq{m/\lambda\choose 2}\cdot\lambda^{2}\cdot\frac{b}{\lambda}\leq O(m^{2}b/\lambda)

and so by Markov’s, we have
c(S_{\bm{r}})\leq O(bm^{2}/\lambda^{2})
and
c(S_{\bm{p}})\leq O(bm^{2}/\lambda)
with probability
0.999
. Thus, we now condition on these bounds. Note that
c(S_{\bm{r}})
is known to the algorithm simply by counting the number of collisions in
S_{\bm{r}}
, and we have access to a
\mathrm{Bern}(c(S_{\bm{p}})/c(S_{\bm{r}}))
random variable using two verification queries to
S_{\bm{r}}
as follows: simply pick a uniform random collision
(x,y)\in C(S_{\bm{r}})
and query these two samples. Observe that this reveals a
\bm{p}
-
\bm{p}
collision with probability exactly
c(S_{\bm{p}})/c(S_{\bm{r}})
.

Now, we can use a standard bias estimation procedure (
Lemma

3.13
), to estimate
c(S_{\bm{p}})/c(S_{\bm{r}})
. In particular, if we can obtain
\hat{\rho}
such that

\displaystyle\left|\hat{\rho}-\frac{c(S_{\bm{p}})}{c(S_{\bm{r}})}\right|\leq\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{100c(S_{\bm{r}})}{m\choose 2}~\Longrightarrow~\left|c(S_{\bm{r}})\hat{\rho}-c(S_{\bm{p}})\right|\leq\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{100}{m\choose 2}

(23)

then we can use the estimator
\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}}):=c(S_{\bm{r}})\hat{\rho}
and this satisfies the claim. Thus, we invoke
Lemma

3.13
to obtain
\hat{\rho}
satisfying the inequality in the LHS of
eq.

23
, using the upper bound
c(S_{\bm{p}})/c(S_{\bm{r}})\leq O(bm^{2}/\lambda c(S_{\bm{r}}))
(by our condition on
c(S_{\bm{p}})
and the fact that the algorithm knows
c(S_{\bm{r}})
explicitly) and additive error parameter
\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{100c(S_{\bm{r}})}{m\choose 2}
. Plugging these bounds into
Lemma

3.13
, the number of queries is bounded by

O\left(\frac{bm^{2}}{\lambda c(S_{\bm{r}})}\cdot\left(\frac{c(S_{\bm{r}})}{\varepsilon^{2}{m\choose 2}}\right)^{2}\right)\leq O\left(\frac{b\cdot c(S_{\bm{r}})}{\varepsilon^{4}m^{2}\lambda}\right)\leq O\left(\frac{b^{2}}{\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{3}}\right)

where the final inequality used our bound
c(S_{\bm{r}})\leq O(bm^{2}/\lambda^{2})
. This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of
5.5
.
First, by Cauchy-Schwarz and our bounds
\left\lVert\bm{r}_{1}\right\rVert_{2}^{2},\left\lVert\bm{r}_{2}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq b
and
\left\lVert\bm{p}_{1}\right\rVert_{2}^{2},\left\lVert\bm{p}_{2}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq b/\lambda
, we have
\langle\bm{r}_{1},\bm{r}_{2}\rangle\leq b
and
\langle\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2}\rangle\leq b/\lambda
. Therefore,

\mathbb{E}_{S_{\bm{r}_{1}},S_{\bm{r}_{2}}}[c(S_{\bm{r}_{1}},S_{\bm{r}_{2}})]=\sum_{x\in S_{\bm{r}_{1}},y\in S_{\bm{r}_{2}}}\Pr[x=y]=\frac{m^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}\langle\bm{r}_{1},\bm{r}_{2}\rangle\leq O(bm^{2}/\lambda^{2})

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{S_{\bm{r}_{1}},S_{\bm{r}_{2}}}[c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})]

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in S_{\bm{r}_{1}},y\in S_{\bm{r}_{2}}}\Pr[x\sim\bm{p}_{1}\text{, }y\sim\bm{p}_{2}]\Pr[x=y~|~x\sim\bm{p}_{1}\text{, }y\sim\bm{p}_{2}]

\displaystyle=\frac{m^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}\cdot\lambda^{2}\cdot\langle\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2}\rangle\leq O(bm^{2}/\lambda)

and so by Markov’s we have
c(S_{\bm{r}_{1}},S_{\bm{r}_{2}})\leq O(bm^{2}/\lambda^{2})
and
c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})\leq O(bm^{2}/\lambda)
with probability
0.999
. Thus, we now condition on these events. Note that
c(S_{\bm{r}_{1}},S_{\bm{r}_{2}})
is known to the algorithm explicitly without using verification queries, and we have access to a
\mathrm{Bern}(c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})/c(S_{\bm{r}_{1}},S_{\bm{r}_{2}}))
random variable using two verification queries as follows: simply pick a uniform random collision
(x,y)\in C(S_{\bm{r}_{1}},S_{\bm{r}_{2}})
and query these two samples. Observe that this reveals a
\bm{p}_{1}
-
\bm{p}_{2}
collision with probability exactly
c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})/c(S_{\bm{r}_{1}},S_{\bm{r}_{2}})
.

Now, we can use a standard bias estimation procedure (
Lemma

3.13
), to estimate
c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})/c(S_{\bm{r}_{1}},S_{\bm{r}_{2}})
. In particular, if we can obtain
\hat{\rho}
such that

\displaystyle\left|\hat{\rho}-\frac{c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})}{c(S_{\bm{r}_{1}},S_{\bm{r}_{2}})}\right|\leq\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{100c(S_{\bm{r}_{1}},S_{\bm{r}_{2}})}{m\choose 2}~\Longrightarrow~\left|c(S_{\bm{r}_{1}},S_{\bm{r}_{2}})\hat{\rho}-c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})\right|\leq\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{100}{m\choose 2}

(24)

then we can use the estimator
\hat{c}(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}}):=c(S_{\bm{r}_{1}},S_{\bm{r}_{2}})\hat{\rho}
and this satisfies the claim. Thus, we invoke
Lemma

3.13
to obtain
\hat{\rho}
satisfying the inequality in the LHS of
eq.

24
, using the upper bound

\frac{c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})}{c(S_{\bm{r}_{1}},S_{\bm{r}_{2}})}\leq O\left(\frac{bm^{2}}{\lambda c(S_{\bm{r}_{1}},S_{\bm{r}_{2}})}\right)

(by our condition on
c(S_{\bm{p}_{1}},S_{\bm{p}_{2}})
and the fact that the algorithm knows
c(S_{\bm{r}_{1}},S_{\bm{r}_{2}})
explicitly) and additive error parameter
\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{100c(S_{\bm{r}_{1}},S_{\bm{r}_{2}})}{m\choose 2}
. Plugging these bounds into
Lemma

3.13
, the number of queries is bounded by

O\left(\frac{bm^{2}}{\lambda c(S_{\bm{r}_{1}},S_{\bm{r}_{2}})}\cdot\left(\frac{c(S_{\bm{r}_{1}},S_{\bm{r}_{2}})}{\varepsilon^{2}{m\choose 2}}\right)^{2}\right)\leq O\left(\frac{b\cdot c(S_{\bm{r}_{1}},S_{\bm{r}_{2}})}{\varepsilon^{4}m^{2}\lambda}\right)\leq O\left(\frac{b^{2}}{\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{3}}\right)

where the final inequality used our bound
c(S_{\bm{r}_{1}},S_{\bm{r}_{2}})\leq O(bm^{2}/\lambda^{2})
. This completes the proof. ∎

5.1.3
Deferred Calculations for Standard
\ell_{2}
Closeness Testing

Proof of
Theorem

5.3
.
Let
\alpha=\left\lVert\bm{p}_{1}-\bm{p}_{2}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
and let
t={m\choose 2}\varepsilon^{2}/2
. Lemmas 3.2 and 3.3 of
[
DGPP19
]
establish that
8
8

8

The variance bound comes from Lemma 3.3 along with the fact that
\left\lVert\bm{p}-\bm{q}\right\rVert_{4}^{2}\leq\left\lVert\bm{p}-\bm{q}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\alpha
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Z]={m\choose 2}\alpha~\text{ and }~\mathrm{Var}[Z]\leq 116m^{2}b+8m^{3}\alpha\sqrt{b}\text{.}

(25)

We first prove item (1). Suppose that
\alpha\leq\varepsilon^{2}/16
. Then, using
eq.

25
and Chebyshev’s inequality yields

\displaystyle\Pr\left[Z\geq{m\choose 2}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{8}\right]\leq\Pr\left[\left|Z-\mathbb{E}[Z]\right|\geq{m\choose 2}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{16}\right]\leq\frac{\mathrm{Var}[Z]}{({m\choose 2}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{16})^{2}}\leq\frac{267264\cdot b}{m^{2}\varepsilon^{4}}+\frac{1152\sqrt{b}}{m\varepsilon^{2}}<1/100

when
m\geq 250000\sqrt{b}/\varepsilon^{2}
, as claimed. We now prove item (2). Suppose that
\alpha\geq\varepsilon^{2}
. Again, using using
eq.

25
and Chebyshev’s inequality yields

\displaystyle\Pr\left[Z\leq{m\choose 2}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\left[\left|Z-\mathbb{E}[Z]\right|\geq{m\choose 2}\frac{\max(\alpha,\varepsilon^{2})}{2}\right]\leq\frac{4\mathrm{Var}[Z]}{({m\choose 2}\max(\alpha,\varepsilon^{2}))^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4176\cdot b}{m^{2}\cdot\max(\alpha^{2},\varepsilon^{4})}+\frac{288\sqrt{b}}{m\cdot\max(\alpha,\frac{\varepsilon^{4}}{\alpha})}\leq\frac{4176\cdot b}{m^{2}\varepsilon^{4}}+\frac{288\sqrt{b}}{m\varepsilon^{2}}<1/100

again when
m\geq 250000\sqrt{b}/\varepsilon^{2}
, as claimed. ∎

6
Sample Query Tradeoff Lower Bounds

In this section, we present our lower bounds.
In particular, we obtain tight (up to constant factors) sample-query trade-offs for bias estimation (
Theorem

1.1
), uniformity testing (
Theorem

1.3
), and closeness testing (
Theorem

1.5
).

6.1
Bias Estimation

We begin with a simple bound stating that any bias estimation algorithm essentially needs to query all samples.
A bias estimation algorithm is
\varepsilon
-accurate if given sample access to mixture
\lambda\bm{p}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}
where
\bm{p},\bm{q}
are Bernoulli random variables, can determine the bias of
\bm{p}
up to error
\varepsilon
with probability at least
2/3
.

See
1.1

Proof.

We will show that any algorithm that can determine if
\bm{p}
has bias
\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon
or
\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon
requires
\varepsilon^{-2}\lambda^{-1}
queries (and therefore
\varepsilon^{-2}\lambda^{-1}
samples).
We will reduce from bias estimation (in the standard setting with clean samples).
Consider the following task:
Given sample access to two coins
\bm{c}_{1},\bm{c}_{2}
, one with bias
\lambda+2\lambda\varepsilon
and one with bias
\lambda-2\lambda\varepsilon
, determine which coin is which.
It is well known via a standard bias estimation lower bound that this task requires
\Omega\left(\lambda^{-1}\varepsilon^{-2}\right)
samples (see
Lemma

A.1
).
Alternatively, it suffices to determine the bias of
\bm{c}_{1}
.

Consider the following instance.
Fix
\alpha=\frac{\lambda}{1-\lambda}
.
Note that
1-\alpha=\frac{1-2\lambda}{1-\lambda}
.
There are two cases:

1.

\bm{p}
is a coin with bias
\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon
and
\bm{q}
is a coin with bias
\alpha(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon)+(1-\alpha)\frac{1}{2}
.

2.

\bm{p}
is a coin with bias
\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon
and
\bm{q}
is a coin with bias
\alpha(\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon)+(1-\alpha)\frac{1}{2}
.

In either case, the mixture
\bm{r}=\lambda\bm{p}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}
is a fair coin with bias
\frac{1}{2}
, since
(1-\lambda)\alpha=\lambda
.

Suppose there is an algorithm
\mathcal{A}
using
m
samples and
q
queries that determines if
\bm{p}
has bias
\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon
or
\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon
.
We design an algorithm for bias estimation of
\bm{c}_{1}
using
q
samples.
Our algorithm begins by providing
m
samples of a fair coin to
\mathcal{A}
, denoted
(y_{1},\dotsc,y_{m})
.
Let
(Z_{1},\dotsc,Z_{m})
denote whether the
i
-th sample is from
\bm{p}
or
\bm{q}
.
Let
(b_{1},\dotsc,b_{q})
denote the
q
samples from
\bm{c}_{1},\bm{c}_{2}
.
Suppose the algorithm
\mathcal{A}
queries indices
(i_{1},\dotsc,i_{q})
.
For each query
j\in[q]
, if
y_{i_{j}}=H
, respond to the query with
\bm{p}
if the
j
-th flip of
\bm{c}_{1}
is heads and
\bm{q}
if tails.
If
y_{i_{j}}=T
respond to the query with
\bm{p}
if the
j
-th flip of
\bm{c}_{2}
is heads and
\bm{q}
if tails.
Finally, if
\mathcal{A}
outputs
\bm{p}
has bias
\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon
, return that
\bm{c}_{1}
has bias
\lambda+2\lambda\varepsilon
.
Otherwise, if
\mathcal{A}
outputs
\bm{p}
has bias
\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon
, return that
\bm{c}_{1}
has bias
\lambda-2\lambda\varepsilon
.

We argue that this algorithm correctly determines the bias of
\bm{c}
.
Suppose
\bm{p}
has bias
\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\Pr(Z_{i}=\bm{p}|y_{i}=H)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Pr(Z_{i}=\bm{p},y_{i}=H)}{\Pr(y_{i}=H)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda(\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon)}{\lambda(\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon)+\lambda(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon)+(1-2\lambda)\frac{1}{2}}=\lambda+2\lambda\varepsilon\text{.}

Similarly, we have

\Pr(Z_{i}=\bm{p}|y_{i}=T)=\lambda-2\lambda\varepsilon\text{.}

Therefore, this successfully simulates the case where
\bm{c}_{1}
has bias
\lambda+2\lambda\varepsilon
while
\bm{c}_{2}
has bias
\lambda-2\lambda\varepsilon
.
In particular, since
\mathcal{A}
returns
\bm{p}
has bias
\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon
, we return that
\bm{c}_{1}
has bias
\lambda+2\lambda\varepsilon
.

On the other hand, suppose
\bm{p}
has bias
\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon
.
Then, using similar arguments as above, we have

\displaystyle\Pr(Z_{i}=\bm{p}|y_{i}=H)

\displaystyle=\lambda-2\lambda\varepsilon

\displaystyle\Pr(Z_{i}=\bm{p}|y_{i}=T)

\displaystyle=\lambda+2\lambda\varepsilon\text{.}

and similarly
\Pr(Z_{i}=p|y_{i}=T)=\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon
.
In particular, this simulates the case where
\bm{c}_{1}
has bias
\lambda-2\lambda\varepsilon
while
\bm{c}_{2}
has bias
\lambda+2\lambda\varepsilon
.
Since
\mathcal{A}
returns
\bm{p}
has bias
\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon
, we return that
\bm{c}_{1}
has bias
\lambda-2\lambda\varepsilon
.

Finally, it is clear to see that our algorithm for determining the bias of
\bm{c}_{1}
has sample complexity
q
.
∎

6.2
Distribution Testing Lower Bound Preliminaries

We present some preliminaries that will be useful for both uniformity and closeness testing.

Definition 6.1

(Poisson Sampling)
.

Given a non-negative measure
\bm{p}
over
[n]
and an integer
m
, the Poisson sampling model samples a number
m^{\prime}\sim\mathrm{Poi}\left(m\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{1}\right)
, and draws
m^{\prime}
samples from
\bm{p}/\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{1}
.
Define
T\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
to be the random vector where
T_{i}
counts the number of element
i
seen.
We write
\mathrm{PoiS}(m,\bm{p})
to denote the distribution of the random vector
T
.
We say
\mathcal{A}
is a Poissonized tester with sample complexity
m
if it takes as input a sample count vector
T\sim\mathrm{PoiS}(m,\bm{p})
.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
{\cal H}_{0},{\cal H}_{1}
be a meta-distribution over a finite universe
{\cal X}
satisfying
\left\lVert\bm{r}\right\rVert_{1}\in(0.5,2)
.
Let
\delta\in(0,1/3)
and
m
be a positive integer satisfying
m\geq\log(10/\delta)
.
Consider the following two statements:

1.

For any deterministic Poissonized tester
\mathcal{A}

with sample complexity
m
,
if
\mathcal{A}
is
\delta
-correct with respect to
{\cal H}_{0}
and
{\cal H}_{1}
, then
\mathcal{A}
requires
q
queries.

2.

For any randomized tester
\mathcal{A}

with sample complexity
m^{\prime}:=m/10
,
if
\mathcal{A}
is
\delta/10
-correct with respect to
{\cal H}_{0}
and
{\cal H}_{1}
, then
\mathcal{A}
requires
q
queries.

The first statement implies the second statement.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a randomized tester that consumes
m^{\prime}
samples.
Consider the negation of the second statement.
In particular, assume that
\mathcal{A}
is
0.1\delta
-correct with respect to
{\cal H}_{0}
and
{\cal H}_{1}
.
We show that this will contradict the first statement.

By Markov’s inequality, with probability at least
2/3
over the choice of the random string
r
, we have that the induced deterministic tester
\mathcal{A}(;r)
is
0.3\delta
-correct with respect to
{\cal H}_{0}
and
{\cal H}_{1}
.

We will now convert the tester into a Poissonized one.
In particular, consider the Poissonized tester
\bar{\mathcal{A}}
obtained as follows.
We first take
k\sim\mathrm{Poi}(m)
samples from the underlying distribution
\bm{r}/\left\lVert\bm{r}\right\rVert_{1}
.
If
k\geq m^{\prime}
, we take the first
m^{\prime}
samples, and feed it to
\mathcal{A}(;r)
.
If
k<m^{\prime}
, we simply return reject.
Since we assume
\left\lVert\bm{r}\right\rVert_{1}\in(0.5,2)
and
m\geq\log(10/\delta)
,
it then follows from standard Poisson concentration (e.g.
Lemma

3.1
) that

k\geq m^{\prime}
with probability at least
1-\delta
.
In particular, this implies that
\bar{\mathcal{A}}
is a deterministic Poissonized tester with sample complexity
m
that is
0.4\delta
-correct with respect to
{\cal H}_{0}
and
{\cal H}_{1}
.
This therefore contradicts the first statement of the lemma.
∎

6.3
Uniformity Testing

In this section, we give our lower bound for uniformity testing.
We begin with a simple argument that resolves the sample-query dependence and shows any
m
-sample algorithm needs
\Omega\left(n/m\right)
samples.
Then, we develop a framework that resolves the sample-query complexity of uniformity testing.

6.3.1
Warm Up: Resolving Sample-Query Dependence

Theorem 6.3
.

Any
\frac{1}{2}
-distributionally robust
0.1
-uniformity tester with
m\leq n^{0.99}
samples requires
\Omega\left(\frac{n}{m}\right)
queries.

We note that the above lower bound can be adjusted to hold for any number of samples
m\leq n^{1-c}
for some
c>0
at the cost of constant factors in the query complexity.

Our result relies on the following bounds on the number of collisions.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
m\geq c\sqrt{n}
for some
c>0
.
Let
X
be the number of collisions given
m
i.i.d. samples drawn from
\bm{p}
on
[n]
.
Then for large enough
n
,

\Pr\left(X\in(1\pm 0.0001)\binom{m}{2}\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)>0.9999\text{.}

For certain applications, we will require the following concentration bound for higher moment collisions.

Lemma 6.5
.

Fix
c\geq 2
an integer.
Let
m\geq 2n^{1-1/c}
.
Let
X
be the number of
c
-collisions given
m
i.i.d. samples drawn from
\bm{p}
on
[n]
.
Then for large enough
n
,

\Pr\left(X\in(1\pm 0.0001)\binom{m}{c}\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{c}^{c}\right)>0.9999\text{.}

We defer the proofs of these facts to
Appendix

A
.

Proof.

By standard uniformity testing lower bounds, we may assume
m=\Omega(n^{1/2})
, even if we query all samples.
We will also assume without loss of generality that the algorithm takes
\mathrm{Poi}(m)
samples, since
\mathrm{Poi}(m)
is
O(m)
with high constant probability.
Under this assumption, the frequency of each bucket is distributed according to independent Poisson processes.

Let
c^{*}
be the smallest integer such that
m\geq 2n^{1-1/c^{*}}
.
Then, note that
2n^{1-1/c^{*}}\leq m<2n^{1-1/(c^{*}+1)}
.
Note that by our assumption on
m
, we have
c^{*}\leq 100
.

We construct the hard instance as follows.
Let
m\ll d\ll n
where
d
is a sufficiently large constant multiple of
m
.

Definition 6.6
.

Let
{\cal M}_{0}
be the distribution over tuples of non-negative measures
(\bm{p},\bm{q})
generated as follows: for all
i\in[n]
,

(\bm{p}[i],\bm{q}[i])=\begin{cases}\left(\frac{1}{n},\frac{1}{2n}+\frac{1}{2d}\right)&w.p.\quad\frac{d}{n}\\
\left(\frac{1}{n},\frac{1}{2n}\right)&w.p.\quad\frac{n-d}{2n}\\
\left(\frac{1}{n},\frac{1}{2n}\right)&w.p.\quad\frac{n-d}{2n}\end{cases}

Similarly, let
{\cal M}_{1}
be the distribution over tuples of non-negative measures
(\bm{p},\bm{q})
generated as follows: for all
i\in[n]
,

(\bm{p}[i],\bm{q}[i])=\begin{cases}\left(\frac{1}{n},\frac{1}{2n}+\frac{1}{2d}\right)&w.p.\quad\frac{d}{n}\\
\left(\frac{4}{3n},\frac{1}{6n}\right)&w.p.\quad\frac{n-d}{2n}\\
\left(\frac{2}{3n},\frac{5}{6n}\right)&w.p.\quad\frac{n-d}{2n}\end{cases}

The meta-distribution
{\cal H}_{U}
is the distribution over random tuples of non-negative measures
(\bm{p},\bm{q})
generated by choosing
X\sim\mathrm{Bern}(1/2)
and
(\bm{p},\bm{q})\sim{\cal M}_{X}
.

Let
\bm{r}:=(\bm{p}+\bm{q})/2
denote the mixture.
Note that we have (regardless of
X\in\left\{0,1\right\}
),

\bm{r}[i]=\begin{cases}\frac{3}{4n}+\frac{1}{4d}&w.p.\quad\frac{d}{n}\\
\frac{3}{4n}&w.p.\quad\frac{n-d}{n}\end{cases}\text{.}

(26)

Let
H,L_{+},L_{-}
be a partition of
[n]
that denotes the set of indices of each type.
Let
W_{i}
be the random variable denoting the type of index
i
.

Poissonization and Measures

Note that
\bm{p},\bm{q},\bm{r}
may not be distributions on
[n]
as their total mass may be more or less than
1
.
We will deal with this in a standard way.
In particular, defining the distribution
\bm{r}/\left\lVert\bm{r}\right\rVert_{1}
and taking
\mathrm{Poi}(m\left\lVert\bm{r}\right\rVert_{1})
samples from
\bm{r}/\left\lVert\bm{r}\right\rVert_{1}
is equivalent to observing
Y_{i}\sim\mathrm{Poi}(m\bm{r}[i])
samples from bucket
i
independently for all
i
.
By a Chernoff bound, we can conclude that with high constant probability we have
|H|=d\pm O(\sqrt{d})
and
|L_{+}|,|L_{-}|=\frac{n-d}{2}\pm O(\sqrt{n})
so that there is a small
\delta<n^{-\Omega(1)}
such that
1-\delta\leq\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{1},\left\lVert\bm{q}\right\rVert_{1}\leq 1+\delta
and so
1-2\delta\leq\left\lVert\bm{r}\right\rVert_{1}\leq 1+2\delta
.
Furthermore, note that since
\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{1},\left\lVert\bm{q}\right\rVert_{1},\left\lVert\bm{r}\right\rVert_{1}\in(1-2\delta,1+2\delta)
, the mass of each element does not change significantly (and in particular neither will higher moment norms of these distributions).
In particular, for any distribution
\bm{p}
,
\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{c}^{c}
changes by a multiplicative factor of
\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{1}^{c}\leq(1+\delta)^{c}
after normalization.
In our instance, we are concerned with
\delta<n^{-\Omega(1)}
and
c=O(1)
so that
(1+\delta)^{c}
for constant
c
is at most an arbitrarily small constant greater than
1
.
For the remainder of this proof, we will mostly ignore the normalization, commenting on its effect where necessary.

In either the yes-instance or the no-instance we have
\bm{r}[i]=\frac{3}{4n}
for
i\not\in H
and
\bm{r}[i]=\frac{3}{4n}+\frac{1}{4d}
for
i\in H
.
In either case the algorithm draws samples from the same distribution
\bm{r}
.
Thus, without any queries we cannot hope to distinguish
X
with probability greater than
\frac{1}{2}
.

To prove a lower bound on the number of queries necessary, we first show that querying singletons yields no information.

Lemma 6.7
.

Consider a bucket
i
with frequency
Y_{i}=1
.
Let
Z
denote the whether the query on bucket
i
reveals
\bm{p}
.
Then
\Pr(Z=\bm{p}|X=0,Y_{i}=1)=\Pr(Z=\bm{p}|X=1,Y_{i}=1)
.

Proof.

Note that
Y_{i}\sim\mathrm{Poi}(m\bm{r}[i])
is distributed identically to
Y_{i}=A_{i}+B_{i}
where
A_{i}\sim\mathrm{Poi}(m\bm{p}[i]/2),B_{i}\sim\mathrm{Poi}(m\bm{q}[i]/2)
are sampled independently.
Then,

\displaystyle\Pr(A_{i}=0,B_{i}=1|X=0)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{n}\exp\left(-\frac{m}{2n}-\frac{m}{4n}-\frac{m}{4d}\right)\left(\frac{m}{4n}+\frac{m}{4d}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{n-d}{n}\exp\left(-\frac{m}{2n}-\frac{m}{4n}\right)\left(\frac{m}{4n}\right)

\displaystyle\Pr(A_{i}=0,B_{i}=1|X=1)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{n}\exp\left(-\frac{m}{2n}-\frac{m}{4n}-\frac{m}{4d}\right)\left(\frac{m}{4n}+\frac{m}{4d}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{n-d}{2n}\exp\left(-\frac{4m}{6n}-\frac{m}{12n}\right)\left(\frac{m}{12n}\right)+\frac{n-d}{2n}\exp\left(-\frac{2m}{6n}-\frac{5m}{12n}\right)\left(\frac{5m}{12n}\right)

so that
\Pr(A_{i}=0,B_{i}=1|X=0)=\Pr(A_{i}=0,B_{i}=1|X=1)
.
Note that
\Pr(Y_{i}=1)=\Pr(Y_{i}=1|X=0)=\Pr(Y_{i}=1|X=1)
.
By the total probability rule, we observe that
\Pr(A_{i}=1,B_{i}=0|X=0)=\Pr(A_{i}=1,B_{i}=0|X=1)
.
Thus, to conclude, we have

\displaystyle\Pr(Z=\bm{p}|X=0,Y_{i}=1)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Pr(A_{i}=1,B_{i}=0|X=0)}{\Pr(Y_{i}=1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Pr(A_{i}=1,B_{i}=0|X=1)}{\Pr(Y_{i}=1)}

\displaystyle=\Pr(Z=\bm{p}|X=1,Y_{i}=1)\text{.}

∎

Lemma

6.7
states that regardless of
B
, a singleton query is equally likely to yield
\bm{p}
, and so yields no information.
Any query that helps the algorithm distinguish
B
must therefore be on a bucket with multiple samples.

Next, we argue that by querying collisions, most queries will look the same to the algorithm, and therefore without many queries, the algorithm still cannot hope to distinguish
X
.
To do so, we will argue that
\bm{r}
generates significantly more collisions than
\bm{p}
.
In fact, for any frequency count
c\geq 2
,
\bm{r}
will generate significantly more elements with frequency
c
than
\bm{p}
will generate collisions at all.
Thus, since the algorithm cannot distinguish any buckets with the same frequency prior to querying, the algorithm must make many queries to detect any sample of
\bm{p}
among the collisions.

First, we argue that
\bm{p}
does not produce too many collisions.
In both the yes and no-instance we note that
\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert^{2}\leq\frac{(1+\varepsilon)^{2}}{n}
and
Lemma

6.4
ensures that with probability at least
0.9999
, the number of
\bm{p}
collisions is at most
1.0001\frac{m^{2}}{2}\frac{(1+\varepsilon)^{2}}{n}\leq\frac{1.0001m^{2}}{n}
by assuming
\varepsilon<0.1
.
Note that to account for normalization by
\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{1}
, we can instead bound the collisions by say
\frac{1.001m^{2}}{n}
.

Next, we argue that for every
2\leq c\leq c^{*}
,
\bm{r}
has significantly more
c
-collisions than
\bm{p}
has collisions.
Fix such a
2\leq c\leq c^{*}\leq 100
.
We begin with a lower bound on the number of
c
-collisions.

Lemma 6.8
.

For any integer
2\leq c\leq 100
,
\left\lVert\bm{r}\right\rVert_{c}^{c}=\Omega(d^{1-c})
.

Proof.

Since
\varepsilon<0.1
and
|H|=\Omega(d)
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\bm{r}[i]^{c}

\displaystyle\geq|T|\frac{1}{(4d)^{c}}=\Omega(d^{1-c})\text{.}

∎

Now, since
m\geq 2n^{1-1/c^{*}}\geq 2n^{1-1/c}
,
Lemma

6.5
states that the number of
c
-collisions from
\bm{r}
is between

(1\pm 0.0001)\binom{m}{c}\left\lVert r\right\rVert_{c}^{c}=\Theta\left(\frac{m^{c}d^{1-c}}{c!}\right)

with high constant probability.
Applying the union bound over all
2\leq c\leq c^{*}
, the above bound holds for all
c
.
Furthermore, since
d\gg m
for a sufficiently large constant, we have that the number of
(c+1)
-collisions is at most a small fraction of the number of
c
-collisions, and therefore the number of buckets with frequency exactly
c
is also
\Omega\left(m^{c}d^{1-c}\right)=\Omega\left(m\right)
since
d=\Theta(m)
.

Finally, we argue that
\bm{p}
has few samples in the buckets where
\bm{r}
has collisions.
For a sample of
\bm{p}
to appear in a bucket where
\bm{r}
has a collision,
\bm{p}
must collide either with itself or with
\bm{q}
in this bucket.
We have already bounded the number of collisions
\bm{p}
causes with itself above.
It remains, therefore, to upper bound the number of collisions
\bm{p}
has with
\bm{q}
.
The probability of a collision between
\bm{p}
and
\bm{q}
in samples
(i,j)
can be written as

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in[n]}2\Pr(Z_{i}=\bm{p},Y_{i}=k)\Pr(Z_{j}=\bm{q},Y_{j}=k)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\in H}2\frac{1}{n}\left(\frac{1}{2n}+\frac{1}{2d}\right)+\sum_{k\not\in H}2\frac{10}{18n^{2}}

\displaystyle=O(1/n)\text{.}

Above, we have bounded the first term by observing
|T|=O(d)
.
In particular, using Markov’s inequality, we have that with high constant probability, there are at most
O(m^{2}/n)
collisions between
\bm{p}
and
\bm{q}
.
Combining our arguments so far, we have shown that there are at most
O(m^{2}/n)
samples from
\bm{p}
in buckets with frequency at most
c^{*}
.
However, for all
2\leq c\leq c^{*}
,
\bm{r}
produces at least
\Omega(m)
buckets with frequency
c
.
In particular, any query of a bucket with frequency
2\leq c\leq c^{*}
finds a sample of
\bm{p}
with probability at most
O\left(\frac{m^{2}/n}{m}\right)=O(m/n)
.
The following lemma summarizes the above discussion.

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
2\leq c\leq c^{*}
.
Any query on a bucket with frequency
c
yields
\bm{p}
with probability at most
O(m/n)
.

To conclude the argument, we show that any bucket with frequency
(c^{*}+1)
or greater is unlikely to have samples from
\bm{p}
.
Towards this, we argue that no bucket outside of
H
produces any
(c^{*}+1)
-collisions.

Lemma 6.10
.

For any integer
c\geq 2
,
\left\lVert\bm{r}[[n]\setminus H]\right\rVert_{c}^{c}<\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)^{c}\frac{1}{n^{c-1}}
.

Proof.

The proof is a simple computation.

\displaystyle\sum_{i\not\in H}\bm{r}[i]^{c}=(n-|H|)\left(\frac{3}{4n}\right)^{c}\leq\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)^{c}\frac{1}{n^{c-1}}\text{.}

∎

In particular, by
Corollary

3.3
, we have that a
(c^{*}+1)
-collision occurs on
[n]\setminus H
with probability at most

\displaystyle\frac{(3/4)^{c^{*}+1}}{n^{c^{*}}}\binom{m}{c^{*}+1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(3/4)^{c^{*}+1}}{n^{c^{*}}}\frac{m^{c^{*}+1}}{(c^{*}+1)!(m-c^{*}-1)!}

\displaystyle<\frac{(3/2)^{c^{*}+1}}{(c^{*}+1)!(m-c^{*}-1)!}

\displaystyle<0.0001

for sufficiently large
n
since
m=\Omega\left(\sqrt{n}\right)
.
In the second inequality, we have used that
m<2n^{1-1/(c^{*}+1)}
and therefore
m^{c^{*}+1}<2^{c^{*}+1}n^{(c^{*}+1)-(c^{*}+1)/(c^{*}+1)}=2^{c^{*}+1}n^{c^{*}}
.
Note that here we have explicitly used
m\leq n^{0.99}
so that
c^{*}\leq 100
.
Assuming
m<n^{1-\delta}
for any
\delta>0
will require the following arguments to proceed assuming that
\bm{p}
does not produce any
\frac{1}{\delta}
-collisions.
Thus, any bucket with frequency at least
(c^{*}+1)
must be in
H
.

Consider then a bucket
i\in H
with empirical frequency
c>c^{*}
.
Each query is distributed as a Bernoulli random variable that equals
\bm{p}
with parameter at most
\frac{\bm{p}[i]}{2\bm{r}[i]}=O(d/n)=O(m/n)
.
Thus, in buckets with frequency at least
(c^{*}+1)
, we can again conclude that any query reveals
p
with probability at most
O(m/n)
.
This yields the following lemma.

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
c>c^{*}
.
Any query on a bucket with frequency
c
yields
\bm{p}
with probability at most
O(m/n)
.

Combining our two bounds, any query on a bucket with frequency at least
c\geq 2
is
\bm{p}
with probability at most
O(m/n)
.
In particular, without at least
\Omega(n/m)
queries, all queries by the algorithm reveal
\bm{q}
and therefore the algorithm cannot distinguish
X
with any non-trivial advantage.
∎

6.3.2
Lower Bound for Uniformity Testing

We prove our main lower bound for uniformity testing.
In addition to obtaining tight dependence on
\varepsilon,\lambda
and relaxing the assumption on
m
(in fact, we only require the assumption
m\ll n
) the techniques used here will prove crucial for obtaining an optimal sample-query trade-off for closeness testing.

Consider an algorithm that successfully distinguishes uniform
\bm{p}
from
\bm{p}
that are
\varepsilon
-far from uniform, i.e. the algorithm guesses the hidden random bit
X
in the hard instance with non-trivial advantage.
It is a standard fact (see e.g.
[
DK16
]
) that any such algorithm must use information that is correlated with
X
.
In particular, if we denote the input of the algorithm (a collection of samples and queries) as
T
, then
I(X:T)=\Omega(1)
as formalized below.

See
3.8

Proof.

We give a proof of this standard fact for completeness.
The conditional entropy
H(X|f(T))
is the expectation over
f(T)
of
h(q)
where
h(q)
is the binary entropy function and
q
is the probability that
X=f(T)
given
f(T)
.
Since
\mathbb{E}[q]\geq 0.51
and
h
is concave,
H(X|f(A))\leq h(0.51)<1-2\cdot 10^{-4}
.
Then, by the data processing inequality

I(X:A)\geq I(X:f(A))\geq H(X)-H(X|f(A))\geq 2\cdot 10^{-4}.

∎

Our lower bound will then follow by bounding the information obtained by an algorithm that makes few queries.

See
1.3

Proof.

By standard uniformity testing lower bounds (see e.g.
[
Pan08
]
), we may assume
m=\Omega(n^{1/2})
, even if we query all samples.
Consider the following hard instance.
Define
\alpha:=\frac{\lambda}{1-\lambda}
.
Since
\lambda\leq\frac{1}{2}
, we have
\alpha\leq 1
.

Definition 6.12
.

Let
{\cal M}_{0}
be the distribution over tuples of non-negative measures
(\bm{p},\bm{q})
generated as follows: for all
i\in[n]
,

(\bm{p}[i],\bm{q}[i])=\begin{cases}\left(\frac{1}{n},\alpha\frac{4\varepsilon}{n}+\frac{1-4\varepsilon\alpha}{m}\right)&w.p.\quad\frac{m}{n}\\
\left(\frac{1}{n},\alpha\frac{4\varepsilon}{n}\right)&w.p.\quad\frac{n-m}{2n}\\
\left(\frac{1}{n},\alpha\frac{4\varepsilon}{n}\right)&w.p.\quad\frac{n-m}{2n}\end{cases}

Similarly, let
{\cal M}_{1}
be the distribution over tuples of non-negative measures
(\bm{p},\bm{q})
generated as follows: for all
i\in[n]
,

(\bm{p}[i],\bm{q}[i])=\begin{cases}\left(\frac{1}{n},\alpha\frac{4\varepsilon}{n}+\frac{1-4\varepsilon\alpha}{m}\right)&w.p.\quad\frac{m}{n}\\
\left(\frac{1+2\varepsilon}{n},\alpha\frac{2\varepsilon}{n}\right)&w.p.\quad\frac{n-m}{2n}\\
\left(\frac{1-2\varepsilon}{n},\alpha\frac{6\varepsilon}{n}\right)&w.p.\quad\frac{n-m}{2n}\end{cases}

The meta-distribution
{\cal H}_{U}
is the distribution over random tuples of non-negative measures
(\bm{p},\bm{q})
generated by choosing
X\sim\mathrm{Bern}(1/2)
and
(\bm{p},\bm{q})\sim{\cal M}_{X}
.

Let
h,l_{+},l_{-}
denote each type, that is let
W_{i}
denote the type of bucket
i
be independently chosen for all
i
where
\Pr(W_{i}=h)=\frac{m}{n}
and
\Pr(W_{i}=l_{+})=\Pr(W_{i}=l_{-})=\frac{n-m}{2n}
.
Let
H,L_{+},L_{-}
denote the number of buckets of each type.
We begin by arguing the non-negative measures are close to real distributions.

Lemma 6.13
.

For sufficiently large
n\geq 1
,

\Pr_{(\bm{p},\bm{q})\sim{\cal H}_{U}}\left(\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{1},\left\lVert\bm{q}\right\rVert_{1}\in(0.9,1.1)\right)\geq 1-\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}\text{.}

Furthermore,

\Pr_{(\bm{p},\bm{q})\sim{\cal M}_{1}}\left(\mathrm{tvd}(\bm{p},U_{n})>\varepsilon\right)\geq 1-\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}\text{.}

Proof.

Let
X=0
.
Note
\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{1}=1
since
\bm{p}=U_{n}
always.
Let
H
denote the number of items of the first type so that
