\left\lVert\bm{q}\right\rVert_{1}=4\varepsilon\alpha+\frac{|H|}{m}(1-4\varepsilon\alpha)
.
By standard concentration bounds, observe that
|H|\in m\pm 0.001m
and
|L_{-}|,|L_{+}|\in\frac{n-m}{2}\pm 0.001n
with high probability with the assumption
m=\Omega(n^{1/2})
.
Note that these conditions are sufficient to guarantee the desired bound on the
\ell_{1}
norm of
\bm{p},\bm{q}
.

Now, let
X=1
.
By standard concentration bounds, we have
H=m\pm 0.001m
and
L_{+},L_{-}=\frac{n-m}{2}\pm 0.001n
with probability at least
1-1/\mathrm{poly}(n)
.
For sufficiently large
n
, these conditions guarantee that the
\ell_{1}
-norm of the measures is in
(0.9,1.1)
(again using the assumption without loss of generality that
m=\Omega(n^{1/2})
).
Similarly, they are sufficient to guarantee that
\bm{p}\sim{\cal M}_{1}
is sufficiently far from uniform.
∎

Thus, when sampling distributions
(\bm{p},\bm{q})
from
{\cal H}_{U}
, the distributions
\bm{p},\bm{q}
(and therefore the mixture
\bm{r}=\frac{\bm{p}+\bm{q}}{2}
) satisfy the desired norm conditions with high probability.
By
Lemma

6.2
, it suffices to consider a deterministic Poissonized tester
\mathcal{A}
with sample complexity
m
.
We will show that if
\mathcal{A}
is a
0.1
-correct
\varepsilon
-uniformity tester,
\mathcal{A}
requires
\Omega\left(nm^{-1}\varepsilon^{-4}\lambda^{-2}\right)
queries.

In fact, we show a stronger lower bound on the number of distinct buckets that the algorithm
\mathcal{A}
must query.
We assume that when the algorithm queries bucket
i
, it receives the source of all samples on bucket
i
.
Note that this only strengthens the algorithm
\mathcal{A}
.

Lemma 6.14
.

Suppose
\mathcal{A}
is a deterministic Poissonized tester with sample complexity
m
is
0.1
-correct with respect to
{\cal H}_{0},{\cal H}_{1}
.
Then,
\mathcal{A}
queries
\Omega\left(\min(nm^{-1}\varepsilon^{-4}\lambda^{-2},m)\right)
distinct buckets.
This bound holds even when a single query on bucket
i
reveals the source of every sample in bucket
i
.

Proof.

Suppose
nm^{-1}\varepsilon^{-4}\lambda^{-2}=o(m)
.
Otherwise,
\mathcal{A}
can simply query all samples by querying at most
m
buckets.
Since
\mathcal{A}
is a Poissonized tester,
\mathcal{A}
receives independently
Y^{(1)}_{i}\sim\mathrm{Poi}(m\bm{r}[i])
samples on bucket
i
, where
\bm{r}[i]=\frac{\bm{p}[i]+\bm{q}[i]}{2}
.
Note that we can equivalently sample
Y_{i}=A_{i}+B_{i}
where
A_{i}\sim\mathrm{Poi}(\lambda m\bm{p}[i]),B_{i}\sim\mathrm{Poi}((1-\lambda)m\bm{q}[i])
are sampled independently.
Of course, the algorithm (without queries) only observes
Y_{i}
.
If the algorithm queries a bucket
i
, then the algorithm learns
A_{i},B_{i}
as well.
Suppose the algorithm queries a set of buckets
Q\subset[n]
.
Let
T_{i}
denote the information observed by the algorithm from the
i
-th bucket, i.e.
Y_{i}
if
i\not\in Q
and
A_{i},B_{i}
if
i\in Q
.
Let
T=(T_{1},\dotsc,T_{n})
.

Then, the algorithm
\mathcal{A}
is a function such that
\mathcal{A}(T)=X
with at least
51\%
probability, and therefore
I(X:T)\geq 2\cdot 10^{-4}
by
Lemma

3.8
.
Suppose for contradiction that
\mathcal{A}
queries
q=|Q|=o(nm^{-1}\varepsilon^{-4})
buckets.
Since
T_{i}
are independent conditioned on
X
, we have

I(X:T)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}I(X:T_{i})\text{.}

For
i\not\in Q
, we claim
I(X:T_{i})=I(X:Y_{i})=0
.
In particular, for all
i\in[n]
, we have
Y_{i}\sim\mathrm{Poi}(m\bm{r}[i])
where

\bm{r}_{j}[i]=\begin{cases}\lambda\frac{1+4\varepsilon}{n}+(1-\lambda)\frac{1-4\varepsilon\alpha}{m}&w.p.\quad\frac{m}{n}\\
\lambda\frac{1+4\varepsilon}{n}&w.p.\quad\frac{n-m}{n}\end{cases}

(27)

regardless of
X
, proving the claim.
Thus, the samples reveal no information it suffices to bound the information gained from the queries via
\sum_{i\in Q}^{n}I(X:T_{i})=\sum_{i\in Q}^{n}I(X:A_{i},B_{i})
.

Before proceeding with the mutual information bound, we make the simplifying assumption that no buckets produce large collisions.

Claim 6.15
.

\Pr\left(\max_{i}Y_{i}=O(\log n)\right)>1-\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}

Proof.

Note that each
Y_{i}
is a Poisson variable with parameter at most
m\bm{r}[i]\leq 2
from (
27
).
The result follows from
Lemma

3.1
and a union bound.
∎

Let
E
denote the event that the conditions of
Lemma

6.13
and
6.15
are met.

Lemma 6.16
.

For all
i
, we have

\displaystyle I(X:A_{i},B_{i})=O\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{2}m}{n}\right)\text{.}

Proof.

Note
A_{i},B_{i}
are pairs of Poisson random variables that sum up to
Y_{i}=y
.
By
3.9
, we have

I(X:A_{i},B_{i})=O\left(\sum_{a,b}\frac{\left(f_{a,b}(0)-f_{a,b}(1)\right)^{2}}{f_{a,b}(0)+f_{a,b}(1)}\right)=O\left(\sum_{a+b=y}\frac{\left(f_{a,b}(0)-f_{a,b}(1)\right)^{2}}{f_{a,b}(0)+f_{a,b}(1)}\right)\text{.}

where
f_{a,b}(x)=\Pr(A_{i}=a,B_{i}=b|Y_{i}=y,X=x,E)
and we note that unless
a+b=y
,
f_{a,b}(x)=0
.
We now compute
f_{a,b}(x)
explicitly.

\displaystyle f_{a,b}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Pr(A_{i}=a,B_{i}=b,Y_{i}=y|X=x,E)}{\Pr(Y_{i}=y|X=x,E)}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{\Pr(A_{i}=a,B_{i}=b|X=x,E)}{\Pr(Y_{i}=y|X=x,E)}&\textrm{ if }a+b=y\\
0&\textrm{ o/w }\end{cases}

Define
g_{a,b}(x)=\Pr(A_{i}=a,B_{i}=b|X=x,E)
and
h_{y}(x)=\Pr(Y_{i}=y|X=x,E)
.
Since samples are independent of
X
, define
h_{y}:=h_{y}(0)=h_{y}(1)
.
Then, we can rewrite

I(X:A_{i},B_{i})=O\left(\frac{1}{h_{y}}\sum_{a+b=y}\frac{\left(g_{a,b}(0)-g_{a,b}(1)\right)^{2}}{g_{a,b}(0)+g_{a,b}(1)}\right)\text{.}

We require the following technical claims.

Claim 6.17
.

h_{y}=\Omega\left(\frac{m}{n}\frac{(1-\lambda)^{y}(1-4\varepsilon\alpha)^{y}}{y!}\right)\text{.}

Claim 6.18
.

If
y=a+b\leq 1
,
then
g_{a,b}(0)=g_{a,b}(1)
.
Otherwise
y=a+b\geq 2

and

\displaystyle(g_{a,b}(0)-g_{a,b}(1))^{2}

\displaystyle=O\left(\left(\frac{\lambda^{a+b}}{a!b!}\frac{m^{a+b}}{n^{a+b}}\right)^{2}\right)\cdot\begin{cases}a^{4}\varepsilon^{4}&b=0,a\geq 2\\
a^{2}\varepsilon^{4}&b=1,a\geq 1\\
(6\varepsilon)^{2b}(1+a^{2}\varepsilon^{2})^{2}&b\geq 2,a\geq 0\end{cases}\text{.}

Claim 6.19
.

For
x\in\left\{0,1\right\}
,

g_{a,b}(x)=\Omega\left(\frac{1}{a!b!}\right)\begin{cases}\frac{m^{y}\lambda^{y}}{n^{y}}&b=0\\
\frac{\lambda^{a}(1-\lambda)^{b}m^{a+1}(1-4\varepsilon\alpha)^{b}}{n^{a+1}}&b\geq 1\end{cases}\text{.}

We will prove these claims at the end of this section.
Recall that our goal is to bound,

\displaystyle I(X:A_{i},B_{i})

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{1}{h_{y}}\sum_{b=0,a=y-b}^{y}\frac{(g_{a,b}(0)-g_{a,b}(1))^{2}}{g_{a,b}(0)+g_{a,b}(1)}\right)\text{.}

To bound the summation, we will consider some cases separately.
Let
\overline{I}_{a,b}:=\frac{1}{h_{y}}\frac{(g_{a,b}(0)-g_{a,b}(1))^{2}}{g_{a,b}(0)+g_{a,b}(1)}
.
We bound a few special values of
\overline{I}_{a,b}
.
Applying
6.17
,
6.18
, and
6.19
, we obtain

\displaystyle\overline{I}_{y,0}

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{\lambda^{2y}m^{2y}y^{4}\varepsilon^{4}}{(y!0!)^{2}n^{2y}}\cdot\frac{y!0!n^{y}}{m^{y}\lambda^{y}}\cdot\frac{y!n}{m(1-\lambda)^{y}(1-4\varepsilon\alpha)^{y}}\right)=O\left(\frac{\lambda^{y}y^{4}m^{y-1}\varepsilon^{4}}{n^{y-1}(1-4\varepsilon\alpha)^{y}(1-\lambda)^{y}}\right)=O\left(\frac{m\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{2}}{n}\right)

where we obtain the first inequality by canceling out like terms, then we obtain the final bound by observing that the quantity is decreasing in
y
since
m\ll n
and
y\geq 2
.
We require
\frac{\lambda m}{n(1-\lambda)(1-4\varepsilon\alpha)}<1
for which it suffices to choose
m\leq n/2
.
Similarly,

\displaystyle\overline{I}_{y-1,1}

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{\lambda^{2y}m^{2y}a^{2}\varepsilon^{4}}{((y-1)!1!)^{2}n^{2y}}\cdot\frac{(y-1)!1!n^{y}}{m^{y}\lambda^{y-1}(1-\lambda)(1-4\varepsilon\alpha)}\cdot\frac{y!n}{m(1-\lambda)^{y}(1-4\varepsilon\alpha)^{y}}\right)

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{y!a^{2}m^{y-1}\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{y+1}}{(y-1)!1!n^{y-1}(1-\lambda)^{y+1}(1-4\varepsilon\alpha)^{y+1}}\right)

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{y^{3}m^{y-1}\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{y+1}}{n^{y-1}(1-\lambda)^{y+1}(1-4\varepsilon\alpha)^{y+1}}\right)

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{m\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{3}}{n}\right)\text{.}

Here we observed that
\frac{y!a^{2}}{(y-1)!1!}\leq y^{3}
, and again used that the quantity is decreasing in
y
.
In particular, we require
\frac{\lambda m}{(1-\lambda)(1-4\varepsilon\alpha)n}<\frac{1}{8}
9
9

9

This is to ensure the multiplicative increase from the
y^{3}
term does not overrule the multiplicative decrease from the left hand side.
for which
m<n/16
suffices.
The final bound follows by choosing
y=2
.
Then, for
b\geq 2
, we have

\displaystyle\overline{I}_{a,b}

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{\lambda^{2y}m^{2y}(6\varepsilon)^{2b}(1+a^{2}\varepsilon^{2})}{(a!b!)^{2}n^{2y}}\cdot\frac{a!b!n^{a+1}}{m^{a+1}\lambda^{a}(1-\lambda)^{b}(1-4\varepsilon\alpha)^{b}}\cdot\frac{y!n}{m(1-\lambda)^{y}(1-4\varepsilon\alpha)^{y}}\right)

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{y!\lambda^{2y-a}m^{2y-a-2}(6\varepsilon)^{2b}(1+a^{2}\varepsilon^{2})}{a!b!n^{2y-a-2}(1-\lambda)^{y+b}(1-4\varepsilon\alpha)^{y}}\right)

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{2^{y}\alpha^{2b+a}m^{2y-a-2}(6\varepsilon)^{2b}(1+a^{2}\varepsilon^{2})}{n^{2y-a-2}(1-4\varepsilon\alpha)^{y}}\right)

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{m^{y+b-2}\alpha^{2b+a}}{n^{y+b-2}}\left(\frac{2}{1-4\varepsilon\alpha}\right)^{y}(6\varepsilon)^{2b}(1+a^{2}\varepsilon^{2})\right)

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{m^{y+b-2}\alpha^{y+b}}{n^{y+b-2}}\left(\frac{2}{1-4\varepsilon\alpha}\right)^{y}(6\varepsilon)^{2b}(1+y^{2}\varepsilon^{2})\right)

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{m^{y}\alpha^{y+2}}{n^{y}}\left(\frac{2}{1-4\varepsilon\alpha}\right)^{y}\varepsilon^{4}(1+y^{2}\varepsilon^{2})\right)

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{m^{2}\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{4}}{n^{2}}\right)\text{.}

In the first inequality we group like terms.
in the second we use
\frac{y!}{a!b!}\leq 2^{y}
,
a+b=y
and
\alpha=\frac{\lambda}{1-\lambda}
.
In the third we again group like terms and use
a+b=y
.
In the fourth, we use
a\leq y
.
In the fifth, we note that for fixed
y
, the quantity is decreasing in
b
since
\frac{m}{n}(6\varepsilon)^{2}\alpha<1
.
Thus, we may assume
b=2
.
In the final bound, we observe that the quantity is decreasing in
y
following a similar argument as the previous case, and thus assume
y=b=2
.
We conclude by observing
\alpha=O(\lambda)
.

Since
y=O(\log n)
,
Then, since
\frac{m^{2}\varepsilon^{4}\log n\lambda^{4}}{n^{2}}=o\left(\frac{m\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{2}}{n}\right)
we have that

I(X:A_{i},B_{i})=O\left(\overline{I}_{y,0}+\overline{I}_{y-1,1}+\sum_{b=2}^{y}\overline{I}_{a,b}\right)=O\left(\frac{m\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{2}}{n}\right)\text{.}

∎

Given
Lemma

6.16
, we conclude that

I(X:T)=O\left(q\frac{\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{2}m}{n}\right)=o(1)

which contradicts
I(X:T)\geq 2\cdot 10^{-4}
.
Thus, we must have
q=\Omega\left(\frac{n}{m\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{2}}\right)
.
∎

It remains to prove the omitted claims, which we prove below.
This concludes the proof of
Theorem

1.3
.
∎

6.17

Proof.

From (
27
) we have

\displaystyle h_{y}

\displaystyle=\frac{m}{n}\exp\left(-\frac{m\lambda(1+4\varepsilon)}{n}-(1-\lambda)(1-4\varepsilon\alpha)\right)\frac{\left(\frac{m\lambda(1+4\varepsilon)}{n}+(1-\lambda)(1-4\varepsilon\alpha)\right)^{y}}{y!}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{n-m}{n}\exp\left(-\frac{m\lambda(1+4\varepsilon)}{n}\right)\frac{\left(\frac{m\lambda(1+4\varepsilon)}{n}\right)^{y}}{y!}

\displaystyle=\Omega\left(\frac{m}{n}\frac{(1-\lambda)^{y}(1-4\varepsilon\alpha)^{y}}{y!}\right)\text{.}

Note that we obtain the lower bound by observing that
\exp\left(-\frac{m\lambda(1+4\varepsilon)}{2n}-\frac{(1-\lambda)(1-4\varepsilon)}{2}\right)=\Omega\left(1\right)
from our assumptions
m\leq n
,
\lambda\leq\frac{1}{2}
and
\varepsilon<0.1
.

See
6.18

Proof.

From
Definition

6.12
, we use the definition of the Poisson distribution and write

\displaystyle g_{a,b}(0)

\displaystyle=\frac{m}{n}\exp\left(-\frac{m\lambda}{n}\right)\frac{(\frac{m\lambda}{n})^{a}}{a!}\exp\left(-\frac{4\varepsilon\lambda m}{n}-(1-\lambda)(1-4\varepsilon\alpha)\right)\frac{\left(\frac{4\varepsilon\lambda m}{n}+(1-\lambda)(1-4\varepsilon\alpha)\right)^{b}}{b!}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{n-m}{n}\exp\left(-\frac{m\lambda}{n}\right)\frac{(\frac{m\lambda}{n})^{a}}{a!}\exp\left(-\frac{4\varepsilon\lambda m}{n}\right)\frac{\left(\frac{4\varepsilon\lambda m}{n}\right)^{b}}{b!}

\displaystyle=\frac{m}{n}\exp\left(-\frac{m\lambda(1+4\varepsilon)}{n}-(1-\lambda)(1-4\varepsilon\alpha)\right)\frac{m^{a}\lambda^{a}}{n^{a}}\frac{1}{a!b!}\left(\left(\frac{4\varepsilon\lambda m}{n}+(1-\lambda)(1-4\varepsilon\alpha)\right)^{b}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{n-m}{n}\exp\left(-\frac{m\lambda(1+4\varepsilon)}{n}\right)\frac{m^{a+b}\lambda^{a+b}}{n^{a+b}}\frac{1}{a!b!}\left((4\varepsilon)^{b}\right)\text{.}

Note we have used
\lambda=\alpha(1-\lambda)
above.
Following similar computations,

\displaystyle g_{a,b}(1)

\displaystyle=\frac{m}{n}\exp\left(-\frac{\lambda m}{n}\right)\frac{(\frac{m\lambda}{n})^{a}}{a!}\exp\left(-\frac{4\varepsilon\lambda m}{n}-(1-\lambda)(1-4\varepsilon\alpha)\right)\frac{\left(\frac{4\varepsilon\lambda m}{n}+(1-\lambda)(1-4\varepsilon\alpha)\right)^{b}}{b!}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{n-m}{2n}\exp\left(-\frac{\lambda m(1+2\varepsilon)}{n}\right)\frac{(\frac{\lambda m(1+2\varepsilon)}{n})^{a}}{a!}\exp\left(-\frac{2\varepsilon\lambda m}{n}\right)\frac{\left(\frac{2\varepsilon\lambda m}{n}\right)^{b}}{b!}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{n-m}{2n}\exp\left(-\frac{\lambda m(1-2\varepsilon)}{n}\right)\frac{(\frac{\lambda m(1-2\varepsilon)}{n})^{a}}{a!}\exp\left(-\frac{6\varepsilon\lambda m}{n}\right)\frac{\left(\frac{6\varepsilon\lambda m}{n}\right)^{b}}{b!}

\displaystyle=\frac{m}{n}\exp\left(-\frac{\lambda m(1+4\varepsilon)}{n}-(1-\lambda)(1-4\varepsilon)\right)\frac{m^{a}\lambda^{a}}{n^{a}}\frac{1}{a!b!}\left(\left(\frac{4\varepsilon\lambda m}{n}+(1-\lambda)(1-4\varepsilon\alpha)\right)^{b}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{n-m}{2n}\exp\left(-\frac{\lambda m(1+4\varepsilon)}{2n}\right)\frac{m^{a+b}\lambda^{a+b}}{n^{a+b}}\frac{1}{a!b!}\left((1+2\varepsilon)^{a}(2\varepsilon)^{b}+(1-2\varepsilon)^{a}(6\varepsilon)^{b}\right)\text{.}

If
a+b\leq 1
, note that
g_{a,b}(0)=g_{a,b}(1)
.
Thus, we may assume that
a+b\geq 2
(in particular, in the following assume that
y\geq 2
).
Note that the contribution from the term where
W_{i}=h
is
0
as
A_{i},B_{i}
are identically distributed in this case.

Thus, in order to bound
(g_{a,b}(0)-g_{a,b}(1))^{2}
, we bound the following quantity.

\displaystyle\eta_{a,b}=\eta_{a,b}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle:=\left|2(4\varepsilon)^{b}-\left((1+2\varepsilon)^{a}(2\varepsilon)^{b}+(1-2\varepsilon)^{a}(6\varepsilon)^{b}\right)\right|\text{.}

We begin by showing

\eta_{a,b}=\begin{cases}O\left(a^{2}\varepsilon^{2}\right)&b=0,a\geq 2\\
O\left(a\varepsilon^{2}\right)&b=1,a\geq 1\\
O\left((6\varepsilon)^{b}(1+a^{2}\varepsilon^{2}))\right)&b\geq 2\end{cases}\text{.}

(28)

If
b=0
, we have
a\geq 2
and

\displaystyle\eta_{a,b}=\left|2-((1+2\varepsilon)^{a}+(1-2\varepsilon^{a}))\right|=\left|2-(1+2a\varepsilon+O(a^{2}\varepsilon^{2})+1-2a\varepsilon+O(a^{2}\varepsilon^{2}))\right|=O(a^{2}\varepsilon^{2})\text{.}

If
b=1
and
a\geq 2
then

\displaystyle\eta_{a,b}

\displaystyle=\left|8\varepsilon-((1+2\varepsilon)^{a}(2\varepsilon)+(1-2\varepsilon)^{a}(6\varepsilon))\right|=\left|8\varepsilon-(2\varepsilon+4a\varepsilon^{2}+O(a^{2}\varepsilon^{3})+6\varepsilon-12a\varepsilon^{2}+O(a^{2}\varepsilon^{3}))\right|

\displaystyle=O(a\varepsilon^{2})\text{.}

Note that the same bound holds when
a=1
(in fact we simply omit the
O(a^{2}\varepsilon^{3})
contribution.
Then, assume
b\geq 2
.
By the triangle inequality we have

\displaystyle\eta_{a,b}

\displaystyle\leq 2(2\varepsilon)^{b}+(1+\varepsilon)^{a}\varepsilon^{b}+(1-\varepsilon)^{a}(3\varepsilon)^{b}

\displaystyle\leq 2(2\varepsilon)^{b}+(3\varepsilon)^{b}\left((1+\varepsilon)^{a}+(1-\varepsilon)^{a}\right)\text{.}

If
a<2
, we may bound the above by
O((3\varepsilon)^{b})
.
On the other hand, if
a\geq 2
, we observe that

\displaystyle\eta_{a,b}

\displaystyle\leq 2(4\varepsilon)^{b}+(6\varepsilon)^{b}\left(1+2a\varepsilon+O(a^{2}\varepsilon^{2})+1-2a\varepsilon+O(a^{2}\varepsilon^{2})\right)

\displaystyle=O\left((6\varepsilon)^{b}(1+a^{2}\varepsilon^{2})\right)\text{.}

Given our bounds on
\eta_{a,b}
, we can conclude

\displaystyle(g_{a,b}(0)-g_{a,b}(1))^{2}

\displaystyle=O\left(\left(\frac{1}{a!b!}\frac{m^{a+b}\lambda^{a+b}}{n^{a+b}}\eta_{a,b}\right)^{2}\right)\text{.}

Applying our bounds on
\eta_{a,b}
from (
28
) we have

\displaystyle(g_{a,b}(0)-g_{a,b}(1))^{2}

\displaystyle=O\left(\left(\frac{\lambda^{a+b}}{a!b!}\frac{m^{a+b}}{n^{a+b}}\right)^{2}\right)\cdot\begin{cases}a^{4}\varepsilon^{4}&b=0,a\geq 2\\
a^{2}\varepsilon^{4}&b=1,a\geq 1\\
(6\varepsilon)^{2b}(1+a^{2}\varepsilon^{2})^{2}&b\geq 2,a\geq 0\end{cases}\text{.}

∎

See
6.19

Proof.

Following
6.18
, we begin with

\displaystyle g_{a,b}(0)

\displaystyle=\Omega\left(\frac{1}{a!b!}\left(\frac{m^{a+1}\lambda^{a}(1-\lambda)^{b}(1-4\varepsilon\alpha)^{b}}{n^{a+1}}+\frac{m^{y}\lambda^{y}(4\varepsilon)^{b}}{n^{y}}\right)\right)\text{.}

Next, we bound

\displaystyle g_{a,b}(1)

\displaystyle=\Omega\left(\frac{1}{a!b!}\left(\frac{m^{a+1}\lambda^{a}(1-\lambda)^{b}(1-4\varepsilon\alpha)^{b}}{n^{a+1}}+\frac{m^{y}\lambda^{y}(2\varepsilon)^{b}}{n^{y}}\right)\right)\text{.}

When
b=0
, we obtain the bound

\displaystyle g_{a,b}(x)

\displaystyle=\Omega\left(\frac{1}{a!b!}\frac{m^{y}\lambda^{y}}{n^{y}}\right)

and when
b\geq 1
, we obtain the bound

\displaystyle g_{a,b}(x)

\displaystyle=\Omega\left(\frac{\lambda^{a}(1-\lambda)^{b}}{a!b!}\left(\frac{m^{a+1}(1-4\varepsilon\alpha)^{b}}{n^{a+1}}\right)\right)\text{.}

∎

6.4
Closeness Testing

In this section, we give our lower bound for closeness testing.

See
1.5

Proof.

By standard closeness testing lower bounds (see e.g.
[
Val08
]
), we may assume
m=\Omega(n^{2/3}\lambda^{-1})
, even if we query all samples.
This follows as any algorithm must observe at least
\Omega\left(n^{2/3}\right)
samples from
\bm{p}
which requires
\Omega\left(n^{2/3}\lambda^{-1}\right)
samples from the mixture
\bm{r}
.

Hard Instance.

Consider the following hard instance.
As before, define
\alpha:=\frac{\lambda}{1-\lambda}\leq 1
.

Definition 6.20
.

Let
{\cal M}_{0}
be the distribution over tuples of non-negative measures
(\bm{p}_{1},\bm{q}_{1},\bm{p}_{2},\bm{q}_{2})
generated as follows: for all
i\in[n]
,

(\bm{p}_{1}[i],\bm{q}_{1}[i],\bm{p}_{2}[i],\bm{q}_{2}[i])=\begin{cases}\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2\lambda m},0,\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2\lambda m},0\right)&w.p.\quad\frac{\lambda m}{n}\\
\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2(1-\lambda)m},\frac{1-\varepsilon\alpha}{(1-\lambda)m},\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2(1-\lambda)m},\frac{1-\varepsilon\alpha}{(1-\lambda)m}\right)&w.p.\quad\frac{(1-\lambda)m}{n}\\
\left(\frac{3\varepsilon}{2(n-m)},\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{2(n-m)},\frac{3\varepsilon}{2(n-m)},\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{2(n-m)}\right)&w.p.\quad\frac{n-m}{2n}\\
\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2(n-m)},\frac{3\varepsilon\alpha}{2(n-m)},\frac{\varepsilon}{2(n-m)},\frac{3\varepsilon\alpha}{2(n-m)}\right)&w.p.\quad\frac{n-m}{2n}\end{cases}

Similarly, let
{\cal M}_{1}
be the distribution over tuples of non-negative measures
(\bm{p}_{1},\bm{q}_{1},\bm{p}_{2},\bm{q}_{2})
generated as follows: for all
i\in[n]
,

(\bm{p}_{1}[i],\bm{q}_{1}[i],\bm{p}_{2}[i],\bm{q}_{2}[i])=\begin{cases}\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2\lambda m},0,\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2\lambda m},0\right)&w.p.\quad\frac{\lambda m}{n}\\
\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2(1-\lambda)m},\frac{1-\varepsilon\alpha}{(1-\lambda)m},\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2(1-\lambda)m},\frac{1-\varepsilon\alpha}{(1-\lambda)m}\right)&w.p.\quad\frac{(1-\lambda)m}{n}\\
\left(\frac{3\varepsilon}{2(n-m)},\frac{\varepsilon\alpha}{2(n-m)},\frac{\varepsilon}{2(n-m)},\frac{3\varepsilon\alpha}{2(n-m)}\right)&w.p.\quad\frac{n-m}{2n}\\
\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2(n-m)},\frac{3\varepsilon\alpha}{2(n-m)},\frac{3\varepsilon}{2(n-m)},\frac{\varepsilon\alpha}{2(n-m)}\right)&w.p.\quad\frac{n-m}{2n}\end{cases}

The meta-distribution
{\cal H}_{C}
is the distribution over random tuples of non-negative measures
(\bm{p}_{1},\bm{q}_{1},\bm{p}_{2},\bm{q}_{2})
generated by choosing
X\sim\mathrm{Bern}(1/2)
and
(\bm{p}_{1},\bm{q}_{1},\bm{p}_{2},\bm{q}_{2})\sim{\cal M}_{X}
.

Let
h_{\lambda},h,l_{1},l_{2}
denote each type, that is let
W_{i}
denoting the type of bucket
i
be independently chosen for all
i
where
\Pr(W_{i}=h_{\lambda})=\frac{\lambda m}{n},\Pr(W_{i}=h)=\frac{(1-\lambda)m}{n}
and
\Pr(W_{i}=l_{1})=\Pr(W_{i}=l_{2})=\frac{n-m}{2n}
.
We begin by arguing that all measures are close to real distributions.
Note that by our assumption
m>\frac{1}{\lambda}
,
\frac{1}{\lambda m}<1
so all masses are at most
1
.

Lemma 6.21
.

Let
X\in\left\{0,1\right\}
.
For all
n\geq 1
,

\Pr_{(\bm{p}_{1},\bm{q}_{1},\bm{p}_{2},\bm{q}_{2})\sim{\cal M}_{X}}\left(\left\lVert\bm{p}_{1}\right\rVert_{1},\left\lVert\bm{q}_{1}\right\rVert_{1},\left\lVert\bm{p}_{2}\right\rVert_{1},\left\lVert\bm{q}_{2}\right\rVert_{1}\in(0.9,1.1)\right)\geq 1-\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}\text{.}

Proof.

Let
H_{\lambda},H,L_{1},L_{2}
denote the number of items in each type.
By standard concentration bounds, we have
H_{\lambda}=\lambda m\pm O(\sqrt{\lambda m\log n})
,
H=(1-\lambda)m\pm O(\sqrt{m\log n})
and
L_{1},L_{2}=\frac{n-m}{2}\pm O(\sqrt{n\log n})
with probability at least
1-1/\mathrm{poly}(n)
.
For sufficiently large
n
, these conditions guarantee that the
\ell_{1}
-norm of the measures is in
(0.9,1.1)
(using the assumption without loss of generality that
m=\Omega(n^{2/3}\lambda^{-1})
).
For
\bm{p}_{1}
when
X=0
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{p}_{1}\right\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle=H_{\lambda}\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2\lambda m}+H\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2(1-\lambda)m}+L_{1}\frac{3\varepsilon}{2(n-m)}+L_{2}\frac{\varepsilon}{2(n-m)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}+O\left(\frac{\sqrt{\lambda m\log n}}{\lambda m}\right)+\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}+O\left(\frac{\sqrt{m\log n}}{m}\right)+\frac{3\varepsilon}{4}+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\pm O\left(\frac{\sqrt{n\log n}}{n}\right)

\displaystyle=1\pm o(1)\text{.}

Note that above we have used
\lambda m=\Omega\left(n^{2/3}\right)
so that all the error terms are
o(1)
for sufficiently large
n
.
The bounds on
\bm{p}_{2},\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2}
(and when
X=1
) follow similarly.
∎

Thus,
Lemma

6.21
implies that when sampling distributions from the meta-distribution
{\cal H}_{C}
, the distributions (and mixtures
\bm{r}_{1},\bm{r}_{2}
) satisfy the desired norm conditions.
By
Lemma

6.2
, it suffices to consider a deterministic Poissonized tester
\mathcal{A}
with sample complexity
m
.
We will show that if
\mathcal{A}
is
0.1
-correct with respect to
{\cal H}_{0},{\cal H}_{1}
,
\mathcal{A}
requires
\Omega\left(n^{2}m^{-2}\varepsilon^{-4}\lambda^{-3}\right)
queries.

As before, we will show a stronger result.

Lemma 6.22
.

Suppose
\mathcal{A}
is a deterministic Poissonized tester with sample complexity
m
is
0.1
-correct with respect to
{\cal H}_{0},{\cal H}_{1}
.
Then,
\mathcal{A}
queries
\Omega\left(n^{2}m^{-2}\varepsilon^{-4}\lambda^{-3}\right)
distinct buckets.
This bound holds even when a single query on bucket
i
reveals the source of every sample in bucket
i
.

Proof.

Given that
\mathcal{A}
is a Poissonized tester,
\mathcal{A}
receives independently
Y^{(1)}_{i}\sim\mathrm{Poi}(m\bm{r}_{1}[i]),Y_{i}^{(2)}\sim\mathrm{Poi}(m\bm{r}_{2}[i])
samples on bucket
i
, where
\bm{r}_{j}[i]=\frac{\bm{p}_{j}[i]+\bm{q}_{j}[i]}{2}
for
j\in\left\{1,2\right\}
.
Note that we can equivalently sample
Y_{i}^{(j)}=A_{i}^{(j)}+B_{i}^{(j)}
where
A_{i}^{(j)}\sim\mathrm{Poi}(\lambda m\bm{p}_{j}[i]),B_{i}^{(j)}\sim\mathrm{Poi}((1-\lambda)m\bm{q}_{j}[i])
are sampled independently.
Of course, the algorithm (without queries) only observes
Y_{i}:=(Y_{i}^{(1)},Y_{i}^{(2)})
.
If the algorithm queries a bucket
i
, then the algorithm learns
A_{i}:=(A_{i}^{(1)},A_{i}^{(2)}),B_{i}:=(B_{i}^{(1)},B_{i}^{(2)})
as well.
Suppose the algorithm queries a set of buckets
Q\subset[n]
.
Let
T_{i}
denote the information observed by the algorithm from the
i
-th bucket, i.e.
Y_{i}
if
i\not\in Q
and
A_{i},B_{i}
if
i\in Q
.
Let
T=(T_{1},\dotsc,T_{n})
.

Then, the algorithm
\mathcal{A}
is a function such that
\mathcal{A}(T)=X
with at least
51\%
probability, and therefore
I(X:T)\geq 2\cdot 10^{-4}
by
Lemma

3.8
.
Suppose for contradiction that
\mathcal{A}
queries
q=|Q|=o(n^{2}m^{-2}\varepsilon^{-4}\lambda^{-2})
buckets.
Since
T_{i}
are independent conditioned on
X
, we have

I(X:T)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}I(X:T_{i})\text{.}

For
i\not\in Q
, we claim
I(X:T_{i})=I(X:Y_{i})=0
.
In particular, for all
i\in[n],j\in\left\{1,2\right\}
, we have
Y_{i}^{(j)}\sim\mathrm{Poi}(m\bm{r}_{j}[i])
where

\bm{r}_{j}[i]=\begin{cases}\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2m}&w.p.\quad\frac{\lambda m}{n}\\
\frac{\alpha(1-\varepsilon)}{2m}+\frac{1-\varepsilon\alpha}{m}&w.p.\quad\frac{(1-\lambda)m}{n}\\
\frac{2\lambda\varepsilon}{(n-m)}&w.p.\quad\frac{n-m}{n}\end{cases}

(29)

regardless of
X
, proving the claim.
Thus, the samples reveal no information it suffices to bound the information gained from the queries via
\sum_{i\in Q}^{n}I(X:T_{i})=\sum_{i\in Q}^{n}I(X:A_{i},B_{i})
.

We begin with the simplifying assumption that no single bucket causes large collisions.

Lemma 6.23
.

\Pr\left(\max_{i}Y_{i}=O(\log n)\right)>1-\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}

Proof.

Note that each
Y_{i}\sim\mathrm{Poi}(m\bm{r}[i])
has parameter at most
2
from (
29
).
The claim follows as before from
Lemma

3.1
and the union bound.
∎

Let
E
denote the event that the conditions of
Lemma

6.21
and
Lemma

6.23
are met.

Lemma 6.24
.

For all
i
, we have

\displaystyle I(X:A_{i},B_{i})=O\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{3}m^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)\text{.}

Proof.

Note
A_{i},B_{i}
are pairs of Poisson random variables that sum up to
Y_{i}=(y,z)
.
By
3.9
, we have

I(X:A_{i},B_{i})=O\left(\sum_{a,b,c,d}\frac{\left(f_{a,b,c,d}(0)-f_{a,b,c,d}(1)\right)^{2}}{f_{a,b,c,d}(0)+f_{a,b,c,d}(1)}\right)=O\left(\sum_{a+b=y,c+d=z}\frac{\left(f_{a,b,c,d}(0)-f_{a,b,c,d}(1)\right)^{2}}{f_{a,b,c,d}(0)+f_{a,b,c,d}(1)}\right)\text{.}

where
f_{a,b,c,d}(x)=\Pr(A_{i}=(a,c),B_{i}=(b,d)|Y_{i}=(y,z),X=x,E)
and we note that unless
a+b=y,c+d=z
,
f_{a,b,c,d}(x)=0
.
We now compute
f_{a,b,c,d}(x)
explicitly.

\displaystyle f_{a,b,c,d}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Pr(A_{i}=(a,c),B_{i}=(b,d),Y_{i}=(y,z)|X=x,E)}{\Pr(Y_{i}=(y,z)|X=x,E)}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{\Pr(A_{i}=(a,c),B_{i}=(b,d)|X=x,E)}{\Pr(Y_{i}=(y,z)|X=x,E)}&\textrm{ if }a+b=y,c+d=z\\
0&\textrm{ o/w }\end{cases}

Define
g_{a,b,c,d}(x)=\Pr(A_{i}=(a,c),B_{i}=(b,d)|X=x,E)
and
h_{y,z}(x)=\Pr(Y_{i}=(y,z)|X=x,E)
.
Since samples are independent of
X
, define
h_{y,z}:=h_{y,z}(0)=h_{y,z}(1)
.
Then, we can rewrite

I(X:A_{i},B_{i})=O\left(\frac{1}{h_{y,z}}\sum_{a+b=y,c+d=z}\frac{\left(g_{a,b,c,d}(0)-g_{a,b,c,d}(1)\right)^{2}}{g_{a,b,c,d}(0)+g_{a,b,c,d}(1)}\right)\text{.}

We require the following technical claims.

Claim 6.25
.

h_{y,z}=\Omega\left(\frac{m}{n}\frac{(1-\varepsilon\alpha)^{y+z}}{y!z!}\right)\text{.}

Claim 6.26
.

For
x\in\left\{0,1\right\}
,

g_{a,b,c,d}(x)=\Omega\left(\frac{\lambda m}{n}\frac{\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}\right)^{a+c}}{a!c!}\right)\text{.}

Claim 6.27
.

If
a+b=0
,
c+d=0
,
then
g_{a,b,c,d}(0)=g_{a,b,c,d}(1)
.
Otherwise
a+b\geq 1
,
c+d\geq 1

and

\displaystyle(g_{a,b,c,d}(0)-g_{a,b,c,d}(1))^{2}

\displaystyle=O\left(\left(\frac{1}{a!b!c!d!}\left(\frac{\varepsilon\lambda m}{n}\right)^{a+b+c+d}\cdot\left(3^{a+c}+3^{b+d}-3^{a+d}-3^{b+c}\right)\right)^{2}\right)\text{.}

We will prove these claims at the end of this section.
Applying
6.27
, we note that if either
Y_{i}^{(1)},Y_{i}^{(2)}
are
0
,
then
I(X:A_{i},B_{i})=0
.
Thus, we assume
1\leq Y_{i}^{(1)},Y_{i}^{(2)}
.
Applying
6.25
,
6.26
and
6.27
, we obtain

\displaystyle I(X:A_{i},B_{i})

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{1}{\frac{m}{n}\frac{(1-\varepsilon\alpha)^{y+z}}{y!z!}}\sum_{a+b=y,c+d=z}\frac{\left(\frac{1}{a!b!c!d!}\left(\frac{\varepsilon\lambda m}{n}\right)^{a+b+c+d}\cdot\left(3^{a+c}+3^{b+d}-3^{a+d}-3^{b+c}\right)\right)^{2}}{\frac{\lambda m}{n}\frac{\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}\right)^{a+c}}{a!c!}}\right)

\displaystyle=O\left(\left(\frac{m}{n}\right)^{2y+2z-2}\frac{2^{y+z}\varepsilon^{2y+2z}\lambda^{2y+2z-1}}{\frac{(1-\varepsilon)^{2y+2z}}{y!z!}}\sum_{a+b=y,c+d=z}\frac{1}{a!b!c!d!}\left(3^{a+c}+3^{b+d}-3^{a+d}-3^{b+c}\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=O\left(\left(\frac{m}{n}\right)^{2y+2z-2}\frac{2^{y+z}\varepsilon^{2y+2z}\lambda^{2y+2z-1}}{(1-\varepsilon)^{2y+2z}}y!z!\sum_{a+b=y,c+d=z}\frac{\left(3^{a}-3^{b}\right)^{2}\left(3^{c}-3^{d}\right)^{2}}{a!b!c!d!}\right)\text{.}

In the first line we simplify by collecting like terms, and observe
(1-\varepsilon\alpha)\geq\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}
and
a+b=y,c+d=z
.
In the second line, we note that
3^{a+c}+3^{b+d}-3^{a+d}-3^{b+c}=(3^{a}-3^{b})(3^{c}-3^{d})
.
To complete the bound, we require the following technical claim, which we prove at the end of this section.

Claim 6.28
.

y!z!\sum_{a+b=y,c+d=z}\frac{\left(3^{a}-3^{b}\right)^{2}\left(3^{c}-3^{d}\right)^{2}}{a!b!c!d!}=O\left(18^{y+z}\right)\text{.}

Applying
6.28
we obtain

\displaystyle I(X:A_{i},B_{i})

\displaystyle=O\left(\left(\frac{m}{n}\right)^{2y+2z-2}2^{y+z}\varepsilon^{2y+2z}\lambda^{2y+2z-1}\frac{18^{y+z}}{(1-\varepsilon)^{2y+2z}}\right)

\displaystyle=O\left(\left(\frac{m}{n}\right)^{2y+2z-2}\frac{(6\varepsilon\lambda)^{2y+2z}}{(1-\varepsilon)^{2y+2z}}\right)\frac{1}{\lambda}

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{3}m^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)

where in the last term we observe that the quantity is decreasing in
y+z
and use
y+z\geq 2
since
I(X:A_{i},B_{i})=0
whenever
y=0
or
z=0
by
6.27
.
∎

Given
Lemma

6.24
, we conclude that

I(X:T)=O\left(q\frac{\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{3}m^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)=o(1)

which contradicts
I(X:T)\geq 2\cdot 10^{-4}
.
Thus, we must have
q=\Omega\left(\frac{n^{2}}{m^{2}\varepsilon^{4}\lambda^{3}}\right)
.
∎

It remains to prove the omitted claims, which we prove below.
This concludes the proof of
Theorem

1.5
.
∎

Proof.

From (

) we have

\displaystyle h_{y,z}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda m}{n}\exp\left(-\frac{1-\varepsilon}{m}\right)\frac{\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}\right)^{y+z}}{y!z!}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{(1-\lambda)m}{n}\exp\left(-\alpha(1-\varepsilon)-2(1-\varepsilon\alpha)\right)\frac{\left(\frac{\alpha(1-\varepsilon)}{2}+(1-\varepsilon\alpha)\right)^{y+z}}{y!z!}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{n-m}{m}e^{-4\varepsilon\lambda m/(n-m)}\frac{(2\varepsilon\lambda m/(n-m))^{y+z}}{y!z!}

\displaystyle g_{a,b,c,d}(0)

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda m}{n}\exp\left(-(1-\varepsilon)\right)\frac{\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}\right)^{a+c}}{a!c!}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{(1-\lambda)m}{n}\exp\left(-\alpha(1-\varepsilon)-2(1-\varepsilon\alpha)\right)\frac{(\frac{\alpha(1-\varepsilon)}{2})^{a+c}((1-\varepsilon\alpha))^{b+d}}{a!b!c!d!}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{n-m}{2n}\exp\left(\frac{-3\varepsilon\lambda m}{(n-m)}\right)\frac{\left(\frac{3\varepsilon\lambda m}{2(n-m)}\right)^{a+c}}{a!c!}\cdot\exp\left(\frac{-\varepsilon\lambda m}{(n-m)}\right)\frac{\left(\frac{\varepsilon\lambda m}{2(n-m)}\right)^{b+d}}{b!d!}

\displaystyle=\Omega\left(\frac{\lambda m}{n}\frac{\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}\right)^{a+c}}{a!c!}\right)\text{.}

Note that a similar bound holds for

Proof.

Following

, we write

\displaystyle g_{a,b,c,d}(1)

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda m}{n}\exp\left(-(1-\varepsilon)\right)\frac{\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}\right)^{a+c}}{a!c!}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{(1-\lambda)m}{n}\exp\left(-\alpha(1-\varepsilon)-2(1-\varepsilon\alpha)\right)\frac{(\frac{\alpha(1-\varepsilon)}{2})^{a+c}((1-\varepsilon\alpha))^{b+d}}{a!b!c!d!}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{n-m}{2n}\exp\left(\frac{-3\varepsilon\lambda m}{(n-m)}\right)\frac{\left(\frac{3\varepsilon\lambda m}{2(n-m)}\right)^{a+d}}{a!d!}\cdot\exp\left(\frac{-\varepsilon\lambda m}{(n-m)}\right)\frac{\left(\frac{\varepsilon\lambda m}{2(n-m)}\right)^{b+c}}{b!c!}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{n-m}{2n}\exp\left(-\frac{3\varepsilon\lambda m}{(n-m)}\right)\frac{\left(\frac{3\varepsilon\lambda m}{2(n-m)}\right)^{b+c}}{b!c!}\cdot\exp\left(\frac{-\varepsilon\lambda m}{(n-m)}\right)\frac{\left(\frac{\varepsilon\lambda m}{2(n-m)}\right)^{a+d}}{a!d!}\text{.}

We consider separate cases.
If either
a=b=0
or
c=d=0
, note that
g_{a,b,c,d}(0)=g_{a,b,c,d}(1)
.

Thus, we may assume
a+b\geq 1
,

(equivalently
y,z\geq 1
).
Note that the contribution from the heavy elements (i.e. where
W_{i}\in\left\{h_{\lambda},h\right\}
) appears in both
g_{a,b,c,d}(0),g_{a,b,c,d}(1)
and therefore cancels out in the difference.
The difference can then be bounded by

\displaystyle(g_{a,b,c,d}(0)-g_{a,b,c,d}(1))^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{n-m}{2n}\frac{1}{a!b!c!d!}\exp\left(-\frac{4\varepsilon\lambda m}{n-m}\right)\left(\frac{\varepsilon\lambda m}{2(n-m)}\right)^{a+b+c+d}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\cdot\left(3^{a+c}+3^{b+d}-3^{a+d}-3^{b+c}\right)^{2}

From our assumption
m\leq n/2
we can simplify the above bound to

\displaystyle(g_{a,b,c,d}(0)-g_{a,b,c,d}(1))^{2}

\displaystyle=O\left(\left(\frac{1}{a!b!c!d!}\left(\frac{\varepsilon\lambda m}{n}\right)^{a+b+c+d}\cdot\left(3^{a+c}+3^{b+d}-3^{a+d}-3^{b+c}\right)\right)^{2}\right)\text{.}

∎

See

Proof.

We begin by computing

\displaystyle\sum_{a+b=y}\frac{(3^{a}-3^{b})^{2}}{a!b!}

\displaystyle=O\left(\sum_{a<y/2}\frac{y!9^{a}}{a!(y-a)!}\right)=O\left(18^{y}\right)

since after factoring out
9^{a}
we are left with a sum of binomial coefficients.
Similarly, we may bound

\sum_{c+d=z}\frac{(3^{c}-3^{d})^{2}}{c!d!}=O\left(18^{z}\right)\text{.}

Combining the two bounds yields the desired result.
∎

7
Query-Optimal Closeness Testing

In this section, we design a closeness tester with nearly optimal
\widetilde{O}(\varepsilon^{-2}\lambda^{-1})
query complexity. Our approach relies on taking enough samples to guarantee empirical estimates with small error. In particular, we will rely on the following standard distribution learning bound.

Theorem 7.1

(Distribution Learning)
.

Let
\bm{r}\colon[n]\to[0,1]
be an arbitrary probability distribution, and let
\widetilde{\bm{r}}\colon[n]\to[0,1]
be an empirical estimate
10
10

10

I.e.,
\widetilde{\bm{r}}[i]
is the number of samples observed from element
i
divided by the total number of samples,
m
.
using
m
samples from
\bm{r}
. Then, for every
i\in[n]
and
\alpha\in(0,1)
, we have

\Pr\left[|\bm{r}[i]-\widetilde{\bm{r}}[i]|>\sqrt{\frac{3\bm{r}[i]\ln(2/\alpha)}{m}}\right]\leq\alpha\text{.}

Proof.

First, note that
m\widetilde{\bm{r}}[i]
follows a
\mathrm{Bin}(m,\bm{r}[i])
distribution, i.e.
m\widetilde{\bm{r}}[i]=X_{1}+\cdots+X_{m}
where each
X_{j}
is an independent
\mathrm{Bern}(\bm{r}[i])
random variable. We have
\mathbb{E}[m\widetilde{\bm{r}}[i]]=m\bm{r}[i]
. Thus, by the Chernoff bound, we have

\delta:=\sqrt{\frac{3\ln(2/\alpha)}{m\bm{r}[i]}}~\implies~\Pr[|m\widetilde{\bm{r}}[i]-m\bm{r}[i]|>\delta m\bm{r}[i]]\leq 2\exp\left(-\delta^{2}m\bm{r}[i]/3\right)=\alpha

or equivalently,
\Pr[|\widetilde{\bm{r}}[i]-\bm{r}[i]|>\delta\bm{r}[i]]\leq\alpha
, as claimed. ∎

The following is a straightforward corollary for mixtures
\bm{r}=\lambda\bm{p}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}
.

Corollary 7.2
.

Let
\bm{p}\colon[n]\to[0,1]
be an arbitrary distribution and let
Z
be a multi-set of
m
samples drawn from
\bm{r}=\lambda\bm{p}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}
. Let
P[i]
denote the number of samples which were drawn by
\bm{p}
on element
i
. Then,

\Pr_{Z}\left[\left|\frac{P[i]}{\lambda m}-\bm{p}[i]\right|>\sqrt{\frac{3\bm{p}[i]\ln(2/\alpha)}{\lambda m}}\right]\leq\alpha\text{.}

Proof.

Consider a distribution
\bm{p}_{\lambda}\colon[n]\cup\{A\}\to[0,1]
where
\bm{p}_{\lambda}[i]=\lambda\bm{p}[i]
and
\bm{p}_{\lambda}(A)=1-\lambda
. For
i\in[n]
, the random variable
P_{i}/m
is equivalent to an
m
-sample empirical estimate of
\bm{p}_{\lambda}[i]=\lambda\bm{p}[i]
. Therefore, by
Theorem

7.1
we have

\Pr_{Z}\left[\left|\frac{P_{i}}{m}-\lambda\bm{p}[i]\right|>\sqrt{\frac{3\lambda\bm{p}[i]\ln(2/\alpha)}{m}}\right]\leq\alpha

and so dividing by
\lambda
completes the proof. ∎

We are now ready to prove our main theorem.

Theorem 7.3
.

(Query-Optimal Closeness Testing.)

Suppose we have verification query access to two mixtures
\bm{r}_{1}=\lambda\bm{p}_{1}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}_{1}
,
\bm{r}_{2}=\lambda\bm{p}_{2}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}_{2}
. There is an algorithm that distinguishes between the case of
\bm{p}_{1}=\bm{p}_{2}
and
\left\lVert\bm{p}_{1}-\bm{p}_{2}\right\rVert_{1}\geq\varepsilon
with probability
2/3
using

O\left(n\left(\log n+\frac{\log^{2}(1/\varepsilon\lambda)}{\varepsilon^{2}\lambda}\right)\right)~\text{ samples and }~O\left(\frac{\log^{3}(1/\varepsilon\lambda)}{\varepsilon^{2}\lambda}\right)~\text{ queries.}

Proof.

We invoke a flattening procedure which allows us to assume
\bm{r}_{b}
and
\bm{p}_{b}
(for
b\in\{1,2\}
) have bounded infinity norm, then design an efficient tester for such distributions. Our flattening procedure is described in the following theorem, which relies on
Theorem

7.1
and is proven in
Section

A.2
. (It is an immediate corollary of
Theorem

A.2
.)

Theorem 7.4

(High Sample Mixture Flattening)
.

Using
O(n\log n)
samples to a mixture
\bm{r}=\lambda\bm{p}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}
over
[n]
, it is possible to produce a
\ell_{1}
-distance preserving
11
11

11

For any two distributions
\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{2}
over
[n]
, we have
\left\lVert f(\mathcal{D}_{1})-f(\mathcal{D}_{2})\right\rVert_{1}=\left\lVert\mathcal{D}_{1}-\mathcal{D}_{2}\right\rVert_{1}
.
random map
f\colon[n]\to[N]
such that with probability
0.999
, we have
\left\lVert f(\bm{r})\right\rVert_{\infty}<\frac{2}{N},\left\lVert f(\bm{p})\right\rVert_{\infty}<\frac{2}{\lambda N}
, and
N\leq 3n
.

We invoke
Theorem

7.4
on
\frac{1}{2}(\bm{r}_{1}+\bm{r}_{2})
so that with probability
0.999
we have
N\leq 3n
and

\displaystyle\frac{2}{N}\geq\left\lVert f\left(\frac{1}{2}(\bm{r}_{1}+\bm{r}_{2})\right)\right\rVert_{\infty}=\left\lVert\frac{1}{2}\left(f(\bm{r}_{1})+f(\bm{r}_{2})\right)\right\rVert_{\infty}\geq\frac{1}{2}\max\left(\left\lVert f(\bm{r}_{1})\right\rVert_{\infty},\left\lVert f(\bm{r}_{2})\right\rVert_{\infty}\right)

which implies that
\left\lVert f(\bm{r}_{1})\right\rVert_{2}^{2},\left\lVert f(\bm{r}_{2})\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq 4/N
. Similarly,

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\lambda N}\geq\left\lVert f\left(\frac{1}{2}(\bm{p}_{1}+\bm{p}_{2})\right)\right\rVert_{\infty}=\left\lVert\frac{1}{2}\left(f(\bm{p}_{1})+f(\bm{p}_{2})\right)\right\rVert_{\infty}\geq\frac{1}{2}\max\left(\left\lVert f(\bm{p}_{1})\right\rVert_{\infty},\left\lVert f(\bm{p}_{2})\right\rVert_{\infty}\right)

and so
\left\lVert f(\bm{p}_{1})\right\rVert_{2}^{2},\left\lVert f(\bm{p}_{2})\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq 4/\lambda N
. Since
f
preserves
\ell_{1}
-distance, it suffices to test closeness of
f(\bm{p}_{1})
and
f(\bm{p}_{2})
. Moreover, we can simulate sample and verification query access to the mixtures

f(\bm{r}_{b})=f(\lambda\bm{p}_{b}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}_{b})=\lambda f(\bm{p}_{b})+(1-\lambda)f(\bm{q}_{b})

for both
b\in\{1,2\}
. Next, we apply another random map which allows us to assume that the mixtures have reasonable mass on every element, which will simplify our later analysis. This reduction is captured in the following lemma.

Lemma 7.5
.

Suppose we have sample and verification query access to arbitrary mixtures
\bm{r}_{1}=\lambda\bm{p}_{1}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}_{1}
and
\bm{r}_{2}=\lambda\bm{p}_{2}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}_{2}
over
[N]
. Let
g\colon[N]\to[N]
be a random map such that for every
j\in[N]
, with probability
1/2
,
g(j)=j
, and with probability
1/2
,
g(j)
is uniformly distributed in
[N]
. Then the following hold.

1.

\left\lVert g(\bm{p}_{1})-g(\bm{p}_{2})\right\rVert_{1}=\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\bm{p}_{1}-\bm{p}_{2}\right\rVert_{1}
.

2.

We can simulate sample and verification query access to
g(\bm{r}_{b})=\lambda g(\bm{p}_{b})+(1-\lambda)g(\bm{q}_{b})
for both
b\in\{1,2\}
.

In particular, the definition of the random map
g
from
Lemma

7.5
guarantees that
g(\mathcal{D})[j]\geq 1/2N
for every
j\in[N]
, and
\left\lVert g(\mathcal{D})\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\left\lVert\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{\infty}
for any distribution
\mathcal{D}
. Moreover,
g
preserves
\ell_{1}
-distances to a factor of
2
. Thus, it now suffices to establish the following theorem, which we prove in
Section

7.1
. (This constitutes the main effort of the proof.)

Theorem 7.6

(Closeness Testing Post-Flattening)
.

Suppose we have sample and verification query access to two mixtures
\bm{r}_{1}=\lambda\bm{p}_{1}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}_{1}
,
\bm{r}_{2}=\lambda\bm{p}_{2}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}_{2}
with the promise
12
12

12

The upper bounds can be guaranteed by flattening
Theorem

A.2
using
O(n\log n)
samples. The lower bound can guaranteed by the reduction
Lemma

7.5
.
that
\left\lVert\bm{r}_{b}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 4/n
,
\left\lVert\bm{p}_{b}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 4/\lambda n
, and
\min_{j\in[n]}\bm{r}_{b}[j]\geq 1/2n
for both
b\in\{1,2\}
. Then, there is an algorithm that distinguishes between the case of
\bm{p}_{1}=\bm{p}_{2}
and
\left\lVert\bm{p}_{1}-\bm{p}_{2}\right\rVert_{1}\geq\varepsilon
with probability
2/3
using

O\left(\frac{n\log^{2}(1/\varepsilon\lambda)}{\varepsilon^{2}\lambda}\right)~\text{ samples and }~O\left(\frac{\log^{3}(1/\varepsilon\lambda)}{\varepsilon^{2}\lambda}\right)~\text{ queries.}

In particular, we invoke
Theorem

7.6
using the mixtures
g(f(\bm{r}_{b}))
as input and with distance parameter
\varepsilon/2
. The query bound follows by our bound on
N
, and this completes the proof. ∎

Proof of
Lemma

7.5
.

We first observe that item (2) holds. Using the definition of
g
, we can clearly simulate samples to
g(\bm{r}_{b})
. Next, suppose we have a sample
x\sim\bm{r}_{b}
and we want to simulate a verification query on
g(x)
. We can simply query
x
and use the oracle’s response for
g(x)
.

We now prove item (1). Observe that for any
j\in[N]
, we have
g(\bm{p}_{b})[j]=\bm{p}_{b}[j]/2+(1/2N)
. Thus,

\displaystyle\left\lVert g(\bm{p}_{1})-g(\bm{p}_{2})\right\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}|g(\bm{p}_{1})[j]-g(\bm{p}_{2})[j]|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}|(\bm{p}_{1}[j]+1/N)-(\bm{p}_{2}[j]+1/N)|=\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\bm{p}_{1}-\bm{p}_{2}\right\rVert_{1}

and this completes the proof.

7.1
Closeness Testing after Flattening: Proof of
Theorem

7.6

The algorithm is defined as follows. Throughout the algorithm’s description, let
\gamma=\varepsilon\lambda/10000
. Using
Lemma

3.16
, we first obtain an estimate
\hat{\lambda}
such that
\hat{\lambda}\in[\lambda/2,\lambda]
using
O(1/\lambda)
samples and queries which we use in place of
\lambda
throughout the algorithm’s description. We have written italicized comments in teal to aid the reader’s intuition for how the analysis coincides with the tester.

1.

(Sampling Phase.)
Draw multi-sets
Z^{(1)},Z^{(2)}
of
m=O(\frac{n\log^{2}(1/\varepsilon\lambda)}{\varepsilon^{2}\lambda})
samples from
\bm{r}_{1},\bm{r}_{2}
. Let
Z^{(1)}_{i},Z^{(2)}_{i}
denote the sets of samples occurring on element
i
. For
b\in\{1,2\}
, let
P_{b}[i],Q_{b}[i]
and
R_{b}[i]=P_{b}[i]+Q_{b}[i]
denote the number of samples in
Z^{(b)}_{i}
generated by
\bm{p}_{b},\bm{q}_{b}
and
\bm{r}_{b}
, respectively.

2.

(Selecting Test Elements.)
For each
j\leq\lceil\log(8/\varepsilon\lambda)\rceil=:k
, let
X_{j}
be a set of
|X_{j}|=10\cdot 2^{j}
samples drawn from
U_{n}
. Let
X=X_{1}\cup\cdots\cup X_{k}
.

(By Markov’s, with high probability all
i\in X_{j}
satisfy
\bm{p}_{b}[i]\leq\widetilde{O}(\frac{2^{j}}{n})
.)

3.

(Coin Definition.)
For each
i\in X
and
b\in\{1,2\}
, let
\rho_{b}[i]:=\frac{P_{b}[i]}{R_{b}[i]}
.

(We can simulate a
\mathrm{Bern}(\rho_{b}[i])
trial by querying a uniform random element of
Z_{i}^{(b)}
. By the learning error bound, we will be able to guarantee that
|\frac{P_{b}[i]}{\lambda m}-\bm{p}_{b}[i]|\ll\frac{\varepsilon 2^{j}}{n}
. By the learning error bound and the fact that
\frac{1}{2n}\leq\bm{r}_{b}[i]\leq\frac{4}{n}
, we will also have
\frac{R_{b}[i]}{m}=\Theta(\frac{1}{n})
. This gives us a bound of
\widetilde{O}(\lambda 2^{j})
on
\rho_{b}[i]
, which helps us bound the query complexity of estimating
\rho_{b}[i]
.)

4.

(Query Phase.)
For
j\leq k
and
i\in X_{j}
:

(a)

Obtain an estimate
\tilde{\rho}_{b}[i]
such that
|\tilde{\rho}_{b}[i]-\rho_{b}[i]|\leq O(\lambda\varepsilon 2^{j})
with probability
1-\gamma
by invoking
Lemma

3.13
.

(This uses
\widetilde{O}(\frac{\lambda 2^{j}}{(\lambda\varepsilon 2^{j})^{2}})=\widetilde{O}(\frac{1}{\lambda 2^{j}\varepsilon^{2}})
queries. Note that this implies
|\frac{R_{b}[i]}{\lambda m}\tilde{\rho}_{b}[i]-\frac{P_{b}[i]}{\lambda m}|=\frac{R_{b}[i]}{\lambda m}|\tilde{\rho}_{b}[i]-\rho_{b}[i]|\leq\frac{R_{b}[i]}{\lambda m}\cdot O(\lambda\varepsilon 2^{j})\leq O(\frac{\varepsilon 2^{j}}{n})
. This last inequality used the learning error bound on the empirical
\bm{r}_{b}[i]
estimate
\frac{R_{b}[i]}{m}
and the bound
\left\lVert\bm{r}_{b}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 4/n
.)

(b)

If
|\frac{R_{b}[i]}{\lambda m}\tilde{\rho}_{1}[i]-\frac{R_{b}[i]}{\lambda m}\tilde{\rho}_{2}[i]|>\frac{\varepsilon 2^{j}}{8n}
, then
reject
.

5.

Accept
.

Claim 7.7
.

With probability
0.999
, for every
j\leq k
,
i\in X_{j}
, and
b\in\{1,2\}
, we have
\bm{p}_{b}[i]\leq\frac{1000\cdot 2^{j}\log(1/\varepsilon\lambda)}{n}
.

Proof.

Note that
\mathbb{E}_{i\sim U_{n}}[\bm{p}_{b}[i]]=1/n
and so by Markov’s
\Pr_{i\sim U_{n}}[\bm{p}_{b}[i]>\frac{t\cdot 2^{j}}{n}]\leq\frac{1}{t\cdot 2^{j}}
. Therefore, setting
t:=1000\log(1/\varepsilon\lambda)
, by a union bound we have
\bm{p}_{b}[i]\leq\frac{1000\cdot 2^{j}\log(1/\varepsilon\lambda)}{n}
for all
b\in\{1,2\},j\leq k
, and
i\in X_{j}
with probability
0.999
. ∎

Therefore, throughout the proof we will assume the above holds for the elements in
X_{1},\ldots,X_{k}
. Next, we condition on the samples obtained in step (1) yielding small learning error. Here, we crucially use the bound from
7.7
.

Claim 7.8
.

With probability
0.996
, for every
j\leq k
,
i\in X_{j}
, and
b\in\{1,2\}
, we have
\left|\frac{R_{b}[i]}{m}-\bm{r}_{b}[i]\right|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{200n}
and
\left|\frac{P_{b}[i]}{\lambda m}-\bm{p}_{b}[i]\right|\leq\frac{\varepsilon 2^{j}}{200n}
.

We defer the proof of this claim to
Section

7.2
and now condition on the bounds from
7.8
. Note that combining
7.8
with the fact that
1/2n\leq\bm{r}_{b}[i]\leq 4/n
, we have
1/3n\leq R_{b}[i]/m\leq 5/n
.

Query complexity.

We use the above properties to bound the cost of each bias estimation in line (4a). Since
R_{b}[i]/m\geq 1/3n
and
\bm{p}_{b}[i]\leq O(\frac{2^{j}\log(1/\varepsilon\lambda)}{n})
, and using
7.8
, we have

\rho_{b}[i]=\frac{P_{b}[i]}{R_{b}[i]}=\lambda\cdot\frac{P_{b}[i]/\lambda m}{R_{b}[i]/m}\leq O\left(\lambda\cdot\frac{\bm{p}_{b}[i]+\varepsilon 2^{j}/n}{1/n}\right)\leq O(\lambda 2^{j}\log(1/\varepsilon\lambda))

and thus, by
Lemma

3.13
, estimating
\rho_{b}[i]
to additive error
O(\lambda\varepsilon 2^{j})
in line (4a) has query complexity

O\left(\frac{\lambda 2^{j}\log(1/\varepsilon\lambda)\log(1/\gamma)}{(\lambda\varepsilon 2^{j})^{2}}\right)=O\left(\frac{\log^{2}(1/\varepsilon\lambda)}{\lambda 2^{j}\varepsilon^{2}}\right)\text{.}

In total, the query complexity of the tester is bounded by (using
|X_{j}|\leq O(2^{j})
)

\sum_{j\leq\log(1/\varepsilon\lambda)}|X_{j}|\cdot O\left(\frac{\log^{2}(1/\varepsilon\lambda)}{\lambda 2^{j}\varepsilon^{2}}\right)=\sum_{j\leq\log(1/\varepsilon\lambda)}O\left(2^{j}\cdot\frac{\log^{2}(1/\varepsilon\lambda)}{\lambda 2^{j}\varepsilon^{2}}\right)=O\left(\frac{\log^{3}(1/\varepsilon\lambda)}{\lambda\varepsilon^{2}}\right)\text{.}

Proof of correctness.

By our choice of
\gamma\ll\varepsilon\lambda
and the fact that
|X_{1}\cup\cdots\cup X_{k}|\leq\frac{20}{\varepsilon\lambda}
, we assume by a union bound that the bias estimation for each
i\in X_{j}
yields
\widetilde{\rho}_{b}[i]
such that
|\widetilde{\rho}_{b}[i]-\rho_{b}[i]|\leq\lambda\varepsilon 2^{j}/1000
, so that

\displaystyle\left|\frac{R_{b}[i]}{\lambda m}\tilde{\rho}_{b}[i]-\frac{P_{b}[i]}{\lambda m}\right|=\frac{R_{b}[i]}{\lambda m}|\tilde{\rho}_{b}[i]-\rho_{b}[i]|\leq\frac{R_{b}[i]}{\lambda m}\cdot\frac{\lambda\varepsilon 2^{j}}{1000}\leq\frac{\varepsilon 2^{j}}{200n}\text{.}

(30)

In the last inequality we used the bound
R_{b}[i]/m\leq 5/n
.

Completeness.

We now provide the proof of correctness. Suppose that
\bm{p}_{1}=\bm{p}_{2}
. Then for every
i\in X
we have
\bm{p}_{1}[i]=\bm{p}_{2}[i]
. By definition of the tester, when step (4b) is reached for some
i\in X_{j}
, for both
b\in\{1,2\}
we have by the triangle inequality

\displaystyle\left|\frac{R_{b}[i]}{\lambda m}\tilde{\rho}_{b}[i]-\bm{p}_{b}[i]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\frac{R_{b}[i]}{\lambda m}\tilde{\rho}_{b}[i]-\frac{P_{b}[i]}{\lambda m}\right|+\left|\frac{P_{b}[i]}{\lambda m}-\bm{p}_{b}[i]\right|\leq\frac{\varepsilon 2^{j}}{200n}+\frac{\varepsilon 2^{j}}{200n}\leq\frac{\varepsilon 2^{j}}{100n}

(31)

where the first term is bounded using the bias estimation error bound
eq.

30
and the second term is bounded by our conditioning on small error due to learning,
7.8
. Therefore, by
eq.

31
and another application of the triangle inequality we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{R_{1}[i]}{\lambda m}\tilde{\rho}_{1}[i]-\frac{R_{2}[i]}{\lambda m}\tilde{\rho}_{2}[i]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\frac{R_{1}[i]}{\lambda m}\tilde{\rho}_{1}[i]-\bm{p}_{1}[i]\right|+\left|\frac{R_{2}[i]}{\lambda m}\tilde{\rho}_{2}[i]-\bm{p}_{2}[i]\right|+|\bm{p}_{1}[i]-\bm{p}_{2}[i]|\leq\frac{\varepsilon 2^{j}}{50n}

(32)

and so the tester does not reject in step (4b).

Soundness.

Now suppose that
\left\lVert\bm{p}_{1}-\bm{p}_{2}\right\rVert_{1}>\varepsilon
. The following is the main lemma for the analysis.

Lemma 7.9
.

Recall that
k=\lceil\log(8/\varepsilon\lambda)\rceil
and for each
j\leq k
,
X_{j}
is a set of
10\cdot 2^{j}
i.i.d. uniform samples from
[n]
. If
\left\lVert\bm{p}_{1}-\bm{p}_{2}\right\rVert_{1}>\varepsilon
, then

\Pr_{X_{1},\ldots,X_{k}}\left[\exists j\leq k,i\in X_{j}\colon|\bm{p}_{1}[i]-\bm{p}_{2}[i]|>\frac{\varepsilon 2^{j}}{4n}\right]\geq 0.999\text{.}

By
Lemma

7.9
, with probability
0.999
, for some
j
, the set
X_{j}
obtained in line (1) of the tester contains some element
i
such that
|\bm{p}_{1}[i]-\bm{p}_{2}[i]|>\varepsilon 2^{j}/4n
. Note that we again have that
eq.

31
holds for both
b\in\{1,2\}
and all
i\in X_{j}
using the same argument from the completeness case.
Then, by the triangle inequality we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{R_{1}[i]}{\lambda m}\tilde{\rho}_{1}[i]-\frac{R_{2}[i]}{\lambda m}\tilde{\rho}_{2}[i]\right|

\displaystyle\geq|\bm{p}_{1}[i]-\bm{p}_{2}[i]|-\left(\left|\frac{R_{1}[i]}{\lambda m}\tilde{\rho}_{1}[i]-\bm{p}_{1}[i]\right|+\left|\frac{R_{2}[i]}{\lambda m}\tilde{\rho}_{2}[i]-\bm{p}_{2}[i]\right|\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\varepsilon 2^{j}}{4n}-\frac{\varepsilon 2^{j}}{100n}-\frac{\varepsilon 2^{j}}{100n}>\frac{\varepsilon 2^{j}}{8n}

(33)

which causes the tester to correctly reject in step (5b). This completes the proof of
Theorem

7.6
.

7.2
Deferred Proofs

Proof of
Lemma

7.9
.
We prove the lemma using a bucketing argument. Let

A=\{i\in[n]\colon|\bm{p}_{1}[i]-\bm{p}_{2}[i]|<\varepsilon/2n\}~\text{ and }~B=\{i\in[n]\colon\varepsilon/2n<|\bm{p}_{1}[i]-\bm{p}_{2}[i]|\leq 4/\lambda n\}\text{.}

Recall that
\left\lVert\bm{p}_{b}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 4/\lambda n
, which implies that
A,B
partition the domain,
[n]
, since
|\bm{p}_{1}[i]-\bm{p}_{2}[i]|>4/\lambda n
would imply
\max(\bm{p}_{1}[i],\bm{p}_{2}[i])>4/\lambda n
, contradicting our bound on the
\infty
-norm. We further partition
B:=B_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup B_{k}
where
k=\lceil\log(8/\varepsilon\lambda)\rceil
and

B_{j}=\{i\in B\colon 2^{j}(\varepsilon/4n)\leq|\bm{p}_{1}[i]-\bm{p}_{2}[i]|<2^{j+1}(\varepsilon/4n)\}\text{ for }j\leq k\text{.}

First, observe that

\varepsilon\leq\sum_{i}\big|\bm{p}_{1}[i]-\bm{p}_{2}[i]\big|\leq|A|\cdot\frac{\varepsilon}{2n}+\sum_{i\in B}|\bm{p}_{1}[i]-\bm{p}_{2}[i]|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}+\sum_{i\in B}|\bm{p}_{1}[i]-\bm{p}_{2}[i]|~\implies~\sum_{i\in B}|\bm{p}_{1}[i]-\bm{p}_{2}[i]|>\varepsilon/2

Now, using the bucketing, we have

\displaystyle\varepsilon/2<\sum_{j\leq k}\sum_{i\in B_{j}}|\bm{p}_{1}[i]-\bm{p}_{2}[i]|\leq\sum_{j\leq k}|B_{j}|2^{j+1}(\varepsilon/4n)~\implies~\sum_{j\leq k}|B_{j}|2^{j}\geq n\text{.}

Observe that

\displaystyle\Pr_{X_{j}}[X_{j}\cap B_{j}=\emptyset]=\left(1-\frac{|B_{j}|}{n}\right)^{10\cdot 2^{j}}\leq\exp\left(-10\cdot\frac{|B_{j}|2^{j}}{n}\right)\text{.}

Combining the previous two inequalities and using independence of the
X_{j}
-s, we obtain

\displaystyle\Pr[\forall j\leq k\colon X_{j}\cap B_{j}=\emptyset]

\displaystyle=\prod_{j\leq k}\Pr_{X_{j}}[X_{j}\cap B_{j}=\emptyset]\leq\exp\left(-\frac{10}{n}\sum_{j\leq k}|B_{j}|2^{j}\right)\leq e^{-10}<.0001\text{.}

Finally, observe that this implies

\displaystyle\Pr_{X_{1},\ldots,X_{k}}\left[\exists j\leq k,i\in X_{j}\colon|\bm{p}_{1}[i]-\bm{p}_{2}[i]|>\frac{2^{j}\varepsilon}{4n}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\Pr[\exists j\leq k\colon X_{j}\cap B_{j}\neq\emptyset]

\displaystyle=1-\Pr[\forall j\leq k\colon X_{j}\cap B_{j}=\emptyset]\geq 0.999\text{.}

This completes the proof. ∎

Proof of
7.8
.
By
Corollary

7.2
, for each
i\in[n]
, we have

\Pr_{Z}\left[\left|\frac{P_{b}[i]}{\lambda m}-\bm{p}_{b}[i]\right|\leq\sqrt{\frac{3\bm{p}_{b}[i]\ln(2/\alpha)}{\lambda m}}\right]\geq 1-\alpha\text{.}

Since,
X_{j}\leq O(2^{j})
where
j\leq k=O(\log(1/\varepsilon\lambda))
, we can set
\alpha:=1/1000|X|
and get that this inequality holds for all
i\in X_{j}
by a union bound with probability
1-\frac{1}{1000k}
. Again by a union over all
j\leq k
, we get that this holds for all
j
with probability
0.999
. Then, recall that from
7.7
, for
i\in X_{j}
, we have
\bm{p}_{b}[i]\leq\frac{10002^{j}\log(1/\varepsilon\lambda)}{n}
for all
i\in X_{j}
. Then, using and
m=O(\frac{n\log^{2}(1/\varepsilon\lambda)}{\varepsilon^{2}\lambda})
(the sample complexity used in line (1) of the tester), we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{P_{b}[i]}{\lambda m}-\bm{p}_{b}[i]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{3\bm{p}_{b}[i]\ln(2/\alpha)}{\lambda m}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{3000\cdot 2^{j}\log^{2}(1/\varepsilon\lambda)}{\lambda n\cdot m}}\leq\frac{\varepsilon 2^{j}}{200n}\text{.}

(34)

Note that last inequality holds as long as
m\geq\frac{Cn\log^{2}(1/\varepsilon\lambda)}{\varepsilon^{2}\lambda 2^{j}}
for a sufficiently large constant
C
. This is of course maximized when
j=1
, and so this bound is satisfied for the choice of
m
in the tester (line (1)).
Similarly, by
Theorem

7.1
we have for each
i\in[n]

\Pr_{Z}\left[\left|\frac{R_{b}[i]}{m}-\bm{r}_{b}[i]\right|\leq\sqrt{\frac{3\bm{r}_{b}[i]\ln(2/\alpha)}{m}}\right]\geq 1-\alpha

and by a union bound with probability
0.999
this holds for every
i\in X_{1}\cup\cdots\cup X_{k}
. Then, using our bound
\left\lVert\bm{r}_{b}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\frac{4}{n}
and
m\geq\frac{Cn\log^{2}(1/\varepsilon\lambda)}{\varepsilon^{2}\lambda}
yields

\left|\frac{R_{b}[i]}{m}-\bm{r}_{b}[i]\right|\leq\sqrt{\frac{3\bm{r}_{b}[i]\ln(2/\alpha)}{m}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{12\ln(2/\alpha)}{nm}}\leq\frac{\varepsilon\sqrt{\lambda}}{200n}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{200n}\text{.}

Taking a union bound over both inequalities for both
b\in\{1,2\}
completes the proof. ∎

8
Query-Optimal Uniformity Testing with Adversarial Contamination

Note that the closeness tester established in
Section

7
implies a uniformity tester with the same sample and query complexity which works against distributional contamination. In this section, we show that for uniformity testing we can achieve similar query complexity even against
adversarial
contamination.
That is, with probability
\lambda
the sample is drawn according to the target distribution
\bm{p}
, and with probability
1-\lambda
the sample is chosen by an adversary. Moreover, all of the adversarial samples can be chosen after all of the
\bm{p}
-samples are fixed. We prove the following theorem.

Theorem 8.1
.

For all
\lambda,\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, there is a
\lambda
-adversarially robust
\varepsilon
-uniformity tester using

O\left(\frac{n\log(1/\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon^{2}\lambda}\right)\text{ samples and }O\left(\frac{\log^{4}(1/\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon^{2}\lambda}\right)\text{ verification queries.}

In the rest of the section, we use
\bm{r}_{A,\bm{p},\lambda}
to denote an oracle, which when sampled, produces a sample from
\bm{p}
with probability
\lambda
, and produces an adversarially chosen element of
[n]
with probability
1-\lambda
.

8.1
Preliminaries

Before proving
Theorem

8.1
, we provide the needed claims for estimating coin bias.

Claim 8.2
.

Let
\rho\in(\beta,1)
be an unknown coin probability, where
\beta
is known. Then
O(\frac{\ln(1/\gamma)}{\beta\delta^{2}})
i.i.d.
\mathrm{Bern}(\rho)
trials suffice to attain a
(1\pm\delta)
-approximation of
\rho
with probability
1-\gamma
.

Proof.

Consider taking
m
i.i.d. trials
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
and let
X=\sum_{j\leq m}X_{j}
and
\mu:=\mathbb{E}[X]=\rho m
. The output of the algorithm will be
X/m
. By the Chernoff bound,

\Pr[X/m\notin[(1\pm\delta)\rho m]]=\Pr[|X-\mu|>\delta\mu]\leq 2\exp(-\delta^{2}(\rho m)/3)

and the RHS is at most
\gamma
when
m=\frac{50\ln(1/\gamma)}{\beta\delta^{2}}
. ∎

Claim 8.3
.

Let
\rho\in(0,1)
be an unknown coin probability, and let
\beta
be known. Then
O(\frac{\ln(1/\gamma)}{\beta})
i.i.d.
\mathrm{Bern}(\rho)
trials suffice to distinguish
\rho\geq\beta
from
\rho<\beta/8
with probability
1-\gamma
.

Proof.

Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{m}
be i.i.d.
\mathrm{Bern}(\rho)
trials and let
X
denote their sum. Then
\mu:=\mathbb{E}[X]=\rho m
. We will conclude that
\rho=\beta
if
X>\beta m/2
. Otherwise we will conclude that
\rho<\beta/8
. If
\rho\geq\beta
it suffices to attain a constant factor approximation of
\rho
using
O(\frac{\ln(1/\gamma)}{\beta})
trials with
8.2
. Now suppose
\rho<\beta/8
. We will use the following statement of the Chernoff bound: for any
\delta>0
, we have

\Pr[X>\mu+\delta\mu]\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\delta^{2}\mu}{2+\delta}\right)

In particular, if
\rho<\beta/8
, then

\Pr[X>\beta m/2]\leq\Pr[X>\rho m+(3\beta/8\rho)\rho m]\leq\exp\left(-\frac{(3\beta/8\rho)^{2}\cdot\rho m}{2+(3\beta/8\rho)}\right)\text{.}

Since
\rho<\beta/8
, we have
3\beta/8\rho>3
and so the RHS above is at most

\exp\left(-\frac{(3\beta/8\rho)^{2}\cdot\rho m}{2\cdot(3\beta/8\rho)}\right)=\exp\left(-\frac{3\beta m}{16}\right)\leq\gamma

for
m\geq c\ln(1/\gamma)/\beta
for sufficiently large constant
c
. ∎

Claim 8.4

(
(\beta,\delta,\gamma)
-Bias Estimation)
.

Let
\rho\in(0,1)
be an unknown coin probability, and let
\beta\in(0,1)
. Then there is an algorithm using
O(\frac{\ln(1/\gamma)}{\beta\delta^{2}})
i.i.d.
\mathrm{Bern}(\rho)
trials with the following guarantee.

1.

If
|\rho-\beta|<\delta\beta/2
, then the algorithm outputs "close" with probability at least
1-\gamma
.

2.

If
|\rho-\beta|>\delta\beta
, then the algorithm outputs "far" with probability
1-\gamma
.

Proof.

Take
m=O(\frac{\ln(1/\gamma)}{\beta\delta^{2}}+\frac{\ln(1/\gamma)}{\beta})
samples where the first term is enough to attain a
(1\pm\delta)
multiplicative approximation of
\rho
when
\rho>\beta/8
using
8.2
and the second term is enough to detect when
\rho<\beta/8

8.3
(this does not require any lower bound on
\rho
). In both cases the empirical estimate is enough to distinguish the two cases with probability
1-\gamma
. ∎

Additionally, we will use the following corollary of
Theorem

7.1
for adversarial mixtures.

Corollary 8.5
.

Let
X\subseteq[n]
and let
\bm{p}\colon[n]\to[0,1]
be an arbitrary distribution. Consider a multi-set
Z
of
m=O(\frac{n\ln|X|}{\lambda\varepsilon^{2}})
samples drawn from
\bm{r}_{A,\bm{p},\lambda}
. Let
P[i]
denote the number of samples which were drawn by
\bm{p}
and which occurred on element
i
. Then,

\Pr_{Z}\left[\forall i\in X\colon\left|\frac{P[i]}{m}-\lambda\bm{p}[i]\right|\leq\frac{\varepsilon\lambda}{100}\sqrt{\frac{\bm{p}[i]}{n}}\right]\geq 0.99\text{.}

Proof.

Consider a distribution
\bm{p}_{\lambda}\colon[n]\cup\{A\}\to[0,1]
where
\bm{p}_{\lambda}[i]=\lambda\bm{p}[i]
and
\bm{p}_{\lambda}(A)=1-\lambda
. For
i\in[n]
, the random variable
P[i]/m
is equivalent to an
m
-sample empirical estimate of
\bm{p}_{\lambda}[i]=\lambda\bm{p}[i]
. Therefore, by
Theorem

7.1
we have

\Pr_{Z}\left[\left|\frac{P[i]}{m}-\lambda\bm{p}[i]\right|>\sqrt{\frac{3\lambda\bm{p}[i]\ln(2/\alpha)}{m}}\right]\leq\alpha

and so setting
m=\frac{Cn\ln|X|}{\lambda\varepsilon^{2}}
for sufficiently large constant
C>0
,
\alpha=1/(100|X|)
, and taking a union bound completes the proof. ∎

8.2
Query-Optimal Uniformity Testing: Proof of
Theorem

8.1

The algorithm is defined as follows. Throughout the algorithm’s description, let
\gamma=\varepsilon^{2}/1000
. Using
Lemma

3.16
, we first obtain an estimate
\hat{\lambda}
such that
\hat{\lambda}\in[\lambda/2,\lambda]
using
O(1/\lambda)
samples and queries which we use in place of
\lambda
throughout the algorithm’s description.

1.

(Sampling Phase.)
Draw a multi-set
Z
of
m=O(\frac{n\ln(1/\varepsilon)}{\lambda\varepsilon^{2}})
samples from
\bm{r}_{A,\bm{p},\lambda}
. Let
Z_{i}
denote the set of samples occurring on element
i
. Let
P[i]
and
A[i]
denote the number of such samples generated by
\bm{p}
and by the adversary, respectively, and let
R[i]=P[i]+A[i]
.

(Note:
P[i],A[i]
are not known to the tester, but
R[i]
is.)

2.

(Selecting Test Elements.)
Draw a set
X_{\bm{p}}
of
|X_{\bm{p}}|=\frac{100}{\varepsilon}
samples from
\bm{p}
.

(This can be done using
O(\frac{1}{\lambda\varepsilon})
samples and queries with high probability.)

For each
j\leq\lceil\log(4/\varepsilon)\rceil=:k
, draw a set
X_{U,j}
of
|X_{U,j}|=10\cdot 2^{j}\log(1/\varepsilon)
samples drawn from
U_{n}
.

(This requires no queries.)

Let
X=X_{\bm{p}}\cup X_{U,1}\cup\cdots\cup X_{U,k}
.

3.

(Coin Definition.)
For each
i\in X
, let
\rho[i]:=\frac{P[i]}{R[i]}
.

(Using one query to a uniform random sample in
Z_{i}
, we have access to a
\rho[i]
-probability coin. Recall that the denominator
R[i]
is known to the tester.)

4.

(Query Phase 1.)
For each
i\in X_{\bm{p}}
:

(a)

Invoke
8.3
with
\beta=\frac{\lambda\varepsilon}{100}
and error probability
\gamma
on
\rho[i]
using queries to uniform random elements of
Z_{i}
. If it returns "
\rho[i]<\beta/8
", then skip the next step and continue the for-loop.

(b)

Invoke
8.2
with
\beta=\frac{\lambda\varepsilon}{20},\delta=\frac{1}{200}
on
\rho[i]
using queries to uniform random elements of
Z_{i}
to obtain a multiplicative estimate
\widetilde{\rho}[i]
satisfying
(1-\frac{1}{200})\rho[i]\leq\widetilde{\rho}[i]\leq(1+\frac{1}{200})\rho[i]
with probability
1-\gamma
. If
\widetilde{\rho}[i]>\frac{3\lambda m}{2R[i]n}
, then
reject
.

5.

(Query Phase 2.)
For
j\leq k
and
i\in X_{U,j}
:

(a)

If
\frac{R[i]}{m}>\frac{2^{j}\log(1/\varepsilon)}{n}
, then skip the next step and continue the for-loop.

(b)

Invoke
(\beta,\delta,\gamma)
-bias estimation (
8.4
) with
\beta=\frac{\lambda m}{R[i]n},\delta=\frac{\varepsilon 2^{j}}{100}
on
\rho[i]
, using queries to uniform random elements of
Z_{i}
. If this outputs "far", then reject. Otherwise continue.

6.

Accept
.

Sample and query complexity.

All samples from
\bm{r}_{A,\bm{p},\lambda}
are drawn in steps (1) and (2), which use
O(\frac{n\ln(1/\varepsilon)}{\lambda\varepsilon^{2}})
and
O(\frac{1}{\lambda\varepsilon})
samples respectively.

We now analyze the number of queries. Step (2) makes
O(\frac{1}{\lambda\varepsilon})
queries. By
8.3
, step (4a) uses
O(\frac{\ln(1/\varepsilon)}{\lambda\varepsilon})
queries. By
8.2
, step (4b) then also uses
O(\frac{\ln(1/\varepsilon)}{\lambda\varepsilon})
queries. Thus, given that the set
X_{\bm{p}}
drawn in step (2) contains
O(1/\varepsilon)
elements, the total number of queries made in step (4) is bounded by
O(\frac{\log(1/\varepsilon)}{\lambda\varepsilon^{2}})
.

We now analyze the number of queries made in step (5). Step (5a) makes no queries. Now note that by virtue of step (5a), step (5b) is only ever invoked with
\beta=\frac{\lambda m}{R[i]n}\geq\frac{\lambda}{2^{j+1}\log(1/\varepsilon)}
. Thus, using
8.4
and
\delta=\frac{\varepsilon 2^{j}}{100}
, the number of queries made by an iteration of step (5b) is at most

O\left(\frac{\ln(1/\gamma)}{\beta\delta^{2}}\right)=O\left(\frac{\log^{2}(1/\varepsilon)}{2^{j}\lambda\varepsilon^{2}}\right)\text{.}

Now, since the set
X_{U,j}
drawn in step (2) is of size
O(2^{j}\log(1/\varepsilon))
, the total number of queries made across all iterations of step (5b) for a fixed
j
is bounded by
O(\frac{\log^{3}(1/\varepsilon)}{\lambda\varepsilon^{2}})
. Then, summing over all
j\leq k=O(\log(1/\varepsilon))
, we see that the query complexity of the tester is bounded by
O(\frac{\log^{4}(1/\varepsilon)}{\lambda\varepsilon^{2}})
, as claimed.

Tester analysis.

First, by
Corollary

7.2
, since
|X|\leq O(1/\varepsilon^{2})
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr_{Z,X}\left[\forall i\in X\colon\left|\frac{P[i]}{m}-\lambda\bm{p}[i]\right|\leq\frac{\lambda\varepsilon}{100}\sqrt{\frac{\bm{p}[i]}{n}}\right]\geq 0.99\text{.}

(35)

We will denote the event inside the above probability by
\mathcal{E}_{X,Z}
and will henceforth assume this holds for
X
and
Z
. Note that the tester does not have knowledge of what
P[i]/m
is, but it is a well-defined quantity given a fixed
Z
. Now, note that using
\rho[i]:=P[i]/R[i]
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{X,Z}~\implies~\forall i\in X\colon\left|\rho[i]-\frac{\lambda\bm{p}[i]m}{R[i]}\right|\leq\frac{\lambda m\varepsilon}{100R[i]}\sqrt{\frac{\bm{p}[i]}{n}}\text{.}

(36)

Throughout the rest of the analysis we will crucially rely on the condition that the inequality in the RHS of
eq.

36
holds.

Completeness.

Suppose that
\bm{p}
is uniform. Then for every
i\in X
we have
\bm{p}[i]=1/n
, and conditioning on
\mathcal{E}_{X,Z}
implies

\displaystyle\left|\rho[i]-\frac{\lambda m}{R[i]n}\right|\leq\frac{\lambda m\varepsilon}{100R[i]n}~\implies~\frac{\lambda m}{R[i]n}\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{100}\right)\leq\rho[i]\leq\frac{\lambda m}{R[i]n}\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{100}\right)\text{.}

(37)

Therefore by
8.2
, the estimate
\widetilde{\rho}[i]
produced in step (4b) will satisfy
\widetilde{\rho}[i]\leq\frac{3\lambda m}{2R[i]n}
with probability at least
1-\gamma
. Similarly, each iteration of step (5b) will output "close" with probability at least
1-\gamma
. Thus, each of these steps reject with probability at most
\gamma
. Step (4b) is invoked
100/\varepsilon
times and step (5b) is invoked
10k\log(1/\varepsilon)\sum_{j=1}^{k}2^{j}\leq\frac{10\log^{2}(1/\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}\leq\frac{100}{\varepsilon^{2}}
. Thus, by a union bound, the probability that any of these steps reject is at most
\frac{200}{\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot\gamma\leq\frac{1}{5}
. Therefore, the tester accepts in this case with probability at least
0.99\cdot\frac{4}{5}>\frac{2}{3}
, as claimed.

Soundness.

Now suppose that
\bm{p}
is
\varepsilon
-far from uniform, and let
B=\{i\in[n]\colon|\bm{p}[i]-1/n|>\varepsilon/2n\}
. Further partition
B:=B_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup B_{k}
where
k=\lceil\log(4/\varepsilon)\rceil
and

B_{j}=\{i\in B\colon 2^{j}(\varepsilon/4n)\leq|\bm{p}[i]-1/n|<2^{j+1}(\varepsilon/4n)\}\text{ for }j<k\text{, and }B_{k}:=\{i\in B\colon\bm{p}[i]\geq 2/n\}\text{.}

The following is the main lemma for the analysis.

Lemma 8.6
.

If
\left\lVert\bm{p}-U_{n}\right\rVert_{1}>\varepsilon
, then one of the two following items hold.

1.

\Pr_{i\sim\bm{p}}\left[\bm{p}[i]>\frac{2}{n}\text{ and }R[i]/m\leq\frac{20}{\varepsilon}\bm{p}[i]\right]\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{8}
.

2.

For some
j^{\ast}<k
,
\Pr_{i\sim U_{n}}\left[i\in B_{j^{\ast}}\text{ and }R[i]/m\leq\frac{2^{j^{\ast}}\log(1/\varepsilon)}{n}\right]\geq\frac{1}{2\cdot 2^{j^{\ast}}\log(1/\varepsilon)}
.

We defer the proof of
Lemma

8.6
to
Section

8.3
. We now complete the tester analysis and the proof of
Theorem

8.1
using
Lemma

8.6
.

Case 1:

Suppose item (1) of
Lemma

8.6
holds. We will argue that some iteration of step (4b) will reject with high probability. Suppose
i\in X
satisfies
\bm{p}[i]>2/n
and
R[i]/m\leq\frac{20}{\varepsilon}\bm{p}[i]
. This implies that

\rho[i]\geq\frac{\lambda\bm{p}[i]m}{R[i]}\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{100}\sqrt{\frac{1}{\bm{p}[i]n}}\right)\geq\frac{\lambda\varepsilon}{20}\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{100}\right)\geq\frac{\lambda\varepsilon}{30}

implying that such an
i
will pass the check in step (4a) and proceed to step (4b), in which we obtain an estimate
\widetilde{\rho}[i]
which by
8.2
satisfies

\widetilde{\rho}[i]\geq\left(1-\frac{1}{200}\right)\rho[i]\geq\left(1-\frac{1}{200}\right)\frac{\lambda\bm{p}[i]m}{R[i]}\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{100}\right)>\frac{3\lambda m}{2R[i]n}\text{~(since }\bm{p}[i]>2/n\text{)}

with probability at least
1-\gamma
, causing the tester to reject.

Now, since we are assuming item (1) of
Lemma

8.6
holds, a sample from
\bm{p}
yields such an element with probability at least
\varepsilon/8
. Therefore, the set
X_{\bm{p}}
drawn in step (2) contains such an element with probability at least
1-(1-\varepsilon/8)^{100/\varepsilon}\geq 0.99
. Thus, the rejection probability in this case is at least
(0.99)^{2}\cdot(1-\gamma)\gg 2/3
.

Case 2.

Suppose item (2) of
Lemma

8.6
holds. We will argue that some iteration of step (5b) with
j=j^{\ast}
will reject with high probability. Suppose
i\in B_{j^{\ast}}
and
R[i]/m\leq\frac{2^{j^{\ast}}\log(1/\varepsilon)}{n}
. First, by the bound on
R[i]/m
, this
i
passes the check in step (5a) and thus continues to step (5b). Now recall that
i\in B_{j^{\ast}}
means that

\frac{\varepsilon 2^{j^{\ast}}}{4n}\leq\left|\bm{p}[i]-\frac{1}{n}\right|\leq\frac{\varepsilon 2^{j^{\ast}+1}}{4n}

and so we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\lambda\bm{p}[i]m}{R[i]}-\frac{\lambda m}{R[i]n}\right|=\frac{\lambda m}{R[i]}\left|\bm{p}[i]-\frac{1}{n}\right|\geq\frac{\lambda m}{R[i]}\cdot\frac{\varepsilon 2^{j^{\ast}}}{4n}

(38)

and using our conditioning on
\mathcal{E}_{Z,X}
yields

\displaystyle\bm{p}[i]\leq\frac{1}{n}\left(1+\varepsilon 2^{j^{\ast}-1}{}\right)~\implies~\left|\rho[i]-\frac{\lambda\bm{p}[i]m}{R[i]}\right|\leq\frac{\lambda\varepsilon m}{100R[i]}\sqrt{\frac{\bm{p}[i]}{n}}\leq\frac{\lambda\varepsilon m}{100R[i]n}\sqrt{1+\varepsilon 2^{j^{\ast}-1}}\leq\frac{\lambda\varepsilon m}{100R[i]n}\text{.}

(39)

Combining the above and using the triangle inequality yields

\displaystyle\left|\rho[i]-\frac{\lambda m}{R[i]n}\right|\geq\left|\frac{\lambda\bm{p}[i]m}{R[i]}-\frac{\lambda m}{R[i]n}\right|-\left|\rho[i]-\frac{\lambda\bm{p}[i]m}{R[i]}\right|\geq\frac{\lambda\varepsilon\cdot m2^{j^{\ast}}}{4R[i]n}-\frac{\lambda\varepsilon m}{100R[i]n}\geq\frac{\lambda\varepsilon\cdot m2^{j^{\ast}}}{10R[i]n}

(40)

and therefore the call to
8.4
in step (5b) (with
\beta=\frac{\lambda m}{R[i]n}
and
\delta=\frac{\varepsilon 2^{j^{\ast}}}{100}
) for this
i
outputs "far" with probability at least
1-\gamma
.

Finally, since we are assume item (2) of
Lemma

8.6
holds, a uniform random sample from
[n]
yields such an
i
with probability at least
\frac{1}{2^{j^{\ast}+1}\log(1/\varepsilon)}
. Thus, the set
X_{U,j^{\ast}}
drawn in step (2) contains such an element
i
with probability at least

1-\left(1-\frac{1}{2^{j^{\ast}+1}\log(1/\varepsilon)}\right)^{10\cdot 2^{j^{\ast}}\log(1/\varepsilon)}\geq 0.99

Therefore, the overall rejection probability in this case is at least
(0.99)^{2}\cdot(1-\gamma)\gg 2/3
. This completes the proof of
Theorem

8.1
.

8.3
Proof of
Lemma

8.6

Proof.

First, observe that

\varepsilon\leq\sum_{i}\big|\bm{p}[i]-1/n\big|\leq|\overline{B}|\cdot\varepsilon/2n+\sum_{i\in B}|\bm{p}[i]-1/n|\leq\varepsilon/2+\sum_{i\in B}|\bm{p}[i]-1/n|~\implies~\sum_{i\in B}|\bm{p}[i]-1/n|>\varepsilon/2

Now, using the bucketing, we have

\displaystyle\varepsilon/2<\sum_{i\in B}|\bm{p}[i]-1/n|=\sum_{i\in B_{k}}|\bm{p}[i]-1/n|+\sum_{i\in B_{1}\cup\cdots\cup B_{k-1}}|\bm{p}[i]-1/n|\leq\sum_{i\in B_{k}}\bm{p}[i]+\sum_{j<k}|B_{j}|2^{j}(\varepsilon/4n)

(41)

Case 1:

Suppose the first term in the RHS of
eq.

41
is at least
\varepsilon/4
. This implies

\displaystyle\Pr_{i\sim\bm{p}}[\bm{p}[i]>2/n]\geq\varepsilon/4\text{.}

(42)

Since we are conditioning on
\mathcal{E}_{X,Z}
, we have

\displaystyle\bm{p}[i]>2/n~\implies~\frac{P[i]}{m}\leq\lambda\bm{p}[i]+\frac{\lambda\varepsilon}{100}\sqrt{\frac{\bm{p}[i]}{n}}=\lambda\bm{p}[i]\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{100}\sqrt{\frac{1}{\bm{p}[i]n}}\right)\leq 1.01\cdot\lambda\bm{p}[i]

(43)

and so to show that item (1) holds, it suffices to show that
\Pr_{i\sim\bm{p}}[\bm{p}[i]>2/n\text{ and }A[i]\leq\frac{16}{\lambda\varepsilon}P[i]]\geq\varepsilon/8
. We now show that this holds. To start, note that

\displaystyle\Pr_{i\sim\bm{p}}\left[\bm{p}[i]>2/n\text{ and }A[i]\leq\frac{16}{\lambda\varepsilon}\cdot P[i]\right]

\displaystyle=\Pr_{i\sim\bm{p}}[\bm{p}[i]>2/n]\cdot\Pr_{i\sim\bm{p}}\left[A[i]\leq\frac{16}{\lambda\varepsilon}\cdot P[i]~\Big|~\bm{p}[i]>2/n\right]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\cdot\left(1-\Pr_{i\sim\bm{p}}\left[\frac{A[i]}{P[i]}>\frac{16}{\lambda\varepsilon}~\Big|~\bm{p}[i]>2/n\right]\right)

(44)

where we used
eq.

42
. Now, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{i\sim\bm{p}}\left[\frac{A[i]}{P[i]}~\Big|~\bm{p}[i]>2/n\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\leq n}\frac{A[i]}{P[i]}\cdot\Pr_{j\sim\bm{p}}[j=i~|~\bm{p}[i]>2/n]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\leq n}\frac{A[i]}{P[i]}\cdot\frac{\Pr_{j\sim\bm{p}}[j=i\wedge\bm{p}[i]>2/n]}{\Pr_{j\sim\bm{p}}[\bm{p}[j]>2/n]}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{\varepsilon}\cdot\sum_{i\leq n}\frac{A[i]}{P[i]}\cdot\bm{p}[i]\cdot\mathbf{1}(\bm{p}[i]>2/n)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{\varepsilon}\cdot\max_{i\colon\bm{p}[i]>2/n}\frac{\bm{p}[i]}{P[i]}\cdot\sum_{i\leq n}A[i]\leq\frac{4}{\varepsilon}\cdot\max_{i\colon\bm{p}[i]>2/n}\frac{\bm{p}[i]}{P[i]/m}

(45)

where in the last step we used
\sum_{i\leq n}A[i]\leq m
. Now, since
\mathcal{E}_{X,Z}
holds, if
\bm{p}[i]>2/n
, then we have

\frac{\bm{p}[i]}{P[i]/m}=\frac{\bm{p}[i]}{\lambda\bm{p}[i]+((P[i]/m)-\lambda\bm{p}[i])}\leq\frac{\bm{p}[i]}{\lambda\bm{p}[i]-\frac{\lambda\varepsilon}{100}\sqrt{\frac{\bm{p}[i]}{n}}}=\frac{1}{\lambda\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{100}\sqrt{\frac{1}{\bm{p}[i]n}}\right)}\leq\frac{2}{\lambda}

and so plugging this back into
section

8.3
yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{i\sim\bm{p}}\left[\frac{A[i]}{P[i]}~\Big|~\bm{p}[i]>2/n\right]\leq\frac{8}{\lambda\varepsilon}~\implies\Pr_{i\sim\bm{p}}\left[\frac{A[i]}{P[i]}>\frac{16}{\lambda\varepsilon}~\Big|~\bm{p}[i]>2/n\right]\leq\frac{1}{2}~\text{(by Markov's)}\text{.}

(46)

Returning now to
section

8.3
, we have

\Pr_{i\sim\bm{p}}\left[\bm{p}[i]>2/n\text{ and }A[i]\leq\frac{16}{\lambda\varepsilon}\cdot P[i]\right]\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{8}

as claimed.

Case 2:

Suppose the first term in the RHS of
eq.

41
is at most
\varepsilon/4
. Then, we have

\varepsilon/4<\sum_{j<k}|B_{j}|2^{j}(\varepsilon/4n)~\implies~\sum_{j<k}|B_{j}|2^{j}>n

and so there exists
j^{\ast}<k
where

\displaystyle|B_{j^{\ast}}|>\frac{n}{2^{j^{\ast}}\log(1/\varepsilon)}~\Longleftrightarrow~\Pr_{i\sim U_{n}}\left[i\in B_{j^{\ast}}\right]>\frac{1}{2^{j^{\ast}}\log(1/\varepsilon)}\text{.}

(47)

Next, by definition we have
\sum_{i\leq n}R[i]=m
and so
\mathbb{E}_{i\sim U_{n}}[R[i]/m]=1/n
. Thus, Markov’s inequality implies

\Pr_{i\sim U_{n}}\left[R[i]/m>\frac{2\cdot 2^{j^{\ast}}\log(1/\varepsilon)}{n}\right]\leq\frac{1}{2\cdot 2^{j^{\ast}}\log(1/\varepsilon)}

and so combining with
eq.

47
using a union bound obtains

\Pr_{i\sim U_{n}}\left[i\in B_{j^{\ast}}\text{ and }R[i]/m\leq\frac{2\cdot 2^{j^{\ast}}\log(1/\varepsilon)}{n}\right]\geq\frac{1}{2\cdot 2^{j^{\ast}}\log(1/\varepsilon)}

and thus item (2) of the claim holds. This completes the proof. ∎

9
Uniformity Testing with Complete Mixture Knowledge

With
Theorem

1.2
we have obtained an algorithm with a smooth sample-query trade-off, using
O(m)
samples and
O(n/m)
queries.
In this section, we show that when the algorithm is given perfect knowledge of the mixture
\bm{r}
there is an algorithm with near optimal sample and query complexity with respect to the domain size
n
.

Theorem

1.3
shows that knowledge of the mixture is necessary to obtain this sample and query complexity.
Note that while
\bm{r}
is known to the algorithm initially, the contamination parameter
\lambda
remains unknown.

Theorem 9.1

(Formal
Theorem

1.9
)
.

Given an explicit description of the mixture
\bm{r}:=\lambda\bm{p}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}
, there is a
\lambda
-distributionally robust
\varepsilon
-uniformity tester with
\tilde{O}\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon^{9/2}\lambda^{7/2}}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{12}\lambda^{6}}\right)
samples and
O\left(\frac{\log^{7}n}{\alpha^{6}\lambda^{8}}+\frac{\log^{12}n}{\alpha^{12}\lambda^{6}}\right)
queries.

We note that our dependence on
n
is optimal in both sample and query complexity up to polylogarithmic factors.
We leave it as future work to improve the dependence on
\varepsilon,\lambda
.

Algorithm Overview

We begin with a high level overview of our algorithm.
Recall the fundamental sub-routine of
Lemma

4.4
which tests uniformity of
\bm{p}
using
O\left(\frac{nb}{\lambda^{2}\varepsilon^{4}}\right)
queries where
\left\lVert\bm{r}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq b
.
A slight generalization of this algorithm uses
O\left(\frac{b\cdot\mathrm{polylog}(n)}{\mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon\lambda)}\right)
queries if we are instead given the promise
\left\lVert\bm{r}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq b\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
.

To see this, consider the case where
\bm{p}
is near uniform, i.e.
\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{O(1)}{n}
.
Then, we have
\left\lVert\bm{r}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{O(b)}{n}
so that
Lemma

4.4
yields
O\left(\frac{b}{\lambda^{2}\varepsilon^{4}}\right)
queries.
On the other hand, when
\bm{p}
has large
\ell_{2}
-norm, we hope to distinguish the case
\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\geq\alpha
from the case
\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{\alpha}{2}
.
In full generality, we need to repeat this by halving
\alpha
from
O(1)
to
\frac{1}{n}
, and so we can also assume without loss of generality that for example,
\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq 2\alpha
.
Towards this goal, we want to estimate the number of
\bm{p}
-collisions up to accuracy
\ll\lambda^{2}m^{2}\alpha
among the total
m^{2}\left\lVert\bm{r}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=O(m^{2}b\alpha)
collisions.
Using
Lemma

3.13
, the analysis of
Lemma

4.4
shows that this can be done with query complexity

O\left(\left(\frac{m^{2}b\alpha}{\lambda^{2}m^{2}\alpha}\right)^{2}\cdot\frac{\lambda^{2}m^{2}\alpha}{m^{2}b\alpha}\right)=O\left(\frac{b}{\lambda^{2}}\right)\text{.}

With this generalization in hand, we can test the uniformity of
\bm{p}
by partitioning the domain such that we can invoke the above algorithm on each part.
We thus partition the domain into parts where
2^{-k}\leq\bm{r}[i]\leq 2^{-k+1}
for all
k\leq\log n
.
If
\bm{p}
is
\varepsilon_{0}
-far from uniform then it must be
\varepsilon:=\varepsilon_{0}/\log n
-far from uniform on at least one part.
It is easy to see that
\left\lVert\bm{r}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq 2^{-k+1}
on this part of the domain.
To apply the above algorithm, it remains to show that
\bm{p}
if
\varepsilon
-far from uniform on this part,
\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
is large.
Since (1)
\sum_{i}\bm{p}[i]\geq\varepsilon
is large, (2)
\bm{p}[i]\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\bm{r}[i]\leq\frac{2^{-k+1}}{\lambda}
is capped for each individual
i
, and (3) there are at most
2^{k}
buckets
i
in each part, we can show that there are many large
\bm{p}[i]
and in particular obtain a bound
\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\geq\mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon\lambda)2^{-k}
.
In particular,
\left\lVert\bm{r}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}{\mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon\lambda)}
allows us to invoke the above algorithm whose query complexity depends only polylogarithmically on
n
, as
\varepsilon=\varepsilon/\log n
.

Proof.

Assume without loss of generality that
\lambda\leq\frac{1}{2}
.
We begin by obtaining an estimate
\hat{\lambda}
such that
\frac{2}{3}\lambda\leq\hat{\lambda}\leq\lambda
via
Lemma

3.16
.

For any distribution
\bm{r}
, we define
S^{\bm{r}}_{i}=\left\{i\textrm{ s.t. }2^{-i}<\bm{r}[i]\leq 2^{-i+1}\right\}
for
1\leq i\leq\log n
and
