S_{\log n+1}^{\bm{r}}=\left\{i\textrm{ s.t. }\bm{r}[i]\leq\frac{1}{n}\right\}
.
Next, define
\overline{S}^{\bm{r}}_{i}=\bigcup_{j\geq i}S_{j}^{\bm{r}}
and
S^{\bm{r}}_{[i:j]}=\bigcup_{i\leq k\leq j}S_{k}^{\bm{r}}
.
Note that all these sets (without any samples or queries) can be constructed given an explicit description of
\bm{r}
.
The construction of these sets is crucially where we exploit mixture knowledge, and in fact without mixture knowledge these sets cannot be constructed without observing many samples.
We will make heavy use of the following sample bound.

Claim 9.2
.

For all
m\gg\log(1/\delta)
, if
m/\hat{\lambda}
samples are taken from
\bm{r}
, then with probability at least
1-\delta/10
,
\frac{\lambda m}{2\hat{\lambda}}\leq m_{\bm{p}}\leq\frac{2\lambda m}{\hat{\lambda}}
.
In particular,
\frac{m}{2}\leq m_{\bm{p}}\leq 3m
.

Proof.

Note that
\mathbb{E}[m_{\bm{p}}]=m\frac{\lambda}{\hat{\lambda}}\geq m
.
For large enough
C
, a standard concentration bound shows that
\frac{m\lambda}{2\hat{\lambda}}\leq m_{\bm{p}}\leq 2m\frac{\lambda}{\hat{\lambda}}
with high probability whenever
m\gg\log(1/\delta)
.
Then, note that
1\leq\frac{\lambda}{\hat{\lambda}}\leq\frac{3}{2}
.
∎

9.1
Testing Distributions with Heavy Buckets

We begin with a subroutine to distinguish
\bm{p}
with large
\ell_{2}
-norm.
The algorithm at a high level is similar to
Lemma

4.4
(i.e. randomly query collisions to estimate the number of
\bm{p}
-collisions on
S
).

Lemma 9.3
.

Let
1\leq k<\log n+3\log\alpha+\log\hat{\lambda}-3
and
\alpha,\delta>0
.
There is an algorithm making
O\left(\frac{n^{1/2}\log(1/\delta)}{\alpha^{9/2}\lambda^{9/2}}\right)
samples and
O\left(\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{\alpha^{6}\lambda^{8}}\right)
queries satisfying the following guarantees:

1.

(Completeness) if
\bm{p}
is uniform, then the algorithm accepts with probability at least
1-\delta
.

2.

(Soundness) if
\alpha<\sum_{i\in S_{k}^{\bm{r}},\bm{p}[i]>\frac{1}{n}}\bm{p}[i]-\frac{1}{n}
, then the algorithm rejects with probability at least
1-\delta
.

Proof.

Fix
m=\frac{Cn^{1/2}\log(1/\delta)}{\alpha^{9/2}\hat{\lambda}^{7/2}}\geq\frac{Cn^{1/2}\log(1/\delta)}{\alpha^{9/2}\lambda^{7/2}}
for some sufficiently large constant
C
and take
m/\hat{\lambda}
samples.
As before, let
m_{\bm{p}},m_{\bm{q}}
denote the number of samples drawn from
\bm{p},\bm{q}
.
Let
c(\bm{p})
denote the number of
\bm{p}
-collisions among the
m
samples.
For any subset
S\subset[n]
, let
c(\bm{p};S)
denote the number of
\bm{p}
-collisions among the observed samples within buckets in
S
.
Analogously define
c(\bm{q}),c(\bm{r}),c(\bm{q};S),c(\bm{r};S)
.

We prove some properties of the distributions
\bm{p},\bm{q}
on the buckets in
S_{k}^{\bm{r}}
.
We begin with a lower bound on the
2
-norm of
\bm{p}
.

Lemma 9.4
.

In the soundness case,
\left\lVert\bm{p}[S_{k}^{\bm{r}}]\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\geq\alpha^{3}\lambda 2^{-k+1}
and
|S_{k}^{\bm{r}}|\geq\alpha\lambda 2^{k-1}
.

Proof.

Recall that in the soundness case, we have

\sum_{i\in S_{k}^{\bm{r}},\bm{p}[i]>\frac{1}{n}}\bm{p}[i]-\frac{1}{n}>\alpha\text{.}

Let
s
denote the number of
i\in S_{k}^{\bm{r}}
with
\frac{1}{n}<\bm{p}[i]\leq\alpha 2^{-k+1}
.
Let
b
denote the number of remaining elements in
S_{k}^{\bm{r}}
with
\bm{p}[i]>\alpha 2^{-k+1}
.
Then,

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in S_{k}^{r},\bm{p}[i]>\frac{1}{n}}\bm{p}[i]

\displaystyle\leq s\alpha 2^{-k+1}+b2^{-k-2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\alpha+\frac{b}{\lambda}2^{-k+1}

where we have used
s\leq 2^{-k}
as each bucket in
S_{k}^{\bm{r}}
has mass at least
2^{-k}
and
\bm{p}[i]\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\bm{r}[i]\leq\frac{2^{-k+1}}{\lambda}
for
i\in S_{k}^{\bm{r}}
.
On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in S_{k}^{\bm{r}},\bm{p}[i]>\frac{1}{n}}\bm{p}[i]\geq\alpha\text{.}

Combining the above equations, we have

\displaystyle\frac{b}{\lambda}2^{-k+1}

\displaystyle\geq\alpha

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\lambda\alpha}{2^{-k+1}}\text{.}

Thus, there are at least
b
elements in
S_{k}^{\bm{r}}
with
\bm{p}[i]\geq\alpha 2^{-k+1}
.
This allows us to conclude with

\sum_{i\in S_{k}^{\bm{r}}}\bm{p}[i]^{2}\geq\left(\frac{\alpha\lambda}{2^{-k+1}}\right)\left(\alpha^{2}2^{-2k+2}\right)\geq\alpha^{3}\lambda 2^{-k+1}\text{.}

∎

We prove a simple upper bound on the
\ell_{2}
-norm of both
\bm{p},\bm{q}
.

Lemma 9.5
.

\left\lVert\bm{p}[S_{k}^{\bm{r}}]\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{2^{-k+1}}{\lambda}\quad,\quad\left\lVert\bm{q}[S_{k}^{\bm{r}}]\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{2^{-k+1}}{\lambda}
.

Proof.

Note that for each
i\in S_{k}^{\bm{r}}
,
\bm{p}[i]\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\bm{r}[i]\leq\frac{2^{-k+1}}{\lambda}
.
Then, via Holder’s inequality, we have

\left\lVert\bm{p}[S_{k}^{\bm{r}}]\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\left\lVert\bm{p}[S_{k}^{\bm{r}}]\right\rVert_{1}\left\lVert\bm{p}[S_{k}^{\bm{r}}]\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\frac{2^{-k+1}}{\lambda}\text{.}

An identical argument holds for
\bm{q}
.
∎

Lemma 9.6
.

Suppose
m_{\bm{p}}=O\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\alpha^{9/2}\lambda^{7/2}}\right)
for some sufficiently large constant.
Then, the following hold:

1.

If
\bm{p}
is uniform,
c(\bm{p};S_{k}^{\bm{r}})\leq c(\bm{p})\leq\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{1+0.01}{n}
with probability
0.999
.

2.

In the soundness case,
c(\bm{p};S_{k}^{\bm{r}})\geq\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}(0.99\alpha^{3})\lambda 2^{-k+1}\geq\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}(0.99\alpha^{3})\hat{\lambda}2^{-k+1}
with probability
0.999
.

3.

c(\bm{r};S_{k}^{\bm{r}})=O\left(m_{\bm{p}}^{2}2^{-k}\lambda^{-3}\right)
and
O\left(c(\bm{p};S_{k}^{\bm{r}})\right)=O\left(m_{\bm{p}}^{2}2^{-k}\lambda^{-1}\right)
with probability at least
0.999
.

Proof.

Towards
Item

1
and
Item

2
, we note that

\mathbb{E}[c(\bm{p};S_{k}^{\bm{r}})]=\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\left\lVert\bm{p}[S_{k}^{\bm{r}}]\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\quad,\quad\mathrm{Var}(c(\bm{p};S_{k}^{\bm{r}}))\leq m_{\bm{p}}^{2}\left\lVert\bm{p}[S_{k}^{\bm{r}}]\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+m_{\bm{p}}^{3}\left\lVert\bm{p}[S_{k}^{\bm{r}}]\right\rVert_{3}^{3}\text{.}

(48)

Suppose
\bm{p}
is uniform.
Recall that
c(\bm{p};S_{k}^{\bm{r}})\leq c(\bm{p})
since the number of
\bm{p}
-collisions among elements in
S_{k}^{\bm{r}}
is at most the number of
\bm{p}
-collisions.
Then, applying (
1
) we have that
\mathbb{E}[c(\bm{p})]=\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{1}{n}
and
\mathrm{Var}(c(\bm{p}))\leq m_{\bm{p}}^{2}/n
.
Thus,
\sigma:=\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}(c(\bm{p}))}\leq\frac{m_{\bm{p}}}{\sqrt{n}}
.
With Chebyshev’s inequality (see e.g.
4.7
), we obtain that with probability
0.999
,
c(\bm{p})\leq\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{1+0.01}{n}
for
m_{\bm{p}}=O\left(\sqrt{n}\right)
for some sufficiently large constant factor.

Now, consider the soundness case.
From
Lemma

9.4
we have
\left\lVert\bm{p}[S_{k}^{\bm{r}}]\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\geq\alpha^{3}\lambda 2^{-k+1}
.
Thus, the expectation can be bounded as

\mathbb{E}[c(\bm{p};S_{k}^{\bm{r}})]\geq\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\alpha^{3}\lambda 2^{-k+1}\text{.}

To bound the variance, we consider two separate cases.

Case 1:
m_{\bm{p}}^{2}\left\lVert\bm{p}[S_{k}^{\bm{r}}]\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\geq m_{\bm{p}}^{3}\left\lVert\bm{p}[S_{k}^{\bm{r}}]\right\rVert_{3}^{3}

From
Lemma

9.5
we have
\mathrm{Var}(c(\bm{p};S_{k}^{\bm{r}}))\leq 2m_{\bm{p}}^{2}\left\lVert\bm{p}[S_{k}]^{\bm{r}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq 2m_{\bm{p}}^{2}2^{-k+1}\lambda^{-1}
.
In particular,
\sigma\leq 2m_{\bm{p}}2^{-(k-1)/2}\lambda^{-1/2}
.
By Chebyshev’s inequality, observe

\displaystyle\Pr\left(c(\bm{p};S_{k}^{\bm{r}})<\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}(0.99\alpha^{3})\lambda 2^{-k+1}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\left(c(\bm{p};S_{k}^{\bm{r}})<0.99\mathbb{E}\left[c(\bm{p};S_{k}^{\bm{r}})\right]\right)

\displaystyle<O\left(\left(\frac{m_{\bm{p}}2^{-(k-1)/2}\lambda^{-1/2}}{m_{\bm{p}}^{2}\alpha^{3}\lambda 2^{-k+1}}\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle<O\left(\left(\frac{2^{k/2}}{m_{\bm{p}}\alpha^{3}\lambda^{1/2}}\right)^{2}\right)<0.001

for
m_{\bm{p}}=O\left(\frac{2^{k/2}}{\alpha^{3}\lambda}\right)=O\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\alpha^{3/2}\lambda}\right)
since

2^{k/2}=O\left(\sqrt{n}\alpha^{3/2}\right)\text{.}

Case 2:
m_{\bm{p}}^{2}\left\lVert\bm{p}[S_{k}^{\bm{r}}]\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq m_{\bm{p}}^{3}\left\lVert\bm{p}[S_{k}^{\bm{r}}]\right\rVert_{3}^{3}

From
Lemma

9.5
we have
\mathrm{Var}(c(\bm{p};S_{k}^{\bm{r}}))\leq 2m_{\bm{p}}^{3}\left\lVert\bm{p}[S_{k}]^{\bm{r}}\right\rVert_{3}^{3}\leq 2m_{\bm{p}}^{3}\left\lVert\bm{p}[S_{k}]^{\bm{r}}\right\rVert_{3}^{3}\leq 2m_{\bm{p}}^{3}2^{-1.5k+1.5}\lambda^{-3/2}
.
In particular,
\sigma\leq 2m_{\bm{p}}^{3/2}2^{-0.75k+0.75}\lambda^{-0.75}
.
By Chebyshev’s inequality, observe

\displaystyle\Pr\left(c(\bm{p};S_{k}^{\bm{r}})<\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}(0.99\alpha^{3})\lambda 2^{-k+1}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\left(c(\bm{p};S_{k}^{\bm{r}})<0.99\mathbb{E}\left[c(\bm{p};S_{k}^{\bm{r}})\right]\right)

\displaystyle=O\left(\left(\frac{m_{\bm{p}}^{3/2}2^{-0.75k+0.75}\lambda^{-0.75}}{m_{\bm{p}}^{2}\alpha^{3}\lambda 2^{-k+1}}\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=O\left(\left(\frac{2^{k/4}}{m_{\bm{p}}^{1/2}\alpha^{3}\lambda^{7/4}}\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{2^{k/2}}{m_{\bm{p}}\alpha^{6}\lambda^{7/2}}\right)<0.001

for
m_{\bm{p}}=O\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\alpha^{9/2}\lambda^{7/2}}\right)
.
Since
\hat{\lambda}\leq\lambda
, the same bound holds if the threshold is defined using our estimate
\hat{\lambda}
instead.

Finally, towards
Item

3
we apply Markov’s inequality we bound the total number of collisions by

\displaystyle O\left(c(\bm{r};S_{k}^{\bm{r}})\right)

\displaystyle=O\left(m^{2}\lambda^{-2}\left\lVert\bm{r}[S_{k}^{\bm{r}}]\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)=O\left(m^{2}2^{-k}\lambda^{-1}\right)=O\left(m_{\bm{p}}^{2}2^{-k}\lambda^{-3}\right)

\displaystyle O\left(c(\bm{p};S_{k}^{\bm{r}})\right)

\displaystyle=O\left(m^{2}\left\lVert\bm{r}[S_{k}^{\bm{r}}]\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)=O\left(m^{2}2^{-k}\lambda^{-1}\right)=O\left(m_{\bm{p}}^{2}2^{-k}\lambda^{-1}\right)\text{.}

∎

We now describe analogous sub-routines to
4.6
and
4.7
.

Claim 9.7
.

With
O\left(\lambda^{-2}\right)
queries, there is an algorithm that with probability
0.99
computes an estimate
\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}
such that

\left|\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}-\binom{\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}}{2}\right|\alpha^{3}\hat{\lambda}2^{-k+1}\leq 0.01\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\alpha^{3}\hat{\lambda}2^{-k+1}\text{.}

Proof.

We compute

\left|\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}-\binom{\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}}{2}\right|\leq\frac{1}{2}\left|m_{\bm{p}}^{2}+m_{\bm{p}}-\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}^{2}+\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}\right|\leq(m_{\bm{p}}+\hat{m}_{\bm{p}})|m_{\bm{p}}-\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}|=O\left(m|m_{\bm{p}}-\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}|\right)\text{.}

In the final inequality, we have used
m_{\bm{p}},\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}=O(m)
from
9.2
.
Since
\frac{m_{\bm{p}}^{2}}{4}\leq\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}
, it suffices to obtain an estimate such that
|m_{\bm{p}}-\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}|\ll\frac{m_{\bm{p}}^{2}}{m}\ll m_{\bm{p}}
.
We can do this by randomly choosing samples and querying them, thus accessing a coin with bias
\frac{m_{\bm{p}}}{m/\hat{\lambda}}
which we must estimate with accuracy
\ll\frac{m_{\bm{p}}}{m/\hat{\lambda}}\ll\lambda
.
The query bound follows from an application of
Lemma

3.13
.
∎

Claim 9.8
.

There exists a universal constant
C>0
such that if
m_{\bm{p}}\geq\frac{C\sqrt{n}}{\alpha^{9/2}\lambda^{7/2}}
there is an algorithm that uses
O\left(\frac{1}{\alpha^{6}\lambda^{8}}\right)
queries, and with probability
0.99
computes an estimate
\hat{c}(\bm{p};S_{k}^{\bm{r}})
such that the following hold:

1.

If
\bm{p}
is uniform,

\hat{c}(\bm{p};S_{k}^{\bm{r}})\leq\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}0.2\alpha^{3}\lambda 2^{-k+1}\leq\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}0.3\alpha^{3}\hat{\lambda}2^{-k+1}\text{.}

2.

In the soundness case,

\hat{c}(\bm{p};S_{k}^{\bm{r}})\geq\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}0.7\alpha^{3}\hat{\lambda}2^{-k+1}\text{.}

Proof of
9.8
.

Note that
0.2\alpha^{3}\hat{\lambda}2^{-k+1}\geq\frac{1+0.01}{n}
as long as
k<\log n+3\log\alpha+\log\hat{\lambda}-3
, which holds by assumption.
Thus, in both cases, following
Lemma

9.6
it suffices to estimate
c(\bm{p};S_{k}^{\bm{r}})
up to error additive error
\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{\alpha^{3}\hat{\lambda}2^{-k+1}}{10}
using queries.
Following similar arguments to
4.7
, this requires query complexity

O\left(\frac{2^{2k}c(\bm{r};S_{k}^{\bm{r}})^{2}}{m_{\bm{p}}^{4}\alpha^{6}\hat{\lambda}^{2}}\cdot\frac{c(\bm{p};S_{k}^{\bm{r}})}{c(\bm{r};S_{k}^{\bm{r}})}\right)=O\left(\frac{2^{2k}c(\bm{r};S_{k}^{\bm{r}})c(\bm{p};S_{k}^{\bm{r}})}{m_{\bm{p}}^{4}\alpha^{6}\hat{\lambda}^{2}}\right)=O\left(\frac{1}{\alpha^{6}\lambda^{8}}\right)\text{.}

In the last inequality, we have used
Item

3
to bound both
c(\bm{r};S_{k}^{\bm{r}})
and
c(\bm{p};S_{k}^{\bm{r}})
.
∎

We now present our algorithm.

1.

Let
m:=\frac{Cn^{1/2}}{\alpha^{9/2}\hat{\lambda}^{7/2}}
.

2.

Repeat the following for
1\leq t\leq T=C\log(1/\delta)
.

(a)

Draw
\frac{m}{\hat{\lambda}}
fresh samples from
\bm{r}
.

(b)

Compute
\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}
using
9.7
.

(c)

Compute
\hat{c}\left(\bm{p};S_{k}^{\bm{r}}\right)
using
9.8
.

(d)

Let
Z_{t}\leftarrow\mathbbm{1}\left[\hat{c}\left(\bm{p};S_{k}^{\bm{r}}\right)<\binom{\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}}{2}0.5\alpha^{3}\hat{\lambda}2^{-k+1}\right]
.

3.

If
\frac{1}{T}\sum Z_{t}>\frac{1}{2}
, accept. Otherwise, reject.

Fix an iteration
t
.
By
9.2
, we have that
m_{\bm{p}}\geq 0.99m
so that
Lemma

9.6
applies.
If
\bm{p}
is uniform, then we claim
Z_{t}=0
with probability at most
0.1
by a union bound.
To see this, we may union bound over the correctness of the estimates
\hat{m}_{\bm{p}},\hat{c}(\bm{p};S_{k}^{\bm{r}})
and obtain

\displaystyle\binom{\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}}{2}0.5\alpha^{3}\hat{\lambda}2^{-k+1}-\hat{c}(\bm{p};S_{k}^{\bm{r}})

\displaystyle\geq\left(\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}0.5\alpha^{3}\hat{\lambda}2^{-k+1}-\hat{c}(\bm{p};S_{k}^{\bm{r}})\right)-\left|\binom{\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}}{2}-\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\right|0.5\alpha^{3}\hat{\lambda}2^{-k+1}

\displaystyle\geq\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}0.2\alpha^{3}\hat{\lambda}2^{-k+1}-0.01\cdot\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\alpha^{3}\hat{\lambda}2^{-k+1}

\displaystyle>0\text{.}

Similarly, in the soundness case we have
Z_{t}=1
with probability at most
0.1
.
Then, by taking a majority vote, we observe that the algorithm fails with probability
e^{-\Omega(T)}<\delta
for
T=O(\log(1/\delta))
.
The sample and query complexities are immediate from the algorithm.
∎

9.2
Testing Distributions with Light Buckets

The next sub-routine we need is an algorithm that can detect if
\bm{p}
is far from uniform for some
k>\log n+3\log\alpha+\log\hat{\lambda}-3
.

Lemma 9.9
.

Let
\alpha>0
and
k_{0}=\log n+3\log\alpha+\log\hat{\lambda}-3
and let
S=\overline{S_{k_{0}}^{\bm{r}}}
.

There is an algorithm using
O\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\alpha^{4}\lambda^{2}}\right)
samples and
O\left(\frac{1}{\alpha^{12}\lambda^{6}}\right)
queries satisfying the following:

1.

(Completeness) if
\bm{p}
is uniform, then the algorithm accepts with probability at least
0.999
.

2.

(Soundness) if
\alpha<\sum_{i\in S,\bm{p}[i]>1/n}p_{i}-\frac{1}{n}
, then the algorithm rejects with probability at least
0.999
.

Proof.

Since, all elements are in
\overline{S_{k}^{\bm{r}}}
,
\left\lVert\bm{r}[S]\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\left\lVert\bm{r}[S]\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\frac{16}{n\alpha^{3}\hat{\lambda}}
.
For all
i
, write
\bm{p}[i]=\frac{1}{n}+a[i]
for some vector
a
.
Then, we have for all
i\in S
that
\bm{p}[i]\leq\frac{\bm{r}[i]}{\lambda}\leq\frac{16}{n\alpha^{3}\hat{\lambda}^{2}}
so that
a[i]\leq\frac{16}{n\alpha^{3}\hat{\lambda}^{2}}
.

We begin with two simplifying assumptions.

Claim 9.10
.

In the soundness case,
|S|\geq\frac{n\alpha^{4}\hat{\lambda}^{2}}{16}
and
\bm{p}[S]\geq\alpha
.
Furthermore, there is an algorithm with
O\left(\log(1/\delta)\alpha^{-2}\right)
queries that outputs accept with probability
1-\delta
if either
|S|<\frac{n\alpha^{4}\hat{\lambda}}{16}
or
\bm{p}[S]<\alpha/2
.

Proof.

In the soundness case,
\sum_{i\in S}a[i]\geq\alpha
implies
|S|\geq\frac{\alpha}{\max_{i}a[i]}\geq\frac{n\alpha^{4}\hat{\lambda}^{2}}{16}
.
Similarly,
\bm{p}[S]\geq\bm{p}[S^{+}]\geq\alpha
.

An algorithm can check for the size of
S
directly since we are given
S
via the mixture
\bm{r}
.
Similarly, we can estimate
\bm{p}[S]
up to additive error
\alpha/6
with
O\left(\alpha^{-2}\right)
samples and queries via
Lemma

3.13
, and output accept if this estimate is at most
\frac{2\alpha}{3}
.
∎

Note that the above algorithm does not output accept in the soundness case.
Thus, let us assume
|S|\geq\frac{n\alpha^{4}\hat{\lambda}^{2}}{16}
and
\bm{p}[S]\geq\alpha/2
.
Let
S^{+}=\left\{i\in S\textrm{ s.t. }\bm{p}[i]>\frac{1}{n}\right\}
.
We consider two cases.

Case 1:
\bm{p}[S]\leq|S|/n

When
\bm{p}[S]\leq|S|/n
, we observe that
(\bm{p}|S)
is uniform (resp. far from uniform) if and only if
\bm{p}
is.
In particular, we test uniformity of
(\bm{p}|S)
via sample access to the mixture
(\bm{r}|S)
using
Lemma

4.4
.

In the completeness case, we have
(\bm{p}|S)
is uniform.
In the soundness case, note that

(\bm{p}|S)[i]=\frac{\bm{p}[i]}{\bm{p}[S]}\geq\frac{n}{|S|}\bm{p}[i]

so that

\sum_{i\in S^{+}}(\bm{p}|S)[i]-(U_{n}|S)[i]=\sum_{i\in S^{+}}(\bm{p}|S)[i]-\frac{1}{|S|}\geq\sum_{i\in S^{+}}\frac{n}{|S|}\bm{p}[i]-\frac{n}{|S|}\frac{1}{n}\geq\frac{n}{|S|}\alpha\text{.}

In particular, we have from
9.10
that

\left\lVert(\bm{p}|S)-(U_{n}|S)\right\rVert_{1}\geq\sum_{i\in S^{+}}(\bm{p}|S)[i]-(U_{n}|S)[i]\geq\frac{n}{|S|}\alpha\geq\alpha\text{.}

Furthermore,

\bm{p}[S]\geq\bm{p}[S^{+}]\geq\alpha
, so that a single sample from
\bm{r}
falls in
S
and is from
\bm{p}
with probability
\frac{1}{\lambda\alpha}
.
If we take
\frac{2m}{\lambda\alpha}
samples, then we have at least
m
samples from
\bm{p}
in
S
with probability at least
1-\exp(-\Omega(m))>1-\delta
if
m\gg\log(1/\delta)
.
Note that since the algorithm knows
S
(as we are given knowledge of the distribution
\bm{r}
), we will only consider samples on
S
and assume that we are sampling from the conditional distribution
(\bm{r}|S)
.

We first show that the mixture
(\bm{r}|S):=\eta(\bm{p}|S)+(1-\eta)(\bm{q}|S)
is not too contaminated.

Lemma 9.11
.

\eta\geq\frac{\lambda\alpha}{\bm{r}[S]}\geq\lambda\alpha
.

Proof.

Fix a sample
Y\sim(\bm{r}|S)
and let
Z
denote its source.

\displaystyle(\bm{r}|S)[i]=\Pr(Y=i|Y\in S)

\displaystyle=\Pr(Z=\bm{p},Y=i|Y\in S)+\Pr(Z=\bm{q},Y=i|Y\in S)

\displaystyle=\Pr(Y=i|Z=\bm{p},Y\in S)\Pr(Z=\bm{p}|Y\in S)

\displaystyle\quad+\Pr(Y=i|Z=\bm{q},Y\in S)\Pr(Z=\bm{q}|Y\in S)

\displaystyle=(\bm{p}|S)[i]\Pr(Z=\bm{p}|Y\in S)+(\bm{q}|S)[i]\Pr(Z=\bm{q}|Y\in S)\text{.}

Thus
\eta
is the probability that a sample in
S
came from
\bm{p}
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\Pr(Z=\bm{p}|Y\in S)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Pr(Z=\bm{p},Y=S)}{\Pr(Y\in S)}\geq\frac{\lambda\alpha}{\bm{r}[S]}\text{.}

∎

Furthermore, note that for all
i\in S
, we have
\bm{r}[S]\geq\lambda\bm{p}[S]\geq\lambda\alpha
.
This allows us to bound
(\bm{r}|S)[i]=\frac{\bm{r}[i]}{\bm{r}[S]}\leq\frac{16}{n\alpha^{3}\hat{\lambda}\bm{r}[S]}\leq\frac{16}{n\alpha^{4}\hat{\lambda}^{2}}
and
Then, directly applying
Lemma

4.4
to the mixture
(\bm{r}|S)
we obtain a uniformity tester using

O\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\alpha^{2}\eta}\right)=O\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\alpha^{3}\lambda}\right)

samples and

O\left(\frac{n\frac{1}{n\alpha^{4}\lambda^{2}}}{\alpha^{4}\eta^{2}}\right)=O\left(\frac{1}{\alpha^{10}\lambda^{4}}\right)

queries.
Recall that we incur an additional
\alpha\lambda
factor in the sample complexity to obtain samples from
(\bm{r}|S)
(yielding
O\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\alpha^{4}\lambda^{2}}\right)
samples).

Case 2:
\bm{p}[S]\geq|S|/n

Consider now the second case.
When
\bm{p}[S]\geq|S|/n
, we note that
\bm{p}
produces many collisions on the subset
S
if and only if
\bm{p}
is far from uniform.
We run the algorithm of
Lemma

9.9
but with different parameters which we state below.
Note that we only run the algorithm once
(T=1)
since we only require high constant probability of correctness.

In the uniform case, we have
\left\lVert U_{n}[S]\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\frac{|S|}{n^{2}}
.
In the soundness case, we have

\left\lVert\bm{p}[S]-U_{n}[S]\right\rVert_{1}=\sum_{i\in S}\left|\bm{p}[i]-\frac{1}{n}\right|\geq\alpha

so that
\left\lVert\bm{p}[S]-U_{n}[S]\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\geq\alpha^{2}/|S|
.
Furthermore, we have

\frac{\alpha^{2}}{|S|}\leq\left\lVert\bm{p}[S]-U_{n}[S]\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\left\lVert\bm{p}[S]\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\frac{|S|}{n^{2}}-\frac{2\bm{p}[S]}{n}\leq\left\lVert\bm{p}[S]\right\rVert_{2}^{2}-\frac{|S|}{n^{2}}

since
\bm{p}[S]\geq\frac{|S|}{n}
.
Rearranging, we obtain

\left\lVert\bm{p}[S]\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{\alpha^{2}}{|S|}+\frac{|S|}{n^{2}}=\frac{\alpha^{2}}{|S|}+\left\lVert U_{n}[S]\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\text{.}

Suppose we take
m/\hat{\lambda}
samples.
Let
c(\bm{p};S)
denote the number of
\bm{p}
-collisions in
S
.
From the above discussion, we expect an expectation gap in the number of collisions
c(\bm{p};S)
of approximately
\Theta\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}m^{2}}{|S|}\right)
.
Following (
48
), we have

\mathrm{Var}(c(\bm{p}))\leq m_{\bm{p}}^{2}\left\lVert\bm{p}[S]\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+m_{\bm{p}}^{3}\left\lVert\bm{p}[S]\right\rVert_{3}^{3}\text{.}

Our goal now is to follow the framework of
Lemma

9.3
.

Analogously to
Lemma

9.6
, we obtain the following result.

Lemma 9.12
.

Suppose
m_{\bm{p}}=O\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\alpha^{7/2}\lambda}+\frac{1}{\alpha^{10}\lambda^{4}}\right)
for some sufficiently large constant.
Then, the following hold:

1.

\left|c(\bm{p};S)-\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\left\lVert\bm{p}[S]\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right|\leq\frac{0.01\alpha^{2}m^{2}}{n}
with probability
0.999
.

2.

c(\bm{r};S)=O\left(\frac{m^{2}}{n\alpha^{3}\lambda^{3}}\right)
and
c(\bm{p};S)=O\left(\frac{m^{2}}{n\alpha^{3}\lambda^{2}}\right)
with probability
0.999
.

Proof.

Since
\left\lVert\bm{p}[S]\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\max_{i\in S}\bm{p}[i]\leq\max_{i\in S}\frac{\bm{r}[i]}{\lambda}\leq\frac{16}{n\alpha^{3}\hat{\lambda}^{2}}
and

\left\lVert\bm{p}[S]\right\rVert_{3}^{3}=\sum_{i\in S}\bm{p}[i]^{3}\leq(\max_{i\in S}\bm{p}[i])\left\lVert\bm{p}[S]\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{256}{n^{2}\alpha^{6}\hat{\lambda}^{4}}\text{,}

we have

\mathrm{Var}(c(\bm{p};S))\leq m_{\bm{p}}^{2}\frac{16}{n\alpha^{3}\hat{\lambda}^{2}}+m_{\bm{p}}^{3}\frac{256}{n^{2}\alpha^{6}\hat{\lambda}^{4}}\text{.}

By Chebyshev’s inequality, we have

\Pr\left(\left|c(\bm{p};S)-\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\left\lVert\bm{p}[S]\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right|>\frac{0.01\alpha^{2}m^{2}}{n}\right)<0.001

as long as
m_{\bm{p}}=O\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\alpha^{7/2}\lambda}+\frac{1}{\alpha^{10}\lambda^{4}}\right)
for some sufficiently large constant.
Note that here we have used
m_{\bm{p}}=\Theta(m)
from
9.2
.

As in
Lemma

9.6
, we have
c(\bm{r};S)=O\left((m/\lambda)^{2}\left\lVert\bm{r}[S]\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)=O\left(\frac{m^{2}}{n\alpha^{3}\lambda^{3}}\right)
and
c(\bm{p};S)=O\left(m^{2}\left\lVert\bm{p}[S]\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)=O\left(\frac{m^{2}}{n\alpha^{3}\lambda^{2}}\right)
with probability
0.999
with Markov’s inequality.
∎

Following
Lemma

9.3
, our goal is to obtain estimates
\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}
,
\hat{c}(\bm{p};S)
satisfying:

1.

\left|\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}-m_{\bm{p}}\right|\ll\frac{|S|\alpha^{2}m}{n}
.

2.

\left|\hat{c}(\bm{p};S)-c(\bm{p};S)\right|\ll\frac{m^{2}\alpha^{2}}{n}

Towards the first estimate, we note that by picking a random sample and querying it, we flip a coin with bias
\frac{m_{\bm{p}}}{m/\hat{\lambda}}
which we must estimate up to accuracy
\ll\frac{|S|\alpha^{2}m}{n}\frac{\hat{\lambda}}{m}=\frac{|S|\alpha^{2}\hat{\lambda}}{n}
.
From
Lemma

3.13
, this requires

O\left(\frac{n^{2}}{\alpha^{4}|S|^{2}\lambda^{2}}\right)=O\left(\frac{1}{\alpha^{12}\lambda^{6}}\right)

queries since
|S|=\Omega\left(n\alpha^{4}\lambda^{2}\right)
.
To use this estimate, we will observe that

\displaystyle\left|\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}-\binom{\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}}{2}\right|\left(\frac{|S|}{n^{2}}+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{|S|}\right)

\displaystyle\leq(m_{\bm{p}}+\hat{m}_{\bm{p}})|m_{\bm{p}}-\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}|\left(\frac{|S|}{n^{2}}+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{|S|}\right)=O\left(\frac{2}{|S|}m|m_{\bm{p}}-\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}|\right)\ll\frac{\alpha^{2}m^{2}}{n}\text{.}

(49)

In the first inequality, we use
a^{2}-b^{2}=(a+b)(a-b)
, in the second we note that
|S|\leq n
implies that
\frac{|S|}{n}+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{|S|}\leq\frac{2}{|S|}
and
m_{\bm{p}}=O(m)
, and in the final inequality we use our error bound on
\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}
.

Towards the second estimate, we pick a random collision and query it, which yields a
\bm{p}
-collision with probability
\frac{c(\bm{p};S)}{c(\bm{r};S)}
.
To estimate this up to accuracy
\ll\frac{m^{2}\alpha^{2}}{c(\bm{r};S)n}
we require

O\left(\frac{n^{2}c(\bm{r};S)^{2}}{m^{4}\alpha^{4}}\cdot\frac{c(\bm{p};S)}{c(\bm{r};S)}\right)=O\left(\frac{n^{2}c(\bm{p};S)c(\bm{r};S)}{\alpha^{4}m^{4}}\right)=O\left(\frac{1}{\alpha^{10}\lambda^{5}}\right)

queries by bounding the number of collisions with
Lemma

9.12
.

Given the estimates, we return
\mathbbm{1}\left[\hat{c}(\bm{p};S)<\binom{\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}}{2}\left(\frac{|S|}{n^{2}}+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2|S|}\right)\right]
.
When
\bm{p}
is uniform, the algorithm accepts as

\displaystyle\hat{c}(\bm{p};S)

\displaystyle\leq c(\bm{p};S)+\frac{0.01\alpha^{2}m^{2}}{n}

\displaystyle\leq\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{|S|}{n^{2}}+\frac{0.02\alpha^{2}m^{2}}{n}

\displaystyle\leq\binom{\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{|S|}{n^{2}}+\frac{0.03\alpha^{2}m^{2}}{n}

\displaystyle\leq\binom{\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{|S|}{n^{2}}+\binom{\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{0.25\alpha^{2}}{|S|}\text{.}

In the first inequality, we apply the error bound on
\hat{c}(\bm{p};S)
, in the second
Lemma

9.12
, in the third the error bound on
\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}
and (
49
), and in the final inequality we use
m\leq 2m_{\bm{p}}
from
9.2
and
|S|\leq n
.
In the soundness case, the algorithm rejects (following similar arguments) as

\displaystyle\hat{c}(\bm{p};S)

\displaystyle\geq c(\bm{p};S)-\frac{0.01\alpha^{2}m^{2}}{n}

\displaystyle\geq\binom{m_{\bm{p}}}{2}\left(\frac{|S|}{n^{2}}+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{|S|}\right)-\frac{0.02\alpha^{2}m^{2}}{n}

\displaystyle\geq\binom{\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}}{2}\left(\frac{|S|}{n^{2}}+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{|S|}\right)-\frac{0.03\alpha^{2}m^{2}}{n}-

\displaystyle\geq\binom{\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}}{2}\left(\frac{|S|}{n^{2}}+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{|S|}\right)-\binom{\hat{m}_{\bm{p}}}{2}\frac{0.25\alpha^{2}}{|S|}\text{.}

The algorithm requires
O\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\alpha^{7/2}\lambda}+\frac{1}{\alpha^{10}\lambda^{4}}\right)
samples and
O\left(\frac{1}{\alpha^{12}\lambda^{6}}\right)
queries.

Putting It All Together

Our final algorithm for
Lemma

9.9
is to run both algorithms for the above cases and output accept if they both accept.
If either of the algorithms reject, we reject.

For correctness, note that if
\bm{p}
is uniform, then
\bm{p}[S]=\frac{|S|}{n}
so that the conditions of both cases are satisfied.
In particular, by a union bound over both cases, both algorithms will accept, as desired.
On the other hand, if
\bm{p}
is far from uniform, then at least one of the conditions are satisfied, and one algorithm will reject, so we reject, as desired.
The correctness probability follows again from a union bound over both cases.
The sample and query complexity bounds follow from summing over both cases.
∎

We are now ready to present the final algorithm for
Theorem

9.1
.

1.

Estimate
\frac{2\lambda}{3}\leq\hat{\lambda}\leq\lambda
using
Lemma

3.16
.

2.

Set
\alpha=\frac{\varepsilon}{1+\log n}
and
k_{0}\leftarrow\log n+3\log\alpha+\log\hat{\lambda}-3
.

3.

For all
1\leq k\leq k_{0}
:

(a)

Run the sub-routine of
Lemma

9.3
with
\delta\ll\frac{1}{k_{0}}
.
If it outputs reject, reject. Otherwise continue.

4.

Run the sub-routine of
Lemma

9.9
.
Return the output.

We argue that our algorithm is correct.
If
\bm{p}
is uniform, then every sub-routine returns accept, and we return accept.
This fails to occur with at most small constant probability by a union bound.

If
\bm{p}
is far from uniform, we claim at least one iteration will reject.
This follows from our setting of
\alpha
and the following lemma.

Claim 9.13
.

Suppose
\bm{p}
is
\varepsilon
-far from uniform.
There is an index
1\leq k\leq 1+\log n
such that
\frac{\varepsilon}{1+\log n}<\sum_{i\in S_{k}^{r},\bm{p}[i]>\frac{1}{n}}\bm{p}[i]-\frac{1}{n}
.

Proof.

By definition of total variation distance,

\varepsilon<\sum_{\bm{p}[i]>\frac{1}{n}}\bm{p}[i]-\frac{1}{n}\text{.}

By partitioning
[n]
into
S_{k}^{\bm{r}}
, we have

\varepsilon<\sum_{k=1}^{\log n+1}\sum_{i\in S_{k}^{r},\bm{p}[i]>\frac{1}{n}}\bm{p}[i]-\frac{1}{n}\text{.}

In particular, there exists some index
k
where

\frac{\varepsilon}{\log n+1}<\sum_{i\in S_{k}^{r},\bm{p}[i]>\frac{1}{n}}\bm{p}[i]-\frac{1}{n}\text{.}

∎

We thus bound the sample complexity via
Lemma

9.3
and
Lemma

9.9
to obtain by
\tilde{O}\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\alpha^{9/2}\lambda^{7/2}}+\frac{1}{\alpha^{12}\lambda^{6}}\right)
and the query complexity by
O\left(\frac{\log n}{\alpha^{6}\lambda^{8}}+\frac{1}{\alpha^{12}\lambda^{6}}\right)
.
We complete the analysis by substituting
\alpha=\frac{\varepsilon}{1+\log n}
and noting that
\alpha\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{2\log n}
.
∎

Appendix A
Omitted Proofs

A.1
Probability and Information Theory

See
3.8

Proof.

The conditional entropy
H(X|f(T))
is the expectation over
f(T)
of
h(q)
where
h(q)
is the binary entropy function and
q
is the probability that
X=f(T)
given
f(T)
.
Since
\mathbb{E}[q]\geq 0.51
and
h
is concave,
H(X|f(T))\leq h(0.51)<1-2\cdot 10^{-4}
.
Then, by the data processing inequality

I(X:T)\geq I(X:f(T))\geq H(X)-H(X|f(T))\geq 2\cdot 10^{-4}\text{.}

∎

See
3.9

Proof.

We give a proof of this standard fact for completeness.
Note
\Pr[X=1]=\Pr[X=0]=1/2
.
By definition,

\displaystyle I(X:M)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a}\sum_{i=0,1}\Pr[X=i,M=a]\log\left(\frac{\Pr[X=i,M=a]}{\Pr[X=i]\Pr[M=a]}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a}\sum_{i=0,1}\Pr[X=i,M=a]\log\left(\frac{2\Pr[X=i,M=a]}{\Pr[M=a]}\right)\text{.}

Fix an
a
.
Then, we have (using
\log(x)\leq x-1
for
x>0
),

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\Pr[X=i,M=a]\log\left(\frac{2\Pr[X=i,M=a]}{\Pr[M=a]}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\Pr[X=i,M=a]\left(\frac{2\Pr[X=i,M=a]}{\Pr[M=a]}-1\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\Pr[M=a]}\sum_{i}2\Pr[X=i,M=a]^{2}-\Pr[X=i,M=a]\Pr[M=a]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\Pr[M=a]}\sum_{i}\Pr[X=i,M=a]^{2}-\Pr[X=i,M=a]\Pr[X=1-i,M=a]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(\Pr[X=0,M=a]-\Pr[X=1,M=a])^{2}}{\Pr[M=a]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2(\Pr[M=a|X=0]-\Pr[M=a|X=1])^{2}}{\Pr[M=a|X=0]+\Pr[M=a|X=1]}\text{.}

In the second equality we used
\Pr[M=a]=\Pr[X=0,M=a]+\Pr[X=1,M=a]
and in the last step we used

\Pr[X=0,M=a]+\Pr[X=1,M=a]=\frac{1}{2}\big(\Pr[M=a|X=0]+\Pr[M=a|X=1]\big)

since
X
is an unbiased coin. Applying the above bound to all
a
concludes the proof. ∎

Next, we prove the concentration bounds required in
Theorem

6.3
.

Proof of
Lemma

6.4
.

From
Lemma

3.2
, the expected number of collisions is
\binom{m}{2}\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
.
A standard calculation (see e.g.
[
Can20
]
) yields

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}(X)

\displaystyle\leq\binom{m}{2}^{2}\left(\frac{2\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}{m(m-1)}+\frac{4\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{3}^{3}}{m}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{m(m-1)\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}{2}+\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{3}^{3}m(m-1)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\binom{m}{2}\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\left(1+2\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}(m-1)\right)\text{.}

Since
\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}
and
m\geq c\sqrt{n}
, we have that
1+2\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}(m-1)\leq(2+c)\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}(m-1)
.
Thus,
\mathrm{Var}(X)\leq O(m^{3}\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{3})
.
By Chebyshev’s Inequality, we set
\sigma\leq O(m^{1.5}\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{1.5})
and obtain

\Pr\left(X\in(1\pm 0.0001)\binom{m}{2}\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)>0.9999

for sufficiently large
n
.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

6.5
.

Let
Y_{1},\dotsc,Y_{m}
denote the individual samples and
X=\sum_{i_{1},\dotsc,i_{c}}\mathbbm{1}\left[Y_{i_{1}}=\dotsc=Y_{i_{c}}\right]
be the number of
c
-collisions where
i_{1},\dotsc,i_{c}
are distinct.
As before, the expected number of collisions is
\mathbb{E}[X]=\binom{m}{c}\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{c}^{c}
.
We proceed to bound the variance, in particular
\mathbb{E}[X^{2}]
which we may write as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[X^{2}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},\dotsc,i_{c}}\mathbbm{1}\left[Y_{i_{1}}=\dotsc=Y_{i_{c}}\right]\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},\dotsc,i_{c},j_{1},\dotsc,j_{c}}\mathbbm{1}\left[Y_{i_{1}}=\dotsc=Y_{i_{c}}\right]\mathbbm{1}\left[Y_{j_{1}}=\dotsc=Y_{j_{c}}\right]\right]\text{.}

We now need to do some case analysis.
Either there is the case
i_{1},\dotsc,i_{c},j_{1},\dotsc j_{c}
are all distinct, or there is some overlap in the tuples
i_{1},\dotsc,i_{c}
and
j_{1},\dotsc,j_{c}
.
Note that
i_{1},\dotsc,i_{c}
are always distinct (similarly for
j_{1},\dotsc,j_{c}
).
In the former case, all indices are distinct and independent and we obtain

\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},\dotsc,i_{c},j_{1},\dotsc,j_{c}}\mathbbm{1}\left[Y_{i_{1}}=\dotsc=Y_{i_{c}}\right]\mathbbm{1}\left[Y_{j_{1}}=\dotsc=Y_{j_{c}}\right]\right]=\binom{m}{2c}\left\lVert p\right\rVert_{c}^{2c}\text{.}

Note that this is at most
\mathbb{E}[X]^{2}
so we can freely subtract this term from the variance calculation.
In the latter case, since there is overlap between
i_{1},\dotsc,i_{c}
and
j_{1},\dotsc,j_{c}
(of which there are at most
2c-1
distinct indices) we can upper bound the desired quantity as

\mathrm{Var}(X)\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},\dotsc,i_{c},j_{1},\dotsc,j_{c}}\mathbbm{1}\left[Y_{i_{1}}=\dotsc=Y_{i_{c}}=Y_{j_{1}}=\dotsc=Y_{j_{c}}\right]\right]=O_{c}\left(\sum_{k=c}^{2c-1}\binom{m}{k}\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{k}^{k}\right)\text{.}

In the above, we note that there are
O_{c}(1)
distinct cases of overlap between the two sets of indices.
We upper bound the sum in each case by noting that when there are
c\leq k\leq 2c-1
distinct, there are at most
O_{c}(\binom{m}{k})
summands (choices of indices
i_{1},\dotsc,i_{c},j_{1},\dotsc,j_{c}
) and the probability they all coincide is
\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{k}^{k}\leq\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{c}^{k}
.
Now, observe that for every
\bm{p}
,
\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{c}\geq\frac{1}{n^{(c-1)/c}}
so that
m\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{c}\geq 2
.
Therefore,

\mathrm{Var}(X)=O_{c}\left(\sum_{k=c}^{2c-1}\binom{m}{k}\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{k}^{k}\right)=O_{c}\left(\binom{m}{2c-1}\left\lVert\bm{p}\right\rVert_{c}^{2c-1}\right)

so the standard deviation is at most
O_{c}\left(m^{c-1/2}\left\lVert p\right\rVert_{c}^{c-1/2}\right)
which for sufficiently large
n
(depending on
c
only) is asymptotically less than
\mathbb{E}[X]
.
The result then follows by applying the Chebyshev inequality.
∎

Finally, we give the sample lower bound for the problem given in
Theorem

1.1
.

Lemma A.1
.

Suppose an algorithm is given sample access to two coins
\bm{c}_{1},\bm{c}_{2}
with biases
a<b
and is asked to determine which coin has which bias.
Then, any algorithm that succeeds with probability at least
\frac{2}{3}
requires
\Omega\left(\frac{\min(a,b,1-a,1-b)}{(b-a)^{2}}\right)
samples.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{A}
denote such an algorithm which takes
m
samples.
Consider an adversary that flips a uniform bit
X
and if
X=0
, assigns
\bm{c}_{1}
to have bias
a
, and otherwise assigns
\bm{c}_{1}
to have bias
b
.
Let
T=(T_{1},T_{2})
denote the samples observed by algorithm
\mathcal{A}
.
From
Lemma

3.8
we have
I(X:T)\geq 2\cdot 10^{-4}
.
On the other hand, since
T_{1},T_{2}
are independent conditioned on
X
, we can upper bound
I(X:T)\leq I(X:T_{1})+I(X:T_{2})
.

We claim
I(X:T_{1})=O\left(\frac{m(b-a)^{2}}{\min(a,b,1-a,1-b)}\right)
(see Lemma 3.9 of
[
HIK+24
]
).
A similar argument proceeds for
I(X:T_{2})
.
Note that
T_{1}
consists of
m
iid samples of
\mathrm{Bern}(a)
if
X=0
and
\mathrm{Bern}(b)
if
X=1
.
In particular, applying the independence of each sample conditioned on
X
and
3.9
, we obtain

I(X:T_{1})=O\left(m\frac{(b-a)^{2}}{\min(a,b,1-a,1-b)}\right)

as desired.
Combined with our lower bound on
I(X:T)
for any accurate algorithm, this yields a lower bound on
m
.
∎

A.2
Flattening with Bounded Infinity Norm

Theorem A.2

(High Sample Mixture Flattening)
.

Using
O(n\log n)
samples to a mixture
\bm{r}=\lambda\bm{p}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}
over
[n]
, it is possible to produce a random map
f\colon[n]\to[N]
such that the following hold.

•

For any two distributions
\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{2}
over
[n]
, we have
\left\lVert f(\mathcal{D}_{1})-f(\mathcal{D}_{2})\right\rVert_{1}=\left\lVert\mathcal{D}_{1}-\mathcal{D}_{2}\right\rVert_{1}
.

•

For any constant
\delta\in(0,1)
,
\left\lVert f(\bm{r})\right\rVert_{\infty}<\frac{1+\delta}{n},\left\lVert f(\bm{p})\right\rVert_{\infty}<\frac{1+\delta}{\lambda n}
, and
N\leq(2+\delta)n
with probability
0.999
.

Proof.

By
Theorem

7.1
, using
O(n\log n)
samples, we can learn
\widetilde{\bm{r}}[1],\ldots,\widetilde{\bm{r}}[n]
satisfying
|\widetilde{\bm{r}}[i]-\bm{r}[i]|\leq\delta\sqrt{\frac{\bm{r}[i]}{n}}
for any constant
\delta\in(0,1/2)
with probability
0.999
. In particular,

\displaystyle\bm{r}[i]-\delta\sqrt{\frac{\bm{r}[i]}{n}}\leq\widetilde{\bm{r}}[i]\leq\bm{r}[i]+\delta\sqrt{\frac{\bm{r}[i]}{n}}~\implies~\bm{r}[i]\left(1-\delta\sqrt{\frac{1}{\bm{r}[i]n}}\right)\leq\widetilde{\bm{r}}[i]\leq\bm{r}[i]\left(1+\delta\sqrt{\frac{1}{\bm{r}[i]n}}\right)\text{.}

(50)

Therefore,

•

When
\bm{r}[i]\geq 1/n
, the RHS of
eq.

50
implies
(1-\delta)\bm{r}[i]\leq\widetilde{\bm{r}}[i]\leq(1+\delta)\bm{r}[i]
.

•

When
\bm{r}[i]\leq 1/n
, the LHS of
eq.

50
implies
\widetilde{\bm{r}}[i]\leq\bm{r}[i]+\frac{\delta}{n}\leq\frac{1+\delta}{n}
.

We now define the random map
f\colon[n]\to[N]
as follows. For each
i\in[n]
, let
B_{i}
be a domain of size
m_{i}:=\lceil\widetilde{\bm{r}}[i]n\rceil
. Then, we define
f(i)
to be a uniform random element of
B_{i}
. Note that
f
maps
[n]
to
B_{1}\cup\cdots\cup B_{n}
which (using the above upper bounds for the
\widetilde{\bm{r}}[i]
-s) is of size

\displaystyle N:=\sum_{i\leq n}m_{i}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\leq n}(1+\widetilde{\bm{r}}[i]n)\leq n\left(1+\sum_{i\colon\bm{r}[i]\geq 1/n}(1+\delta)\bm{r}[i]+\sum_{i\colon\bm{r}[i]<1/n}\left(\bm{r}[i]+\frac{\delta}{n}\right)\right)\leq 2(1+\delta)n\text{.}

(51)

We now show
1
-norms are preserved under
f
. For a distribution
\mathcal{D}
, the probability mass given to
j\in B_{i}
is exactly
\mathcal{D}[i]/m_{i}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\left\lVert f(\mathcal{D}_{1})-f(\mathcal{D}_{2})\right\rVert_{1}=\sum_{i\leq n}\sum_{j\in B_{i}}\left|\frac{\mathcal{D}_{1}[i]}{m_{i}}-\frac{\mathcal{D}_{2}[i]}{m_{i}}\right|=\sum_{i\leq n}|\mathcal{D}_{1}[i]-\mathcal{D}_{2}[i]|=\left\lVert\mathcal{D}_{1}-\mathcal{D}_{2}\right\rVert_{1}\text{.}

(52)

Next, we bound the infinity norm of
f(\bm{r})
and
f(\bm{p})
. For
j\in B_{i}
, the probability mass of
f(\bm{r})
on
j
is
\bm{r}[i]/m_{i}=\bm{r}[i]/\lceil\widetilde{\bm{r}}[i]n\rceil
and so
\left\lVert f(\bm{r})\right\rVert_{\infty}=\max_{i}\frac{\bm{r}[i]}{\lceil n\widetilde{\bm{r}}[i]\rceil}
,
\left\lVert f(\bm{p})\right\rVert_{\infty}=\max_{i}\frac{\bm{p}[i]}{\lceil n\widetilde{\bm{r}}[i]\rceil}
. We get the following bounds:

•

When
\bm{r}[i]\geq 1/n
, we have
\widetilde{\bm{r}}[i]\geq(1-\delta)\bm{r}[i]
(as shown above) and so

\frac{\bm{r}[i]}{\lceil n\widetilde{\bm{r}}[i]\rceil}\leq\frac{\bm{r}[i]}{n\cdot(1-\delta)\bm{r}[i]}=\frac{1}{(1-\delta)n}~\text{ and }~\frac{\bm{p}[i]}{\lceil n\widetilde{\bm{r}}[i]\rceil}\leq\frac{\bm{p}[i]}{n\cdot(1-\delta)\bm{r}[i]}\leq\frac{1}{(1-\delta)\lambda n}

•

When
\bm{r}[i]\leq 1/n
, by the use of the ceiling function the denominator is at least
1
, and so

\frac{\bm{r}[i]}{\lceil n\widetilde{\bm{r}}[i]\rceil}\leq\bm{r}[i]\leq\frac{1}{n}~\text{ and }~\frac{\bm{p}[i]}{\lceil n\widetilde{\bm{r}}[i]\rceil}\leq\bm{p}[i]\leq\frac{\bm{r}[i]}{\lambda}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda n}

and so
\left\lVert f(\bm{r})\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{(1-\delta)n}<\frac{(1+2\delta)}{n}
(since
\delta<1/2
) and
\left\lVert f(\bm{p})\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\frac{1+2\delta}{\lambda n}
, as claimed. ∎

A.3
Reduction to Equal Mixture Parameters

In
Section

5
and
Section

7
, we described closeness testers for distributions
\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2}
under sample and verification query access to mixtures
\bm{r}_{1}=\lambda\bm{p}_{1}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}_{1}
and
\bm{r}_{2}=\lambda\bm{p}_{2}+(1-\lambda)\bm{q}_{2}
. In particular, we assumed a common mixture parameter
\lambda
in both distributions, which may be unrealistic in certain applications. However, in this section we give a simple reduction showing that this assumption is without loss of generality, at the cost of extra polynomial factors of
O(\lambda^{-1})
in the sample and query complexity. We remark there may exist a more efficient reduction that avoids this increase.

Lemma A.3
.

Suppose we are given sample and query access to two mixtures
\bm{r}_{1}=\lambda_{1}\bm{p}_{1}+(1-\lambda_{1})\bm{q}_{1}
and
\bm{r}_{2}=\lambda_{2}\bm{p}_{2}+(1-\lambda_{2})\bm{q}_{2}
with unknown (and possible unequal) mixture parameters
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in(0,1)
. Then, there exist distributions
\bm{q}_{1}^{\prime},\bm{q}_{2}^{\prime}
for which we can simulate sample and query access to
\bm{r}_{1}=\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\bm{p}_{1}+(1-\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2})\bm{q}_{1}^{\prime}
and
\bm{r}_{2}=\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\bm{p}_{2}+(1-\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2})\bm{q}_{2}^{\prime}
. In particular, to simulate one sample costs one sample to the original mixture, and to simulate one query costs two queries and one sample to the original mixtures.

Proof.

First, write

\displaystyle\bm{r}_{1}=\lambda_{1}\bm{p}_{1}+(1-\lambda_{1})\bm{q}_{1}

\displaystyle=(\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}+(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}))\bm{p}_{1}+(1-(\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}+(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2})))\bm{q}_{1}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\bm{p}_{1}+(1-\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2})\left(\frac{(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2})\bm{p}_{1}+(1-(\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}+(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2})))\bm{q}_{1}}{1-\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\bm{p}_{1}+(1-\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2})\bm{q}_{1}^{\prime}=\bm{r}_{1}^{\prime}

(53)

where

\bm{q}_{1}^{\prime}:=\frac{(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2})\bm{p}_{1}+(1-(\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}+(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2})))\bm{q}_{1}}{1-\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}\text{.}

Clearly
\bm{r}_{1}=\bm{r}_{1}^{\prime}
and so samples are trivial to simulate. We now show how to simulate queries to
\bm{r}_{1}^{\prime}
. Suppose we sample
x\sim\bm{r}_{1}^{\prime}
and wish to simulate a query on
x
. Then, administer a query on
x
using the
\bm{r}_{1}
verification query oracle. If the oracle returns "irrelevant", then we return "irrelevant". If the oracle returns "relevant", then we return "relevant" with probability
\lambda_{2}
and "irrelevant" with probability
1-\lambda_{2}
. To do this, we need access to a
\lambda_{2}
-probability coin. Crucially, we can simulate such a coin with one sample and one query to the
\bm{r}_{2}
verification query oracle. All in all, we have

\Pr_{x\sim\bm{r}_{1}^{\prime}}[x=i\text{ and }\bm{r}_{1}^{\prime}\text{-oracle returns }\bm{p}_{1}]=\Pr_{x\sim\bm{r}_{1}^{\prime}}[x=i\text{ and }x\sim\bm{p}_{1}]\cdot\Pr[\bm{r}_{1}\text{-oracle returns }\bm{p}_{1}~|~x=i]=\lambda_{1}\bm{p}_{1}(i)\cdot\lambda_{2}\text{.}

Similarly, we let
\bm{r}_{2}^{\prime}=\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\bm{p}_{2}+(1-\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2})\bm{q}_{2}^{\prime}
where

\bm{q}_{2}^{\prime}:=\frac{(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2})\bm{p}_{2}+(1-(\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}+(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2})))\bm{q}_{2}}{1-\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}

and the simulation proof for the
\bm{r}_{2}^{\prime}
verification query oracle is exactly analogous. ∎
